Despatch No. 2271 from Embassy, Tokyo,
"Interpellations in the Onening Diet
Sessions of February 15 and 16":
In the opening Diet session inter-
pellations were made which reflect dis-
satisfaction in the Diet with mounting
military expenditures and with the army's
participation in and interference with
politics, esnecially its successful
blocking of the formation of a cabinet
by Ugaki. There was also criticism of
Japan's position to improve relations
with China.
COMMENT:
In regard to the last noint it is
worthy of note that the remier's reply to
an inter. ellation inicates an intention
to solve Sino-Janar ese problems gradually,
a nolicy to which circcrstances would
seem to leave no alternative.

on Februtry 1s. These interpellations had principally to
do with the following to ics: the internal political
situation, es ecially with reference to the Army's participa-

tion in politics; government finance; Soviet Russia; and
Chine. Although both the Government and the Army have been
severely criticized in the Diet interpellations, a situa-
tion of an explosive nature such as that brought about by
Hamada's attack on the Government in the Diet's session
of January 21 and Generul Terauchi's awkwerd reply, has
thus far been avoided.*
1. The Intornal Politic: 1 Situation.
Mr.  ts jiro Moizumi of the Minseito was the first
interpellator at the opening session of the Diet on Janu-
ary lu. he asked way rremier layashi had requested men
belonging to the political  arties to resign from their
orties when ho offered them Cabinet posts, to which the
Prelier eplied vaguely th: t he had considered this step
"appropriate". Interpellated concerning the need for
freedom of speech and of the press as the basis for the
expression of "the will of the people ", General Hayashi
replied tnt t he was untirely in accord with Mr. Koizumi's
ideas.
Dr. tsujiro ehara of the Seiyukai stated that in
the understandine of the ceneral public the Army had been
responsible for the collapse of the Hirota Cabinet and
that the public hed wanted the formation of a cabinet by
Goneral Uguki, adding significantly that he would like to
know why the foimation of a Cabinet by General Ugaki
would hn ve disturbed the unity of the Army, as alleged
by

by military leaders. According to the newspaper versions
of Premier Hayashi's reply to Uehara, the Premier either
avoided answering these questions or answered them nly in
vague teras.
On February 16 Mr. Chokichi Miyawaki, of the Seiyukai,
interpellated first the avy Minister and next the War Min-
ister concernine discipline in the defense services and in-
terference by the Navy and the Amy in politics and diplo-
macy.
Admiral Yonai, the Navy Minister, replied that
the problem of diseiyline seemed to be more peculiar to
the Army then to the Wavy, that there was no worry on this
score as reg rds the Navy, which endeavored at all times
to be faithful to the will of the mperor. The Navy Min-
ister's answer was reportealy greeted with cheers from all
sections of the House. Tne War Minister, General Sugiyama,
replied to teha 's uestions by stating that since the
appointmont of Genor 1 Lerauehi as War Minister in the
Mirota abinet the rmy had been striving to the best of
its ability to assure perfect discipline in the Ammy; that
in his opinion diseipline was not yet perfect but that he
intended to mtke even reater efforts to bring about per-
fect discipline; that the Army authorities were strictly
pronibiting interference in politics by "the military in
general"; and that nevertheless as a State Minister he
believed it lis duty to ive voice to his views on state
affairs. The Fremier supported General Sugiyama, assert-
ing that while a soldier should not participa te in politics
it wes only a pert of a State Minister's duty to do so.

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)

With regerd to a pertinent question from Mr.
Miyawaki concerning the responsibility of the Army for
the outbreak of the February 2o Incident, the War Minister
said that the Army repented the affair but that concern-
ing its causes there were "many points on which the people
would do well to admonish one another". Again teking the
rostrum, Mr. Miyawaki said that he was quite satisfied with
the reply of the Havy Minister and that the War Minister's
answers seened satisfactory on the whole.
Reitelaulng the points already raised in previous
interpullations, Mr. Tetsutaro Hemano of the Minseito asked
why the my had opposed the formation of a Cabinet by
General gaki oven before he had indicated his platform,
why Premier Hayashi excluded political party men from the
new Cabinet, and whether in holding "so many concurrent pos ts"
the Tremier was imitating fascism. To the first question
the War Min ster replied somewhat speciously that the Army
had not interfered with the efforts of General Ugaki to
form a Cabinet but that the candidates for the office of
War Minister closen y the three chiefs of the Army had all
declined appointment to this positi n. In reply to the
second and third questions Premier Hayashi denied that
politicel party men had been excluded from the Cabinet
and that in holding concurrent portfolios he wished to
imitate the fascists.
Another leading question was put to the Government
by Mr. Hemano, viz., what would be done in case the re-
forms said by the Army to be suited to the needs of the
times were not in consonance with the wishes of the pub-

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
lic as expressed by the Lower House of the Diet. Ap-
parently this question was side-stepped by the Govern-
ment.
2. Government Finance.
Mr. Yuki, t.e Finance Minister, was interpellated
as to the concrete measures that would be adopted by
the Government to remedy certain financial evils, in
particular the one-sided relationship between the enormous
national defense expenditures and the small appropriations
for the stabilization of the livelihood of the people, and
also the difficulty of continued absorption by the nation
of public bonds. In skillfully worded replies the Finance
Minister stated that he vas much concerned about the lack
of equilibriun between the funds for armaments and those
for stabilication of the people's livelihood and asked the
Diet for a little more time in which to study the matter,
porticulc ly tre tuestion of local srants to the impover-
ished farming comaunities. The Finance Minister added
that in his belief all should cooperate with the defense
services "without opposing them and without bowing before
them."
In reference to the absorption of publie bonds,
Ir. Yuki adnitted thct the solution of this question was
growing more difficult but asserted that it depended not
only on the ability of the peo le to save but also on con-
fidence in national finances.
Both on Febru ry 15 and February 16 the Finance Min-
ister steted that he could not foretell with accuracy
the future of nation 1 finances and that there seemed to
be no help for the unbalanced budget, for which the
"conditions

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
"conditions in society todcy are responsible." He explain-
ed that he proposed to take a miadle course between that of
the late Mr. Takahashi and that of Dr. Baba, stating further
"though I do not know Whether I shall succeed". According
to Domei, the new Finance Ilinister's frankness was greeted
with approving lauchter by the members of the Diet.
3. Soviet Russia.
As concurrent Minister for Foreign Affairs, Fremier
Hayashi was interpellated by Baron Sakatani in the House
of Peers concernin the Governnent's views on the Soviet
Union's proposal for a non-aggression pact with Japan and
also concerning the border disputes between the Soviet Union
and "Manchukuo". The Pranier replied that his Government
had no intention at the moment of concluding a non-aggression
pact with the Soviet Union and that "Manchukuo"-Soviet frontier
guestions were bein gradually solved on the spot.
4. China.
In reply to questions concernin the Government's diplom-
acy vis-à-vis Chia, Fremier Hayashi admitted that Japan's
previous policy hed led to misunderstanding among the Chinese
of "Japan's true intentions", stated his belief that the re-
lations between the two countries should be made "serene" in
order to dispell all such misunderstanding, and expressed
the hope that definite problems that had arisen between the
two countries would be solved cradually, explaining that it
was his intention to begin with concrete problems. The
Premier's remarks about China would seem to connote a con-
templated change in Japan's former policy toward that country,
which

-7-
which aimed at the imiedicte settlement of all
pending issues as a whole.*

the Govermaent's attitude co tinues to be conciliatory. To
til ap sa t ces, the govermaent in tends to have its own

with the politicel arties in the Diet and the consecuent

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
AMERICAN EMBASSY
No. 2273.
Tokyo, February 18, 1937.
SUBJECT: NEW PARTY MOVEMENT IN JAPAN.
894.00
1937 1 27
is.
J
894.00/704
oui-miD
FE(38)
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
18in
I have the honor to report that a movement is gaining
strength in Japan for the formation of a new political party,
apparently to be composed of the rightist, or nationalist,
elements of the nation and to be supported by and to support
the military.
Although the situation is still nebulous, it appears that
two opposing "fronts" are developing in Japan. One is the
Nationalist (Military or Fascist) Front, which apparently will
be composed of the military, the reactionary element of the
people, and certain politicians who have fascist leanings.
FILFD
The other is the so-called "Popular Front" and will, according
F/G
11

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
to present indications, be composed of the existing poli-
tical parties, the proletarians and members of financial
and industrial groups. The Popular Front is not yet or-
ganized, although in l936 the proletarians, consisting of
various farmer-labor groups, organized a union to oppose
fascism and military expansion. As there is almost no
material available as yet upon which to base a discussion
of the Popular Front, this despatch will deal primarily
with the development of the Nationalist Front.
Nationalist sentiment has always been strong in Japan,
although for fifty years or more past the growth of demo-
cratic ideas and institutions has to some extent obscured the
idea of extreme nationalism. After the Manchurian Incident
of l93l, which aroused the patriotic ardor of the people and
inspired them with a new sense of national power, nationalist
sentiment gained new vigor, although it remained in an
inchoate state, without political power and little opportunity
to express itself except by acts of terrorism, such as the
incidents of May 15, 1932, and February 26, 1936. In recent
months, however, circumstances have altered, and it now ap-
pears that the nationalists groups are tending to amalgamate
for the purpose of obtaining political authority.
Perhaps the most powerful factor impelling the unifica-
tion of the nationalist groups for political purposes lies
in the outspoken enmity between the Army and the existing
political parties, especially the Seiyukai, the Minseito
and the Shakai Taishu-to, or Social Masses Party. A section
of the Army is undoubtedly opposed to democratic institutions,
while

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
while almost the whole Army is definitely opposed to govern-
ment by the political parties. This opposition, which has
been steadily growing in strength during the past few years,
now threatens so to disrupt the governmental machinery that
its smooth functioning will soon become impossible. The
Army (or at least the Itagaki section of the Army, which is
backing the movement for the unification of the nationalist
groups) desires the formation of a political party which will
be able to control the government and which will work with,
and not in opposition to, the Army.
Another factor which has inspired the nationalist groups
to political activities consists of the "national crisis" -
the possible contingency of war. In preparation for the war
which they believe to be impending, the military desire to
place Japan's industries and finance on a "semi-wartime"
basis - a step which is meeting with strong resistance from
the industrialists, financiers and the liberal elements of
the government. In order to force their economic plans on
the nation and to meet the so-called "emergency situation",
a radical change in government will probably be necessary. It
appears that this change is now under contemplation and will
be effected, if possible, by the formation of a strong nationa-
list political party against which the people and the liberal
elements in the government will be powerless and which will
therefore monopolize the government.
It is also believed that the failure of previous attempts
to foist a sort of military dictatorship upon the nation,
such as was tried at the time of the incident of February 26,
1936,

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
1936, has a bearing upon the present plans to form a nationalist
political party. The extreme nationalists in the Army are
beginning to realize that attempts to alter the present scheme
of government in Japan from above are hopeless, meeting as
they do the strong resistance of the populace. They now
realize that, as in Italy and Germany, the leaders of the
nationalist movement must be supported by a strong, organized
section of the people themselves before radical steps can be
taken. The formation of a nationalist party would appear
to be intended for the purpose of demonstrating to the nation
that any change in government which may take place in the
future was accomplished by the will of the people, as expressed
by the new party.
The success of the fascist party in Italy and of the nazi
party in Germany also appears to have inspired the nationalists
in Japan to new activities. There has been a slow but steady
trend toward a Japanese variety of fascism in Japan, and the
marked success of such systems in European countries has un-
doubtedly accelerated the movement here.
The present leaders of the nationalist movement are chief-
ly retired Army or Navy officers or leaders of reactionary
groups. Lieutenant-General Yoshiji Tatekawa and Vice-Admiral
Shozaburo Kobayashi are generallyreported to be the principal
backers of the movement, but the organizers are Colonel Kin-
goro Hashimoto, Colonel Junichiro Kobayashi, Major-General
Genkuro Kudo (all retired Army officers), Mr. Mitsuru Toyama
(the well-known reactionary leader), Mr. Tanenori Irie, Mr.
Kyuzo Takayama and Mr. Masuzo Yoshida, all of reactionary
groups.
Other prominent persons interested in the movement
are reported to be Prince Sanetaka Ichijo, Major-General
Baron

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Baron Takeo Kikuchi, Baron Iwakusu Ida and Mr. Teisuke
Imaizumi.
The first large step toward unification of the rightist
groups was taken by Colonel Hashimoto in October last, when
he formed the Ninpon Seinen-to, or Japan Young Men's Party.
This is an association of the more promising young men
selected from the various reactionary groups, organized for
the avowed purpose of inaugurating a nationalist political
party. Colonel Hashimoto has adopted fascist methods, with
uniforms and flags. The Nipron Seinen-to flag is a white
circle in a red field ( the reverse of the Japanese flag)
which Colonel Hashimoto states signifies that the members of
the party "in the white heat of the sun are to serve the
Emperor with blood-red patriotism". More of Colonel Hashi-
moto's views will be given later as explanatory of the ideo-
logy of the nationalists.
During 1936 some leaders of reactionary groups organized
the Ishin Seido Kenkyu-kai, or Research Association for the
Imperial Restoration System. A conference was called by this
association on December 15, 1936, which was attended by most
of the leaders mentioned before in this despatch. The princi-
ples advocated at this conference were (1) the establishment
of a government of the Imperial Way, (2) the destruction of
the existing political parties, and (3) the organization of
a richtist political party. The conference decided to call
a general meeting on December 21, 1936. This general meeting
was attended by some two hundred persons, who elected Baron
Ida as chairman. Colonel Hashimoto proposed a resolution
1n

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
in which he brought out the following points of policy:
1. Establishment of governmental administration under
the Imperial Way.
2. Movements for pure constitutional government.
3. Destruction of political power based on individualism,
democracy, materialism and utilitarianism.
This association appears to have taken no further open
steps toward the formation of a political party since the
meeting on December 21.
In addition to the above-mentioned
two associations, however, some eighty-two reactionary groups
of lesser importance are known to be working together for
the abolition of the existing political parties under the
name of the "Kokutai Yogo Rengo-kai", or Federation of National
Constitution Defense Associations. None of the three associa-
tions, or amalgamations of reactionary groups, seems to have
come forward with a definite plan for a political party as
yet, but Colonel Hashimoto, in a newspaper interview, stated
that the party would be formed and would have its candidates
for seats in the Lower House of the Diet by the time of the
next general election, which he thought would not take place
for three years.
The ideology of the nationalists is not entirely clear.
It is claimed by the leaders of the movement that their ideas
are not based on fascism or national socialism, but on "Nippon
Shugi", or the Japanese principle. This principle has never
been clearly explained, and does not seem to be entirely com-
prehensible to the leaders themselves. Essentially, however,
it appears to advocate the Imperial rule and to be opposed to
any

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
any sort of democratic regime. It seeks to unify the people
and the national strength under the Emperor. It is as ab-
stract and vague as the principles advocated in former years
by General Araki. Its character can perhaps best be under-
stood through the following statement of Colonel Hashimoto,
made to a HOCHI reporter in October last:
"Britain, the United States and France are the
sinking sun at dusk. They no longer have the driving
force of the past. National Socialists and Fascists
are the crescent moon against the evening sky, only
sharp. The Soviets are a bright star of early night,
its rays of light long, but adequate only to illumin-
ate a corner of the sky. Things can revive only in
the mornin sunlight of Great Japan.
"Fascism is only the crescent moon, just sharp and
cold. I have nothing to do with this sort of thing.
I am looking far and wide for pure-hearted youths to
work for the cause of the Imperial nation whose polity
is to write itself in letters of fire in the eight
cycles of the universe."
The strength of the nationalist movement in Japan lies
in the fact that it has one outstanding objective - the des-
truction of the existing political parties and the establish-
ment of one powerful nationalist party which would have a
monopoly of the government. In this respect, and in the fact
that it is opposed to democratic institutions, it resembles
fascism. But unlike fascism, it has no definite economic
platform and, in fact, no concrete proposals in regard to
government, finance or industry. In this lies its great
weakness. When the nationalist party (should it ever come
into existence) attempts to define its policies, it will pro-
bably be found that there are as many ideas as there are
members of the party, with the result that, once it has
achieved its principal object of abolishing the present poli-
tical parties, it will split into dozens of factions. However,
Colonel

UNITED STATES OF AMERICA

AMERICAN EMBASSY
Tokyo, February 18, 1937
SUBJECT: INTERPELLATION BY MR. YUKIO OZAKI.
937  1 27
C
894.00/705
11
O.N.L.ANDM
A
Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
In pursuance of the concluding paragraph of the
Embassy's despatch No. 2271,/February 17, 1937, I have
MMAR
1/
the honor to transmit herewith a newspaper report of
interpellations and replies on February 17 in the House
of Representatives of the Japanese Diet, including parti-
cularly the interpellatory speech of Mr. Yukio Ozaki,
distinguished liberal.
Mr. Ozaki's very able speech was given the rapt
attention
F/G

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
attention of the packed House, and was met with general
applause. Its reception was respectful and thoughtful,
and the Government's reply was restrained. There was no
disorder in spite of incisive criticism contained in the
speech.
Outstanding among the points made by Mr. Ozaki are
(1) that the appropriations demanded by the army are not
necessary for defense, (2) that the agreement with Germany
against communism was an error, (3) that Japan's policy
toward the Soviet Union should be friendship, (4) that
no external emergency in the Far East exists, and (5)
that the Japanese army does interfere, and should not
interfere, in the conduct of politics. The last point
he effectively elaborated by asserting that in first rate
nations the military are limited to their special fields
and that in second and third rate nations (Spain at the
present time cited as an example) where the military
interfere in politics the result is disaster. Mr. Ozaki
drew attention to the fact that when the Japanese War
Minister expresses a view in the Cabinet, from somewhere
in the army there comes an arbitrary voice of political
criticism.
Mr. Ozaki's speech is the featured news in today's
Japanese newspapers.
Respectfully yours
Enclosure:
Joseph C. Grew

In my initial talk with the new Foreign
Minister today Mr. Sato expressed regret that he had
had no direct experience in Japanese-American affairs
but he feels that our mutual relations must be largely
influenced as in the case of other countries by Japan's
relatiors with China. The inister therefore proposes
first of all to study the Sino-Japanese situation with a
view to bringing about a marked improvement in thos e
relat ons and having especially in mind the effect
4 1 197
of such an improvement on Japan's relations with the
rest of the world.
Two. The Minister is perhaps unduly optimistic
with regard to the possihility of achieving so desirable
a purpose without oventually embroiling himself with cer-
tain elements of the military, yet on the other hand the
policy which he envisages would appear to coincide with
F/G
the general aim of the Hayashi Government already
enunciated and almost certainly must reflect the talks
which he has alrendy had with his colleagues in the Cabinet.

and an unprovocative attitude toward Nanking is therefore
wise. Furthermore, the aggressive policy and tactics
recently pursued by Japan n China have signally and
ignominiously failed to produce desired results. Sato's
remarks to me would app car to substantinte our belief that
the Cabinet recognizes the present need for a more

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.98)
Emelosure to despateh No. sos,
January 30, 1937, from George D.
Ameriean Consul, Harbin, emtitled
"Editorial Comment on the Politieal
Situation in Japm."
HARBIN NICHINICHI (Japanese
SOURCE:
language da1ly), January 30
1937.
Translated by the Amerioan
Consulate General, Harbin.
EDITORIAL
(translated in part only)
EXIT UGAKI AND IAT NEXT ?
General Ugaki had to quit beoause of the head-on oppo-
sition by the army. It was impossible for the army to
achieve or maintain its discipline under General Ugaki, who
holds the political idea of democracy, and is supported by
men of the same thought; namely party-men and plutoorats.
The propo sed wholesale reorganization of the administrative
system could not be hoped for either.
The clash was the last and dcisive battle between libe-
ralism and autocracy, and the misoarriage of the Ugaki
Cabinet is virtually the funeral knell for democracy, which
has ruled the country in one form or other since the Meiji
era. The new Government should therefore be one of entire-
ly different politioal principles from that of the three
preceding coalition Government. To speak plainly, it
should be a more powerful and more rightist one than the
coalition, whoever be the man to form it.
The democratio thought, however, is still deeply
rooted among the nation, while the anti-democratic idea has
not yet grown enough. One false step will give rise to
uncontrollable confusion throughout the country. It is
hoped that the army authorities, who constitute the head-
quarters of the anti-democratic foroe, will lose no time
in drawing up and showing the nation a conarete plan for
a stabilization of the situation.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
In continuation of the mbassy's despatch No. 2274,
February 13, 1937, re orting an interpellatory speech by
Ir. Tukio Ozaki in the lower house of the Japanese Diet
on February 17, 1937, I heve the honor to submit the
folloving translation from the official report which adds
C
mentioned despatch. Toward the end of Mr. Ozaki's speech
he stated (in translation):
Nothing
G

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Nothing is easier and more pleasing to junior
officers than to interfere in politics, while protected
by their uniforms, against a background of arms. So
pleasing to them is this role that they are apt to run
amuck and finally to ruin the state. (Applause.) The
more they operate so, the more easily will the state
and the people be handled at their pleasure. That
would impose a military administration. The history
of Japan for seven hunured years beginning with the
Kamakura period and continuing to the last days of
the Tokugawa shogunate is ample proof of what is the
nature of a military administration. (Applause.) That
period of military administration in old Japan was
the result of a sally forth on the political stage by
military men backed by arms. Today the military or-
ganization is nearer perfection; and if today the
people permit a military administration the men of
the army will find it easier to impose their will
than in the days of the Kamakura and Tokugawa military
regimes. hat will then be the fate of the Throne?
What indeed will be the fate of the people? Nothing
is more sincerely to be üreaded, and I fervently
hope this change never comes.
.ilitary men might be ellowed to take part in
politics, but if they do so they should assume a civi-
lian status. So long as they vear the uniform they
might be allowed to make investigatory studies, for
any of the ministers of state, but their activities
must not exceed this limit. hen obnoxious writings
appear anong the peo le, the authorities lose no
time in arresting and prosecuting under the criminal
law those who are responsible. In recent years the
army has published many pamohlets; if there be necessity
for any further publishing by the army, all such pub-
lications shoula be over the signature of the liinister
of Jar. (Applause.) Any military pam hlets published
without the signature or the ar Minister should be
looked upon as cts of insubordination by junior offi-
cers, end should be prosecuted under the ordinary cri-
minai rovisions. If this action be not taken, what is
Military discipline? (Applause.)
I reconmend this subject to militery men. I have no
intention of estranging the people from the army. My
reconmend tion is to the army, for the army's benefit.
I an fearful that, if things sre allowed to take their
present course, a split between the army end the people
is inevitable. (Applause.)
Perheps the gentlemen of the military here present
have not yet had occasion to hear it, but, according to
my information, whenever the good lady of a certain family
has lapses of excessively obdurate wilfulness, she is
referred to by the members of the family as "the army".
(Laughter

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
(Laughter and applause.) I find this shocking.
I find it even frightening. When to indicate
wilfulness and great arrogance, the head of the house
and the children call the good lady the army, and
this being whispered about with tremulation as a
secret causes others (not housewives, you may be
sure, but husbands) to sey "Oh, we have that kind of
a person in our house too" -- when there are not a
few housenolds in which persons who are unreasoning
and wilful are nicknamed the army -- this is a state
of effairs which I res ectfully submit is worthy
of the army's anxiety. I am not one to favor an
estrangement between the army and the people, and
it is only concern on this score wnich has led me
thus to speak frankly.
Respectfully yours,
No.
Joseph C. Grew.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Jashington.
Sir:
I heve the honor to report that Mr. Seigo Nakano,
leader of the chauvinistic Tohokai (Eastern Association),
FTRD
n
made a lengthy interpellation in the Lower House of the
Diet on February 18 which stands in strong contrast to
Mr. Ozeki's interpellation of the day before*.
Mr. Nakano made the following statements: that in
his opinion military expenditures must continue to in-
crease, especially in view of the pressure "being exerted
every
* Embassy's despatch No. 2274, February 18, 1937.
705
F
Q

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
every day against Japan from all sides"; that although
the Government has been criticized for its failure to
stabilize the national livelihood such stabilization
can be effected only when security on Japan's fron-
tiers, particularly the "Manchukuo"-Soviet Russian
border, and adequacy of national defense are assured;
that it must be the duty of all Japanese subjects to
bear the heavy burden of the moment in order that
the nation may gein security forever; that Japan
should have advanced on Manchuria at the end of the
World War when the Powers were exhausted but that
instead it scrapped warships, reduced the number
of Army divisions, and pursued dreems of disarmament;
and that it was time for Japan to stop "serving Great
Britain in the name of Anglo-Japanese friendship."
Finally, the intpellator asserted that Japan was
being tre ted vith diplomatic contempt even by China
and asked what the attitude of the Foreign Office
was toward Greet Britain and the United States,
countries ich it placed far a ov: China.
In reply to these interpellations, Premier
shi agreed, of course, the the repletion of
national defenses was essential. He added that it
was a mistake to call his diplomatic policy one of
subnission and that he was only anxious to deal with
foreign Powers in a spirit of cooperation and not in
a uarrelsome spirit.
Mr. Nukano's interpellations by no means re-
present the oninions of most Diet members. However,
they

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
they do represent the ideas of a limited number of
nationalistic members of the Diet.
It will be recalled that Nakano was one of the
persons rumored by the Jepanese press to have re-
ceived part of the Yen 3,000,000 alleged to have been
distributed as bribes in Japan by Mr. Castle, American
Ambassador to Japan at the time of the London Naval
Conference of 1930, in order that the Japanese might
accept the Treaty.
Respectfully yours,

Joseph C. Grew.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,

Sir:
1705
With reference to the Embasoy's despatch No. 3274 of
OBF
February 18, 1937, concerning r. Yukio Ozaki's Diet in-
terpellation of February 17, I have the honor to report
by r. Ozaki on February 27 is,in some of its aspects,
2
even more significant than were the oral interpellations
Perhaps the most outstanding point raised by Mr. Ozaki
in his memorandum is that the Army's assumed right to ob-
struct the formation of a Cabinet by refusing to furnish a
War Minister amounts to disobedience of the Emperor's wishes.
In Mr. Ozaki's opinion, as expressed in his questionnaire,
such an attitude on the part of the Army is an insult to
7
and

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and serious disregard of the Imperial prerogative of ap-
pointment and dismissal of high Government officials. The
following note-worthy statements are made by Mr. Ozaki
in this connection: "Established precedent demands that,
except in cases of illness or truly unavoidable circumstances,
no subject of the Emperor is privileged to decline the Im-
perial Order. This is a point which is especially borne
in mind by soldiers. .......... It goes without saying that
the appointment of a Minister of War falls within His
Majesty's prerogative, the exercise of which no subject of
the Emperor is privileged to obstruct, or hinder."
Another important point in the memorandum is Mr. Ozaki's
suggestion to the Emperor that it would be more in the in-
terest of the nation for the Emperor directly to select a
Premier-designate rather than upon the Genro's recommenda-
tion.
hile r. Ozaki's oral interpellations of February 17
were concerned with both the Government's foreign and domes-
tic policies, his written interpellation is confined to
the internal political situation, with stress being laid
on the Army's part therein. In his first seven questions
Mr. Ozaki takes up the Army's interference in politics, and
hence with the Imperial prerogative, which led to the resigna-
tion of the Hirota Cabinet, the difficulties encountered by
General Ugaki in forming a new Cabinet, and formation of
the present Cabinet by General Hayashi allegedly after con-
sultation with General Terauchi and Admiral Nagano, who
had already resigned as War and Navy Ministers, respectively.
MT.

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
Mr. Ozaki makes the specific statement that, according
to available "external" information, it appears that
the Emperor was desirous of General Ugaki's succeeding
in organizing a Cabinet.
Questions 8, 9, and 10 deal with the proposed abolish-
ment of the Genro system and the direct selection of a
new Premier by the Emperor himself.
So far it is not known whether the Government will
take any action on Mr. Ozaki's questionnaire or what form
this action will assume, if taken.
1/
There is enclosed THE JAPAN ADVERTISER's translation
of Mr. Ozaki's questionnaire, which was originally published
in Japanese by the TOIYO MICHI NICHI of February 28.
Respectfully yours,
Noreth
Joseph C. Grew

Tokyo, March 1, 1937.
SUBJECT:
DIET INTERPELLATIONS FROM FEBRUARY 17 TO
FEBRUARY 28 INCLUSIVE.

The Honorable

Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No.
713
2271 of February 17, 1937, I have the honor to submit a
report on the more important Diet interpellations from
Februery 17 to February 28, inclusive.
Ever since the delivery of Generel Heyashi's
tactful initiel speech before the House of Peers on
February 15 and the adoption of a careful policy by
the
F/G

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the Government in replying to s everely critical inter-
pellations, as evidenced by the interpellations and the
replies of the first two days of the present Diet session,
the Government has maintained a conciliatory attitude
toward the political parties. Although for the past
ten days the Diet session has been relatively quiet,
the political perties have continued to criticize the
Government on a number of issues. It would seem, however,
that the interpellations have been made almost solely for
the purpose of political debate and not in an intent to
overthrow the Hayashi Cabinet or even to obstruct its
budgetary plans.*
For the purpose of convenient reference, the inter-
pellations are divided into five headings: internal
political situation; foreign policy; economic conditions;
Government finance; and labor.
1. Internal Political Situation.
Interpellated concerning the clarificetion of the
national polity end plans for administrative renovation,
Premier Hayeshi replied that for the promotion of the
national spirit, politics and education must be permeated
with the idea of the worship of the Divine and adoration
of the Emperor, and that he hoped to s tudy the plans for
administretive renovation left by the Hirota Cabinet
and formulate his own lans, retaining whatever was
commendable in the original proposals. On another
occasion the Premier emphatically denied that he con-
sidered the present non-perty Cabinet as the only form
of
* Embassy's despatch No. 2294, March 1, 1937.
71

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of Government that would fulfill the requirements in-
herent in "the parliamentary politics peculiar to Japan".
He explained that by "parliamentary politics peculiar
to the nation" is meant the taking into consideration
of the difference between the provisions of the Con-
stitution peculiar to Japan and those of the Constitu-
tions of foreign countries, a still very vague explanation
of the phrase.
After the violent attacks launched during the first
two days of the session, Diet members continued to make
lengthy interpellations criticizing the interference by
the Army in politics but obtained little concrete satis-
faction from the Government.
On February 17 Mr. Jotaro
Kawakami of the Sociel Mass Party asserted that the best
and quickest way to aclieve perfect discipline in the
Army would be for the Government to make a thorough re-
port of the facts
of the February 26 Incident in the
Diet and to await the "people's judgment."
General
Sugiyama, the War Minister, replied that he was not at
liberty to disclose the full details of the February 26
Incident but hoped to have an opportunity to do so in
the future.
Replying to an interpellation on February 22, the
War Minister denied that the Army was opposed to the
existence of the political parties but added that the
Army wanted to work with the people to do away with the
corruption and other evils of the political parties.
Although pressed to do so, General Sugiyama evaded an
explanation of what he meant by the "corruption and
other

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.95)
-4-
other evils of the political perties".
ei21937
On the same day Mr. Ryozo Makino, Seiyukai, avowed
that there was an unknown and "invisible" element in
NB
the Army which was dictating to its upper stratum. Mr.
F.S
Makino stated by inference that it was this "invisible"
Scal
juki
element in the Army which was responsible for the Amy's
lack of discipline and its interference in political af-
fairs. As far as the newspapers indica te, neither the
Premier nor the War Minister vouchsafed any reply to this
interpellation. With reference to the War Minister's
frequent assertions that discipline in the Army cannot
be finished until administrative renovation is achieved,
111
Mr. Giiji Yamamasu, Minseito, remarked that if
this were
11
the case there would never be establishment of complete
discipline in the Army because there would always be need
for reform of the administrative system. The Premier's
reply to this interpellation was worded vaguely and had
no significance.
On several occasions the War Minister denied that the
Army had interfered with the formation of a Cabinet by
General Ugaki. On February 24, however, General Sugiyama
tacitly admitted this interference when he confessed in
reply to en interpellation that Lieutenant-Gencral Kesago
Nekajima, Commander of the Gendarmerie, acting on the
orders of the War Minister, had stopped General Ugaki's
automobile on January 25 and advised him to decline the
Imperial command to form a Cabinet.
As compared with the Army, the Navy has received
relatively little attention in the Diet sessions. On
February 22 an interpellator stated that the nation had
not

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
not so much confidence in the Army as it had in the
Navy. Questioned as to whether the naval strength of
the country was besed on imaginary enemies or on
strategic standards, the Navy Minister replied that
Japan's naval strength must be such as to meke it im-
possible for any foreign Power to engage in aggressive
operations in the Western Pacific and that this naval
strength was designed to facilitate the enforcement of
national policies. At the same session, Admiral Yonai
declared that although the Navy had a certain peaceful
scheme, the details of which he was not in a position to
disclose, the Navy had no such aim as the alleged aggressive
"southward expansion policy".
2. Foreign Policy.
(a) Soviet Russia.
The interpellations from February 17 to February 28
have revealed nothing new with respect to the Government's
policy toward Soviet Russia.* In keeping with the policy
of the former Cabinet, the War Minister told the House of
Peers on February 22 that the increasing strength of Soviet
armaments in the Far East was one of the reasons for the
insistence of the Japanese Army on expansion of armaments.
Interpellating the Premier on February 25, Mr. Toyokichi
Toyoga, Minseito, made the suggestion that for the es-
tablishment of peace in the Far East Japan might join
hands with the Soviet Union to present a strong front
against Great Britain, "which is interfering too much in
Far Eastern affairs". General Hayashi replied evasively
that
793.84/8529
* Embassy's despatches No. 2266 of February 16, 1937, and
No. 2271/of February 17, 1937.
894.00/703

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
that it was the Government's avowed policy to strive
for peace in a spirit of co-existence and co-prosperity.
(b) China.
The Government continued to stress the desirability
of reaching a rapprochement with China based on economic
and cultural ties and to deny that Japan had territorial
designs on North China.* Interrogated concerning the Army's
ideas on North Chine, the War Minister stated that the with-
drawal of the Japanese troops from that region at the time
of the Jehol expedition demonstrated clearly that the Army
has no territorial ambitions in North China. He went on to
state that in his belief amity between China and Japan must
begin with economic cooperation, which the Army actively
advocated. Answering a question on Japanese plans for the
economic development of North China, General Sugiyama told
the interpellator that the Government was considering the
cultivation of cotton, development of the electric power
industry, mining of salts for industrial use, and advance-
ment in North China of the Japanese cotton spinning industry.
With regard to the concrete measures to be adopted by the
Government for cultural undertakings in China, Mr. Horino-
uchi, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, stated that
the Government intended to take more practical steps in
this direction. Asked if loans should not be made to
China, Mr. Horinouchi replied that China unfortunately
had not asked for a loan in recent years but that if it
did ask for one the matter would be studied carefully.
(c)
793.94
* Embassy's despatch No. 2281, of February 25, 1937.

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
(c) Great Britain.
Little attention has been paid to Great Britain
in the Diet proceedings except in the interpellation
mentioned above and in that concerning the Keelung in-
cident, which elicited a nebulous sort of reply from
the Government.* The only other interpellation of note
concerning Great Britain was one mede on February 28
by Mr. Hyogoro Sakurai, Minseito, who professed the
belief that there might be some need for Japan to lay
aside its traditional friendship taward Great Britain,
which country had become "increasingly indifferent".
The Premier replied that there was need to convince
Great Britain of Japan's position in the Far East.
(a) The United States.
It should be noted that the Diet proceedings have
been almost completely devoid of mention of the United
States. On February 2, however, Mr. Toyoda, Minseito,
deflecting the course of his interpellation on Soviet
Russia, stated that the United States Navy League was
spreading harmful propoganda concerning the two countries;
that the United States was concerned about Japan because
it misinterpreted Japan's continental policy as one of
aggression; and that those giving this false impression
were the military and diplomatic authorities on the spot.
The interpellator asked if the Japanese Government had
any intention of inaugurating "people's diplomacy" between
Japan and the United States to clear up these misunder-
standings
* Embassy's telegram No. 61 of February 24, 1937.

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
standings in the latter country. Premier Hayashi answered
that he fully agreed with the interpellator on the need
for instituting "people's diplomacy."
(e) The Netherlands.
At a budget meeting on February 28, Mr. Sakurai sug-
gested the possibility of concluding a non-aggression
pact between Japan and the Netherlends with a view to
promoting Japenese in terests in the Netherland East Indies
and also a possible proposal for obtaining a Japanese per-
petual lease in Netherland New Guinea for colonization pur-
poses. The Pre ier replied to the first question by stating
that Japan had no territorial embitions in the Netherland
East Indies but was trying to develop Japan's economic and
commercial interests in that region through peaceful means.
According to Domei, General Hayashi offered no reply to the
second question.
With respect to Japan's foreign policy in general
Premier Hayashi stated on February 25 that whatever was
done on diplomatic lines should be done frankly and that
the Government's foreign policy would depend not only on
econcmic diplomacy but also on cultural and peace diplomacy.
3. Economic.
Both on January 17 and on January 24, in answer to
interpellations regarding state management of indus tries,
the Government stated that it had no intention of extend-
ing state management to all industries. There has been
an incessant clamor on the part of Diet interpellators
for the withdrawal by the Government of the former Cabinet's
proposal

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
-9-
proposal for state control of the electric power industry,
but this matter has apparently not yet been decided. On
Februery 24, in denying that the Cabinet intended to control
the entire economic system for the sake of nationel defense,
the Premier admitted that some industries requiring it would
be controlled but added that the spirit of individual enter-
prise would not be restrained. In this connection, War
Minister Sugiyama declared that the Army did not desire
state control of the munitions industry.in times of peace.
4.
Government Finance.
As in the first two days of the Diet session on
February 15 and 16, the political parties continued to
criticize the Government on the gro und that the military
expenditures in the proposed bu get for 1937-38 were too
large and that there was too great a disproportion between
these expenditures and those set aside for stabilization
of the livelihood of the people and in particuler for finan-
cial aid for the farming and fishing communities. There
has also been criticism of the proposed taxes on the s core
that they will increase the financial burdens on the masses.
Among the more pertinent interpellations concerning
the Government's future financial policy was one made on
February 19 by Mr. Giichi Masuda, Minseito, who asked the
Government if it had any intention of presenting bills for
a permanent system of grants to the farming communities,
if it proposed to make radical changes in the local and
national tax systems, and if it had any plans for remedying
the

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
the evils of the present income tax. The gist of the
Finance Minister's reply to these questions was that
the Government was considering these metters carefully
and could give no definite answers at the present.
Another interpellation of interest was made by Mr.
Chosaburo Mizutani of the Sociel Mass Party on the
OB
same day. He accused the Finance Minister of resort-
ing to sophistry when he stated that he had no clear
financicl prospects for the future, declared that social
unrest would not be elimina ted until the capitalistic
system had been completely ove rthrown, and interpreted
yidu
the appointment of Mr. Seihin Ikede as Governor of the
Bank of Japan as a step toward financial Fascism.
Mr.
Yuki's replies were evasive.
Mr. Jitsutaro Okamoto, Minseito, questioned Finance
Minister Yuki on February 25 regarding the heavy military
expenditures provided for in the Government's proposed
budget. He asked if the Cabinet intended to continue the
practice observed last fall of fixing the appropriations
for the Army and the Navy at the stert of the drafting
of the budget and shaping those of the other brenches of
the Government in proportion thereto. The Finance Minister
answered reassuringly that he would like to avoid "thinking
in such a mechanical manner" and intended to compile the
budget by a proper balance of bond issues, defense ex-
penditures, and other necessary factors. However, Mr.
Yuki did not state that there would be any material re-
duction in the budgets of the Army and the Navy. Although
the Cabinet promised that the Army and the Navy would not
spend

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
-11-
spend during the fiscel year but would defer to the next
year Y46, 000,000 of the sums allotted to them in the
budget for 1937-38, this amount was included in the bud-
get as presented to the Diet by the Heyashi Cabinet.
This procedure met with criticism in the Diet on Febru-
ary 27 as being unconstitutional.
5. Labor.
Interpellators belonging to the proletarian parties
on February 21 charged that the proposed export tax to
provide funds for the control of trade would most likely
find its way to the shoulders of the workers. The Gov-
ernment did not answer this assertion directly. In reply
to an inquiry on the same day as to whether the Cabinet,
as an answer to foreign charges of social dumping by
Japan, had any intention of enforcing the 42-hour work
week stipulated in the International Labor Treaty, the
Home Minister stated that the Government had no inten-
tion of adhering to the Treaty at present.
Conclusion.
It is thought that the majority of significant Diet
interpellations have already been mede and that for the
remainder of the session the paramount issues will be
discussion of the Government's budget and enactment of
legislation. Despatches on these subjects will be sub-
mitted to the Department as developments occur.
Respectfully yours,
803
Joseph C. Grew.

In continuation of the Embassy's despatches No. 2268
705
and No. 2274/of February 18, 1937, I have the honor to
report that Japanese politics in the period February 17
considerations:
(1) It is generally accepted that the budget
A
will pass.
FURO
(2) Statements against the army seem admoni-
tory only, for the present.
(3) The question of political reform has been
kept alive, but in indefinite form.
(4) Monetary tension has been eased.
(5) Army assignments and promotions show
F/G
dominant

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
dominant influence of the moderate army element,
with deference to the Itagaki group.
The scene of activity in the Diet shifted to the
budget committee of the lower house, where, in spite of
894,00/715
considerable questioning (Embassy's despatch No. 2293,
March 1, 1937), the budget is expected to pass as pre-
sented by the Hayashi Cabinet, or with only unimportant
modification. Neither in the press nor in the Diet has
any effort been launched aiming at defeating the budget
or materially reducing its large military items. The
Cabinet appears confident of success, and its attitude
toward interpellations is one of generous conciliation
rather than necessary concession.
Anti-army expressions of opinion (Embassy's despatches
8440712
4.714
894.0715
No. 2283, February 26,1937, and No. 2291 and No. 2293,
March 1, 1937) are tinged by a similar tacit acceptance
of the thought that, at least for the present, the army
shall not be interfered with but is simply to be warned.
But, as warnings, the expressions continue strong. The
effort appears to be intended to forestall apprehended de-
velopments. These warnings conmonly take the form of state-
ments to the effect thet any tampering with constitutional
parliamentary government would alienate the army from
popular sympathy.
Premier Hayashi, addressing the budget committee on
February 23, paid tribute to the army-sponsored plan of
political reform of the government, though he put important
decision in the matter off till an indefinite later date.
The

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
The Fremier stated that the Cabinet legislation bureau
is weighing plans for the establishment of a body for
unification of national policy. It is well known that
the Itagaki group of the army is advocating the creation
of a general affairs board (on the model so effective in
Manchuria), which would no doubt mean in practice a
paralleling of the cabinet by an army-controlled decision-
making administration even less amenable to Diet influence
than is the cahinet at present. Hayashi has not seen fit
to put a stop to this agitation but has temporized by re-
ferring it for further investigation. In general the Pre-
mier has dealt with the Diet interpellations with considera-
ble skill by indicating sympathy with the points of view
presented, yet without comnitting the Government to speci-
fic measures. He has clearly tried, with success, to avoid
creatin: open antagonisms by the nature and tone of his
replies.
Finance Minister Yuki has been successful in inspiring
confidence in his capacity for controlling the financial
situation, and a consequent easing of monetary tension
has been noticeable. The public has been prepared for
gold exports in the near future, not as an inevitable re-
sult of foreien exchange complications but as a consequence
of Japan's possessing unneeded gold from the activity in
recent years of Japanese gold mines. The Bank of Japan
is holding specie reserves greatly in excess of currency
security requirements (if the yen is calculated at its
present exchange value) and a considerable profit can be
realized by selling some of this cold abroad at a higher
price

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
price (in yen) than was paid for it in Japan, at the
same time stabilizing the yen exchange and possibly
reducing interest charges by buying up some of the
high-interest loans floated abroad.
Announcement on February 24 of the regular periodic
list of assinments and promotions in the army is worth
noting because of the importance of military questions to
politics. The changes were briefly summarized in the
Military Attaché's report o. 8669, February 26, 1937. No
spectacular chenges are included in the list; it has the
appearance of being the work of impartial officers of
regular, moderate inclinations, and has been so accepted by
the public. It should be observed that Premier Hayashi's
respect for Lieutenant General Itagaki and his group is
reflected in the assinment of several of them to division
commands previously reserved for officers longer graduated
from the military academy. The assienment of these several
officers is not so marked a promotion as to indicate favorit-
ism, but it is a substantial advance which definitely does
show that the Itagaki group has the support of this adminis-
tration, whatever the political restraints called for by
political exigencies. It will be recalled that Itagaki
was layashi's first choice for war minister.
During the period under review publicity was given
to the Coneo basin pact as a precedent which Japan might
well advocate for development into a general principle
guaranteeing to all nations equal access to colonies (Em-
bassy's despatch No. 2289, February 26, 1937).
A military and naval secrets bill has been placed in
the

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.91)
- 5 -
the hands of the Cabinet for presentation to the present
session of the Diet. The press reports that the bill is
an improvement in constituting a clearer definition of
offenses, with less latitude left in enforcement.
Respectfully yours,

arch 16, 1937
Department of State
Political Developments in Japan
MAR 17 1937
NOTED
Mr. Becretary.
On March 8 and March 11, 1937, the new Japanese
894.00
Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Sato, in the course of
statements in the Diet on foreign relations, observed that
respect by Japan for the rights of other countries in China
and fair dealing with China by Jepan would react favorably
894.00/717
upon Anglo-Japanese and American-Japanese relations. He
mentioned the need of starting with a fresh point of view
in dealing with China, and advocated negotiating with China
on a basis of equality and in a conciliatory spirit. He
declared that if Japan did not want a crisis in its foreign
relations this could be avoided at any time. Sato's comment
on the question of a crisis contradicts a stetement made by
the Prime Minister when, while concurrently Minister for
Foreign Affairs, the Prime Minister said that the situation
in the Far East since the Manchurian incident "might expldte
V
at any moment".
Although Sato's statements appear to have been well
received in Japanese financial and business circles, exception
has been taken to some of the statements by members of certain
groups in the Diet and in the army, expressly on the ground
that Sato lacked a clear conception of Japan's domestic and
F/F
foreign
11
G

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
foreign circumstances and that his views were at variance
with those of the Prime Minister. On March 12, in the Budget
Committee meeting, the Foreign Minister, probably under the
pressure of these criticisms, made a qualifying statement,
and the Prime Minister and the War Minister also made state-
ments denying that there were material discrepancies between
their views and those of the Foreign Minister.
These developments reveal the internal conflict which
is aroused in Japan by the outspoken assertion of liberal
views. They indicate, however, that the wisdom of past
policy is being more openly called into question in Japan,
and that the colleagues of the new Foreign Minister recognize
the necessity of making a fresh start if Japan is to break
through its present impasse with China and improve its
relations with Great Britain, Soviet Russia and the United
States. It remains to be seen whether the proposed fresh
start will influence the attitude of the Japanese army
leaders in Manchuria and north China and affect objectives
as well as methods of policy.
Following upon the announcement by the British Govern-
ment of the appointment of Sir Robert Craigie to be Great
Britain's Ambassador to Japan, there came from Tokyo
yesterday press reports stating that in Japan this move is
interpreted as possibly foreshadowing resumption of efforts
toward negotiation of an agreement for naval limitation.
This information is significant as indicating that the
Japanese

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
Japanese are not indifferent in regard to the subject of
naval limitation. This Division continues of the opinion,
several times previously expressed, that, given time, the
situation in Japan is likely so to develop as to create an
attitude on the part of the Japanese political public
favorable toward entering upon (or even initiating) sug-
gestions that there be held an international conference or
conferences for the purpose of negotiating agreements toward
regulation of international relations in the Far East and
the Pacific Ocean.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 2311.
AMERICAN EMBASSY
Tokyo, March 16, 1937.
894.00
SUBJECT: JAPAN'S "SOUTHWARD ADVANCE" POLICY.
not
756.94
P 1 40
1
894.00/718
Coky an Fe(e)
A
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
雾
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatches Nos.
1652
1798, dated Apri1 30, 1936, and 1847, dated May 15, 1936,
on the subject of the "southward advance" policy ad-
vocated by certain sections of the Japanese nation, in-
cluding the Navy, I have the honor to report that some
phases of this policy were discussed in the Diet re-
cently.
At a meeting of the budget committee of the Lower
House on February 28, 1937, Mr. Hyogoro Sakurai, of the
Minseito,
F/FG

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Minseito, addressed an interpellation along interest-
ing lines to General Hayashi, the Premier and then
Minister for Foreign Affairs. He said that Netherland
New Guinea, which is larger in erea than the main
island of Japan, has not yet been fully developed.
He offered the suggestion that the Japanese Govern-
ment consider the question of opening negotiations
with the Netherlands Government with a view to se-
curing a perpetual lease of Netherland New Guinea
for purposes of colonization, thereby relieving the
pressure of population in Japan. In order, however,
to preclude any suspicion of territorial aggression
and in order to promote Japanese interests in the
Netherland East Indies, he suggested the conclusion
of a non-aggression pact between Japan and the Nether-
lands.
In reply the Premier said that in general he
shares the views of Mr. Sakurai. Japan has no terri-
torial designs on the Netherland East Indies, but the
Japanese Government is endeavoring to develop Japan's
economic interests in the South Seas by peaceful
means. In shaping policies to this end, the Premier
added, the Government must take into careful con-
sideration the position and feelings of the other
parties. In regard to the question of a lease of
New Guinea, the Premier said that the Government had
not yet studied the matter but he promised that the
suggestion

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
suggestion of Mr. Sakurai would be given careful at-
tention.
Mr. Sakurai also brought up the question of Japa-
nese immigration into the Netherland East Indies. He
said that the number of Japanese immigrants was limited
to 800 (presumably annually) and asked if the Jepanese
Government would negotiate for a larger quota. Mr.
Horinouchi, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, re-
plied that the Japanese Government had already sub-
mitted such a request.
Mr. Sakurai then said that the Japanese Government
should take steps to counteract the effects of propaganda
which was being disseminated in the Netherland East In-
dies and elsewhere in the South Seas, charging Japan
with aggressive designs in the Philippine Islands.
Premier Hayashi replied merely to the effect that Japan
has no such aims.
From the Japanese version of the interpellations
and replies, the exact nature of Mir. Sakurai's proposal
or suggestion in regard to New Guinea is not entirely
clear. It appears that he had in mind some sort of a
permanent or perpetual lease of all of Netherland New
Guinea, whereby for a stipulated annual rental payable
to the Netherlands, Japan could administer the terri-
tory and develop it for the benefit of Japan. This would
imply practically a sale of the territory to Japan, pay-
ment being on the installment plan. On the other hand,
Mr. Sakurai suggested that, in order to avoid any sus-
picion of territorial ambitions, Japan conclude a non-
aggression

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
aggression pact with the Netherlands, while the Premier
asserted that Japan has no territorial designs on the
Netherland East Indies. The two ideas seem to be con-
tradictory.
As the JAPAN CHRONICLE pointed out in an editorial
in the issue of March 3, 1937, Japan's "southward advance"
policy has hitherto been very vague as to the territory
covered by the policy and the objectives of that policy,
it not being clear whether Japan wanted only trade and
economic opportunities or actual acquisition of terri-
tory. Mr. Sakurai's interp ellations and the replies
of the Premier have shed some light on the subject, as
the discussion brought out the fact that the Japanese
Government recognizes the necessity of peaceful eco-
nomic development by Japanese in the South Seas, while
at the same time giving consideration to the position
and feelings of the other parties interested in the
territories.
Mr. Sakurai's proposal, which may appear somewhat
fantastic, seems to have been taken seriously in some
quarters. The ASAHI reported on March 1 that definite
action in regard to the possible lease of Netherland
New Guinea by the Jananese Government, as well as action
in regard to Japan's peaceful economic expansion into
other parts of the Netherland East Indies, would await
the return to Japan of Mr. Kazue Kuwashima, formerly
Chief of the Bureau of Far astern Affairs of the
Foreign Office and newly-appointed Minister to the
Netherlands, who, before proceeding to his new post,
18

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
is making a tour of the Netherland East Indies.(In
this connection, it is of significance to note that
this is the first time that a Japanese Minister to
the Netherlands has been sent to the Netherland East
Indies to acquire first-hand knowledge of those islands
before taking up residence at The Hague.) On March 9
the ASAHI reported that, according to information which
had reached the Japanese Foreign Office, the Dutch
people had apparently misunderstood Japan's real in-
tentions in regard to the Netherland East Indies,
because all of the newspapers in the Netherlands were
voicing strong opposition to Mr. Sakurai's suggestion
that Japan acquire a lease of Netherland New Guinea.
In regard to the practical aspects of the matter,
the Embassy has been informed by a Dutch official re-
sident in the Netherland East Indies that the Japanese
probably would be no more successful in colonizing
and developing Netherland New Guinea than have the
Dutch. The Japanese already possess several con-
cessions in the territory and have been drilling
(as yet unsuccessfully) for oil and have been ex-
perimenting with rubber and other plantations. The
island is very mountainous and is inhabited by wild
tribes, rendering penetration into the interior ex-
tremely dangerous, while the coastal plains are most
unhealthy. It is very unlikely that the Japanese,
accustomed to the temperate climate of Japan, would
be able to colonize and work in the territory, any
more

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.96)
-6 -
more than they have in Formosa. According to this
official, Mr. Sakurai is interested in some of the
Japanese concessions in Netherland New Guinea, and
his suggestion probably was made from interested
motives.
While the suggestion of Mr. Sakurai, therefore,
appears to be impractical and even somewhat fantastio,
it seems to the Embassy that there is a hint of a veiled
threat in the attention which was given by the Premier
to the suggestion. It will be remembered that the
premier, in reply to Mr. Sakurai's interpellation,
said that in general he shared the views of Mr. Sakurai,
and that while Japan has no territorial designs
on the Netherland East Indies, the Government will
give careful consideration to Mr. Sakurai's sug-
gestion that Japan lease Netherland New Guinea.
The interpellation and reply carry the implication of a
notice to the Netherlands that Japan must obtain con-
trol of territories in the South Seas through peaceful
and legal means (such as a lease) if possible. No
indication was given as to what means would be employed
if the peaceful and legal means failed to produce the
desired result.
Rospectfully,yours,

800.
Joseph O. Grew.

894.0312

Embassy's despatch 2367 April 16, 1937 last paragraph.
One. ho Japanese press interprets the returns of the
general election of April 30 as a distinct defeat for the
Hayashi Government. In the new Lower House the seats of the
opposition partios, the Minseito and Seiyukai total 354 or
22 less than in the previous House; those of the opposition
Shakai To ishuto 37 or 19 more than previously; those of the
Government-suporting Kokumin Domei and Showakai 30 or 7
less than proviously and those of the lesser groups and
independents 45 or 10 more than previously.
197
Two. A mollifluous declaration by the Cabinet today
states that b cause regeneration of the Diet is the
dornerstone o! governmental reform the Lower House was
disselved a month ago; that the Government looks to the
newly eleete members to be guided by a spirit of self-
sacrifice in developing the constitutional system
peculiar
F
0/

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.99)
62-2
-2-
JR
#122, May 3 6 p.m., from Tokyo.
peculiar to Japan; that this hope is generally entertained
by the people; and that the Government will continue on its
way confident of the nation's support of the Government's
earnest desire for perfect cooperation with the people.
Three. The Hayashi Cabinet is apparently determined to
remain in power as long as possible but the measures which
it will take to accomplish this purpose in the face of the
declared oppcsition of the major parties remain obscure.
However, it is probable that the Hayashi Cabinet can remain
in office until the extraordinary session of the Diet
(probably in August) as the Government can claim that the
election failed to show a popular mandate for Government
by either the Minseito or the Seiyukai which have nearly
equal representation in the new house. The Shakai Taishuto
made impressive gains but still lacks strength enough to
hold the balance of power for either major party. The
Embassy is therefore of the opinion that the temper of
the New House will be restrained although superficially
blustering because of its realization of the futility of
attempting to insist upon a party Government in the face of
the opposition of the military.
Repeated by mail to Peiping.

SKH:
This despatch discusses the announced
policies of the Hayashi Cabinet. The
Embassy's comment may be outlined as
follows:
In general the nolicies (which are
listed on nage two of the despatch) repre-
sent idealistic aims rather than concrete
proposals and, as the press noints out,
express almost impossible hopes. They
anpear definitely to noint in the direc-
tion of furtrer efforts to regiment the
nation on a semi-wartime basis and toward
a totalitarian state.
The policies of the Hayashi government
co not apnear to differ greatly from those
of the Hirota govermment. The obvious
explanation is that the same military in-
fluence dominated the two cabinets.
The Embassy considers it unlikely that
the Cabinet will be able to carry into
execution many of the policies which it has
anncunced. It seems certain that the Diet
will remain recalcitrant, and the Cahinet
will therefore either be compelled to re-
sign or to dissolve the Lower House again,
-- an unlikely step in view of the
criticism of the last dissoluticn. A
possible third course of acticn is for the
Cabinet

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Cabinet to bow to circumstances and be
satisfied with the execution of a few of
its plans.

SUBJECT: POLICIES OF THE HAYASHI CABINET.
