COPIES SENT TO
O.N.L. ANDM. I.D.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
With reference to the Embassy's telegram No. 107,
nounced policies of the Hayashi Cabinet, I have the
1/
honor to enclose herewith a clipping from the JAPAN
17 1937
ADVERTISER of April 11, 1937, containing a translation
of the eight points of policy as issued by Domei. The
ADVERTISER translation has been checked with the Japanese
text as published in the vernacular newspapers and has
been found to be substantially correct.
It
F/FG

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
It will be remembered that the Hayashi Dabinet
dissolved the Lower House of the Diet and suspended
the session of the House of Peers on March 31, 1937,
claiming that the Lower House lacked a correct under-
standing of the national situation and lacked sincerity
in dealing with the Government's measures. A general
election will be held on April 30, thirty days after the
dissolution of the Lower House, and the Cabinet has now
placed its policies before the people in order to meet
the coming election.
The policies, which may be classified under the
headings of (1) fundamental educational reform and
thorough clarification of the national polity, (2)
reform of the parliamentary system and improvement of
administrative organs, (3) establishment of a concrete
national diplomatic policy, (4) perfection of armaments
and preparation for a general national mobilization, (5)
establishment of adequate social policies and stabilization
of the living of the people, (6) promotion of national
strength through the development and control of industry,
(7) rehabilitation of farming and fishing communities,
and (8) adjustment of taxation, prevention of an undue
rise of prices and improvement of the balance of inter-
national payments, are somewhat more concrete than the
original policies announced shortly after the formation
of the Cabinet (Embassy's 43, February 6, 11 a.m.) but
are still generally considered to be too vague and mean-
ingless. It will be noted that no concrete measures are
suggested, except the establishment of a central delibera-
tive organ in the Cabinet for the purpose of promoting
a

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
a thorough clarification of the national polity, and
the possible establishment of central administrative
organs for the promotion of aviation and the reform
of the Government personnel system. The other policies
include no concrete proposals and, as has been pointed
out by several of the newspapers, express almost impossible,
idealistic hopes rather than feasible policies.
One point, however, induces reflection in regard to
the policies. It will be noted that the policies include
provision for the establishment of a deliberative organ
for the clarification and the thorough permeation among
the people of the national polity and for the enhancement
of the national spirit; for the perfection of armaments
and the preparation for a general mobilization of the
nation; for self-sufficiency in iron, liquid fuel and
other industrial materials; for the control of the elec-
tric power industry; for the propagation of the idea 
solidarity among the farming and fishing villages; and
for the control of prices, the adjustment of supply and
demand of materials, etc. These policies (or suggestions)
would appear definitely to point to a further attempt
to regiment the nation on a semi-wartime basis and ap-
parently to work toward a totalitarian state. As several
of the vernacular newspapers point out in their edi-
torials, the policies enumerated by the Hayashi Cabinet
do not differ greatly from those previously announced
by the Hirota Cabinet. The newspapers are inclined to
blame this on a lack of originality and intelligence
in the Hayashi Cabinet, but the Embassy is rather in-
clined to ascribe this similarity in policies to military
influence

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
influence. Neither of the two cabinets probably has
been able really to formulate its own policies and
the plans announced by both cabinets were undoubtedly
to a large extent dictated by the military. The in-
tention to work toward a Japanese variety of a totali-
tarian state is certainly contemplated by the military,
and there can be little doubt that similar policies
will be announced by each succeeding cabinet as long
as the military influence continues to dominate the
Government.
It is largely because of this fact that economic
and financial circles are dissatisfied with the announced
policies of the Hayashi Cabinet. They are inclined to
place considerable importance on the fourth policy,
namely, the perfection of national defense and prepa-
ration for general mobilization. They believe that this
policy points to the formation of a semi-wartime system
and are somewhat fearful as to the methods which the
Government will employ in the execution of this policy.
Financial circles are reported to be of the opinion
that the fiscal policies ennounced in the eighth item
of policy are vague, idealistic, and appear to have
been taken from some text book on economics. They believe
that the Cabinet will find difficulty in attempting to
carry out this idealistic program and are inclined to
resent the fact that the statement of policy tends to
attribute the rise in prices solely to speculation and
ignores the factor of credit expansion caused by huge
Government expenditures. Economic circles had believed
that

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
that, through the efforts of Mr. Yuki, Minister of
Finance, the Hirota Cabinet's policy of stricter
control would be modified by the Hayashi Cabinet,
and consequently they are disappointed by the indi-
cation that the Hayashi Cabinet will follow the same,
if not more, drastic measures in this connection.
These circles point out that strict control of the
nation's economic system is not consistent with the
policy of the expansion of production in Japan.
The Hayashi policies were not greeted with en-
thusiasm by any of the vernacular newspapers. The
ASAHI on April 1l, editorially pointed out that the
Cabinet is attempting the impossible when it offers to
reform the taxation system and stabilize the liveli-
hood of the people while at the same time incurring
enormous expenditures for the perfection of armaments,
rehabilitation of the farming and fishing communi-
ties, reform of the educational system, etc. The Cabinet
would have to perform magic to accomplish these ends.
But, according to ASAHI, the Hayashi Cabinet will not
live long enough to put its policies into effect. The
YOMIURI, on April ll, stated that the policies are all
imitations of the Hirota Cabinet and express no definite
ideology. Moreover, at the moment it is important to
take definite action, not to enumerate impractical poli-
cies. The CHUGAI SHOGYO on April 1l, editorially ex-
pressed the opinion that the policies of the Hayashi
Cabinet are senseless unless the Cabinet can carry them
into

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
into practise. No cabinet, much less the present one,
can possibly carry all the policies into execution, but
the Hayashi Cabinet has now promised the nation certain
things, and must do its best against great odds. The
KOKUMIN, on April 1l, stated that the policies are
too vague and are mostly legacies of the Hirota Cabinet.
There is no definite expression of concrete policies,
but on the other hand, this is the usual practise with
all cabinets. The IYAkO, on April 1l, stated that the
policies are lacking in originality. The proposal to
establish a central administrative organ for aviation
is a good plan, but the Cabinet will not live long
enough to accomplish this purpose. The HoCHI, on
April 1l, pointed out that the policies of the Hayashi
Cabinet show that the Government intends to lead the
country into a semi-wartime status. The Government,
however, has given no concrete indication of how it
intends to tide over the crisis and perfect national
defense.
Apparently in an effort to indicate to the nation
that the Government is sincere in its intention to carry
into execution its statement of policies, it was announced
after the Cabinet meeting on April 13, that the Cabinet
had definitely decided on three measures for immediate
action. The three measures consist of the establishment
of a national policy organ to implement the policies
already announced, the establishment of a commission to
renovate education and clarify the national polity, and
the issuance of instructions to the Army, the courts
of

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
of law, the post-offices, and other public organs coming
into contact with the people, to simplify their admin-
istrations and to be more courteous toward the publio.
(This last measure is in pursuance of the last clause
of the second item of the announced policies). The
national policy organ mentioned above is presumably
the so-called "Planning Board" which has been under
discussion for some time past in Japan. The exact
status of the Planning Board is not yet clear, al-
though for a time it was rather generally believed that
this board, which was to plan and coordinate all national
policies, was intended to be a sort of super-cabinet,
the ministers of state being relegated to the position
of administrative officers only. In this form the
Planning Board (which is one of the Army's pet ideas)
would appear to have been intended as the central policy
organ of a totalitarian or fascist state. Recently,
however, the vernacular newspapers have stated that the
Planning Board is now intended to be only a sort of
deliberative council, and that the actual authority
for deciding upon and execution of policies will remain
in the Cabinet.
It is not believed that the Hayashi Cabinet will be
able to carry into execution many of the policies which
it has announced . It was confronted before with a re-
calcitrant Diet, and there is no good reason to believe
that the nert Diet will be more compliant. The Cabinet
will therefore either be compelled to resign before
hardly

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8-
hardly more than starting its program, or will have to
dissolve the Lower House again (an unlikely step in view
of the criticism of the last dissolution) and try to
obtain a more complaisant Diet, or will have to bow to
circumstances and be satisfied with the execution of a
few of its plans. Some of the plans (those which do
not require budgetary appropriations) can be put into
effect by Imperial decree. This method of obtaining
its objectives has already been started, as mentioned
above, but the great bulk of the policies of the Cabinet
require considerable sums for execution, and it is im-
probable that the next, or any readily foreseeable
Diet, will, in view of the fiscal condition of the
country, grant large sums for the schemes of the present
Cabinet.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

the school system realized and author-

thc personnel system and renovation
of various administrative procedures
in the interests of public convenience.
"3. To make concrete a diplomatic
policy based on national unanimity.
"4. To advance the perfection of ar-

people will be improved, various social
insurance systems established, the wel-
fare and prosperity of workers advanc-

creased and national life stabilized.
Self-Sufficiency Aimed

to achieve self-sufficiency in iron.
liquid fuel and other staple industrial
materials. Various industries will be
promoted, smaller industries and busi-

control of the electric power industry
strengthened and various communica-
tions facilities improved and expanded.

fishing villages. Policies will be estab-
lished in connection with farming and
and agricultural insurance. Agrarian
industries will be spread over the
land. production conditions in agricul.
ture. forestry and fishing improved.
the idea of solidarity among the farm-

ton of the farming. forestry and fish-
ing villages striven for.
"8. To adjust the tax systems and
improve the price situation and the
balance of international payments
Equilibrium will be sought in the
burdens of the people. Taxes common
to the national and local systems will
be adjusted and reformed in order to
obtain the revenue necessary for the
existence and development of the
State. Steps will be taken to restrain
the increase of domestic prices caused
by speculations operations, adjust
fundamentally the relations between
the demand and supply of materials,
obtain sources of raw materials, ad-
vance foreign trade, develop shipping
and encourage emigration and thus to
improve the balance of international

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
patriotic societies for the formation of a rightist or
MAY 2 17
ported by and would support the military. This par-
ticular movement among the reactionaries appears to be
quiescent at the moment, but in the meantime another
movement to form a new political party has appeared.
The new movement differs from the previous movement
in
F/FG

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
in that it is being fostered by professional politicians,
but its purpose appears to be much the same as that of
the other movement, namely, to provide the necessary
political support to the military or to administrations
under military dominance.
This new movement for the formation of a rightest
political party has come into prominence since the
Hayashi Government obtained the dissolution of the Lower
House of the Diet on March 31, 1937. The Hayashi Cabinet
had discovered that it is difficult, if not impossible,
for a cabinet to function properly without the backing
of the majority of the members of the Lower House of
the Diet. In this connection it should be understood
that the Japanese Diet differs from parliaments in most
other countries in the world, in that it is not properly
a law-making body. Laws are comparatively rarely intro-
duced into the Diet by the members themselves. The Diet
is rather a law-passing body, concerned with examining,
discussing, and passing or rejecting bills placed before
it by the government in power at the moment. It is
essential, therefore, to any cabinet to have sufficient
support in the Diet to insure the passage of its measures -
otherwise the Government is unable to carry out its legis-
lative and administrative progrem.
After the formation of the Hayashi Cabinet, it at
first appeared that the Diet would simply harass the
Government, but towards the close of the session it
became clear that the Lower House of the Diet was be-
coming distinctly obstructive. Dissolution of the Lower
Houge

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
House was therefore ordered, apparently with the hope
that a new party willing to act as a rubber stamp for
the Government would spring into existence before the
general elections on April 30. Activities were imme-
diately started by a group of politicians toward this
end. Those prominently mentioned in the press as con-
cerned with the formation of the new party are as follows:
Yonezo Maeda, Seiyukai and former Railways Minister.
Zembei Horikiri, Seiyukai.
Chikuhei Nakajima, Seiyukai.
Ryutaro Nagai, Chief Secretary, Minseito.
Seiichi Aoki, Showakai.
Keisuke Mochizuki, Showakai.
Tatsunosuke Yamazaki, formerly Showakai and present
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry.
Count Hideo Kodama, Minister of Commerce and Industry.
Seigo Nakano, Tohokai.
Kiyoshi Akita, formerly Speaker of the Lower House.
Dr. Ichiro Kiyose, Kokumin Domei.
Count Yoriyasu Arima, Kenkyukai (House of Peers)
Fumio Goto, former Minister for Home Affairs.
Toyotaro Yuki, Finance Minister.
It appeared to be the intention of the organizers
that the new party should be formed with some of the smaller
political parties, notably the Showakai, the Kokumin Domei,
and the Tohokai as its nucleus, and that the party should
attract to it members of the Seiyukai and Minseito who
were dissatisfied with the attitude of their parties, and
who "appreciated the significance of the present situation".
It

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
It is not clear what connection, if any, exists between
the proposed party and the reactionary bodies, but ac-
cording to the CHUGAI SHOGYO the reactionary societies
are not displaying a great deal of interest in the new
party movement.
The movement appeared to be progressing rapidly
until about a week ago, when it became apparent that the
opposition to the new party was gaining considerable
strength. This opposition seems to be based on a general
belief among the people that the new party indicates a
trend towards a dictatorship. If it becomes possible for
a premier to organize a party to protect and perpetuate
his own interests, a dictatorship could easily arise.
The discussions in the newspapers indicate a general
opinion that sound parliamentary practise requires that
any government in power be opposed by some of the political
parties. Moreover, it soon became apparent that the new
party would attract very few of the members of the old
established parties. Even the members of the old parties
who were formerly inclined to break their allegiance and
take part in the formation of a new party have been an-
tagonized by the Government's arbitrary dissolution of the
Lower House and are now inclined to adhere to their old
allegiances and to present a common front opposed to the
Government.
On April 18, according to the press, Premier Hayashi
called on Prince Fumimaro Konoye, reportedly to ask him to
head the new rightist party. Prince Konoye, however, ap-
parently refused to give a definite reply. After the interview
the Prince said to newspapermen that the Premier desires
the

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
the establishment of an ideal party, which he apparently
hopes will be composed of the reformist elements in the
nation and of members of established parties who appreciate
the current situation in the same light as does the Govern-
ment. After the apparent refusal of Prince Konoye to head
the new party it was runored that Mr. Toyotaro Yuki, the
present Finence Minister, or Mr. Koki Hirota, the former
Premier, would be approached on the subject. The CHUGAI
SHOGYO, however, believes that Prince Konoye will even-
tually be prevailed upon to head the new party. In a
newspaper interview, Mr. Yuki denied his connection with
the new party, and stated his belief that the new party
would not materialize before the general election.
It having been found to be difficult to establish
the new party before April 30, it appears that the Govern-
ment has now adopted other tactics in regard to the future
composition of the Lower House. The members of the Cabinet
are now apparently hoping that a sufficient number of
candidates more or less in sympathy with the ideas and
objectives of the present Government will be returned at
the general election. The Premier announced on April
10 that he had no intention of taking positive action
for the formation of a new party and that he would be
satisfied if the new Diet proves to be a gathering
of members of the reformed parties. On April 1l, Dr.
Shiono, the Minister of Justice, said that it was no
concern of the Government whether the Seiyukai or the
Minseito obtained a majority in the Lower House or
whether a new political group controlled the Diet, but
it was important that a majority of the members have a
fu11

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
full and correot understanding of the prevailing situation.
The Government therefore hopes that as many representa-
tives as possible with a real conception of the prevailing
situation will be elected. Mr. Yuki, the Finance Minister,
also said that it did not matter if there was the same
distribution of seats in the Lower House as before the
election, so long as the parties seem to realize the
gravity of the situation.
The new party movement has not met with much support
from the press or the public. The public generally seems
to be on the side of the established parties, apparently
because it resents the arbitrary and uncalled-for dis-
solution of the Lower House. The newspapers are watching
the situation with interest but, with few exceptions,
are not inclined to boost the movement for a new party.
The TOKYO NICHI NICHI, on April 13, expressed itself
as in favor of a new party because it will give the Govern-
ment much needed political support and will give the
supporters of the layashi Cabinet an opportunity to register
their opinions and to indicate clearly the general senti-
ment of the nation in regara to the issue.
At the moment it does not appear that the new rightist
party will come into existence before the general election
on April 30, but there a pears to be no doubt that ad-
vantage will be taken of this election to aid the movement.
The new party will probably appear after the election.
It seems certain that the Premier dissolved the Lower
House with the hope that he could form a new Government
party out of the political chaos resulting from the
dissolution

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.95)
- 7-
dissolution, but he miscalculated the general sentiment
of the nation and was disappointed in his hopes. As
General Hayashi himself frequently affirms, he is just
a soldier and knows nothing of politics.
Respectfully yours,
Noh.
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo, April 16, 1937.
SUBJECT:
JAPANESE ELECTION CAMPAIGN TO APRIL 16, 1937.

PAN EASTERN AFFAIRS
894.00/729
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Six:
994.5327
In pursuance of the Embassy's despatch No. 2350,
April 2, l937, I have the honor to submit the following
report of the progress, to the present date, of the poli-
tical campaign for the election of a new Japanese House
of Representatives.
Promptly after the dissolution on March 31, persons
13 1937
began to register as candidates in the various election
districts, to stand for the 466 seats comprising the
AYM
Lower House. Such registrations have been mostly in the
names of the previously existing political parties, al-
though ten percent of the total number of candidates
registered
F/FG

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
14
registered by the close of April^are independents who
1/ have never held seats in the Diet (enclosure No. 1). In
respect of registration of candidates thus far, this
election is not markedly different from previous cam-
paigns.
On April 10 the Cabinet issued a platform which
in no way served to clarify the situation (Embassy's
telegram No. 107, April 10, 9 p.m.). Like previous
statements of policy, it was so worded in generalities
as to be useless for defining a logical political cleavage,
doing nothing more specific than generally calling for
improvement in all directions - better education and
culture, better administration, better diplomacy, better
armaments, better living, better industry, better rural
conditions, better taxes. But although the content of
the platform is worthless, there is one circumstance with
regard to its issue which should be noted: it was issued
as a platform which the Cabinet desires candidates to sup-
port. This platform is a corollary to the Premier's in-
vitation to men in politics (or who may be induced to take
up politics) to support the Government. Its object is to
make campaigning easy for friends of the Government.
The parties were not slow in going to the attack. On
April 13 the Seiyukai gave out a denunciation of dissolu-
tion and of the Hayashi Cabinet. The party statement up-
braids the Government's pose of having dissolved the
Lower House because of bad faith in obstructing legislation,
and declares that if refusal to defer blindly to the Govern-
ment is to be branded as bad faith then constitutional
politics

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
politics and respect for public opinion are nothing
but a hoax. It emphatically asserts the necessity
of respect for the Diet by the Government.
2/
The Minseito joined the cause with a statement
issued on April 15. The Minseito likewise castigates
the dissolution as an outrage without precedent in the
history of constitutional government. The party calls
upon the voters to vote Minseito as a public demand for
resignation of the Hayashi Cabinet. The problem of
planks for a platform is more difficult because of the
Government's having used, a few days before, most of the
platitudes of the political vocabulary. The Minseito
Party nevertheless puts itself on record in favor of bet-
ter defense and better living for the people.
The Shakai Taishuto (Social Mass Party) has gone
into the campaign with clearer definition and presentation
of political ideas than any other party. On April 14,
at a rally in Tokyo, was announced a platform denouncing
the enormous military budget, the trend toward fascism,
and the shameless ineffectiveness of the major political
parties, and calling for nationalization of basis indus-
tries, prevention of rise in prices, and control of the
Diet by the workers. In addition to putting up twenty-two
candidates with previous diet experience, forty-one new
candidates have already been registered on the Shakai
Taishuto ticket.
The present general election held the possibility
of being a historic one, but the failure of the parties
to join together threatens, at the present writing, to
reduce

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
reduce this cempaign to the customary formless futility.
The Hayashi Cabinet's attitude toward the Diet was
high-handed, and if the major parties were willing to
go to the country in unison on that one issue the weight
of the resultant protest could not be ignored, even in
Japan. But neither the Seiyukai nor the Minseito are
so disinterestedly devoted to the concept of constitu-
tional politics. Hayashi's advisers no doubt counted
on this discord when they favored dissolution. The
Premier probably thought that by a general election he
would gain time without great risk; and as far as the
campaign has now gone it looks as if he was right. A
likely outcome of the election of April 30, in the Em-
bassy's opinion, is this: Some increase in Shakai Tai-
shuto members of the Lower House. (not enough to require
their admission to the Government); some increase in in-
dependents and small party members, among whom the Govern-
ment could find supporters; a falling off of the Minseito
and Seiyukai. With such an outcome the Government would
have no great cause for concern. A non-party cabinet
has been possible heretofore, and it would continue with
greater justification should popular support of the major
parties be weakened. Meantime, with a core of Government
supporters in the new Diet, fascist party preparations
for the subsequent election could steadily advance.
Respectfully

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
Respectfully yours,
o
Joseph C. Grew

The Situation in the Far East.
NOTED
S
Mr. Secretary:
In China, there occurred during the week in review
894.00
no significant political developments.
In Japan, there was held yesterday (April 30) the
election of members of the House of Representatives, but
no definitive information in regard to the results of
894.00/730
the election has as yet been received.
Although it is not possible to foretell what action
the present Government will take if the election proves
adverse to it, as is generally expected, there would
appear to be a number of courses open to the Government,
as follows: (1) to continue in power notwithstanding an
adverse vote; or (2) to dissolve the House cf Repre-
sentatives as soon as the House convenes (under the
Constitution the Diet must be convened within five
months of the date of dissolution of the former House
of Representatives, or in this case before August 30,
1937); or (3) to resign; or (4) to reconstruct the
Cabinet by bringing in party representatives with a
view to strengthening the support of the Cabinet in the-
FILED
House of Representatives.

12
The Hach Between the Army and the Political Parties
P
The clash between the militarists and the political parties,
1--
u:
which wastaged the very day the Diet reconvened for business after
the year end holiday, was not unexpected. For several months evidences
ofdicsatfsfaction with the army's domination and interference in po-
1 tical affairs had been growing more apparent, not alone among the
pgiticians but amcng other sections of the public as well. A severe
drubbing of the Government for allowing the army to dictate to it had
been predicted, but no one had supposed that a parliamentary crisis
with ramifications extending into an uncertain future vould be pre-
cipitated so soon.
The immediate cause of the clash was an interpellation by
Kunimatsu Hamada, Seiyukai leader and former Speaker of the Lower House.
After the speeches of several Ministers had been booed at by the rep-
894.00/73
resentatives, the veteran parliamentarian, amid cheers from the benches,
attacled the army's policies both at home and in Manchuria, its diplo-
matic activities, lack of discipline and interference in politics, the
unprecedented budget caused by military demands, and called the Hirota
Government the most unpopular the country had had in 30 years. The
War Minister, General Terauchi, made an angry rejoinder and accused
Hamada of insulting the army. The latter then offered to commit
harakiri if the insult were proved; otherwise Gencral Terauchi "must
take full responsibility." The session ended abruptly in an uproar,
and that same day the Government obtained the Emperor's permission to
prorogue the Diet for two days, after sidestepping the War Minister's
demand that it be dissolved. Two days later the Government resigned
because negotiations -- the Navy Minister, Admiral Nagano, taking a
prominent part -- to effect a coapromise between the army leaders and
the Lover House of the Diet had been unsuccessful.
The army's temper is indicated in the following excerpts from
an official statement issued by the War Office on January 22, the day
after the Diet was prorogued. "Under the present conditions," it said
"it is impossible for the army to cooperate with political parties for
administrative reforms . . . their ideas are negative and . . . abso-
lutely opposed to the reform tendencies . . . arisen since the February
26th incident . . . Political parties have alvays considered party in-
terests and intrigues and . . . not the interests of the people."
After consulting with his advisers the Emperor asked General
Kazunari Ugaki, former Governor General of Korea and several times
War Minister, to form a government. The Premier designate energetically
attempted to carry out his sovereign's order, but after five days was
compelled to admit defeat, because the army leaders refused to name a
Minister of War for the nem cabinet. Their ostensible reason was ina
bility to control the anti-Ugaki element in the army. This element
FOTHD
is kmom to be the Kwantung Army faction, headed by Lieutenant General
Seishiro Itagaki, then Chief of Staff of that force. Another obscure
reason was hinted, namely, certain breaches of army discipline with
which his name is connected, probably unfustly. The military's real
-16160-
F/FG

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.99)
objections, however, were based on General Ugaki's kmown moderate views
and his cardinal blunder of 1925 when, as Minister of War, he assented
to the army's reduction by four divisions. It mattered not that his
appointment had the hearty approval of every importent section of Japa-
nese public opinion except the army-influenced reactionary societies
nor that the military faction was accused of flouting the Emperor's
will. Not all the army was opposed to General Ugaki. but the Itagaki
faction simply would not have him; hence his failure. The Japanese
political system provides no way around such an obstruction, short of a
personal intervention by the Emperor, which is extremely unlikely.
General Ugali, as a rebuke to the militarists, resigned from the army
officers' retired list.
The Emperor's choice for the Premiership finally fell upon
General Sen juro Hayashi, War Minister during part of the Okada regime,
whose efforts to rid the army of politics brought about the assassina-
tion of one of his chief War Office assistants, Lieutenant General
Nagata, on August 12, 1935, and, indirectly, the following February 26th
incident. Because he had kept free of factions, hovever, he was ac-
ceptable enough to the army leaders and no special objection to his
appointment was raised on the outside.
General Hayashi formally presented his cabinet to the Emperor
on February 2. It contains no party representatives except one who was
obliged to resign his political affiliations in order to accept office,
and several ministers hold two portfolios concurrently, an ominous
presage of the cabinet roform urged by the army. Its strongest member
is General Gen Sugiyama, the War Minister, and its most popilar member,
so it has developed, is Toyotaro Yuki, former President of the Indus-
trial Bank cf Japan, for personal reasons and because he has made an
attempt at budget retrenchment.
The Diet, which had been prorogued during the cabinet crisis
and while the new Hayashi Government was reconsidering the budget pre-
pared by the preceding regime, reconvened on February 15 under the un-
precedented circumstance that there was not one member of the Lower
House to support the Government. General Hayashi's speech in his con-
current rcles of Premier and Foreign Minister contained nothing of out-
standing importance and the Government's statement of policy was a
masterpicce of ambiguity, couched -- perhaps purposely -- in the cld
classical language, which added to its impressiveness but did not aid
the people's understanding of the issues involved.
The following four days were taken up by members' interpella-
tions of the Government, covering a wide rango of topics such as
national defense, the rumored formation of a political party of Fas-
cistic leanings, governmental reorganization, and control of industry.
The interpellations were pointed, but restraint was evident on both
sides, the Diet not wishing to force dissolution and the Government
being desirous of securing approval of the budget. The War Minister
said in part: "The desire of the army to improve armaments is not
based on any presumption of war. The army believes, on the contrary,
that adequate armaments will prevent war." In outlining a six-year
program of expansion, however, he stressed Japan's need of increased
armament to counter Russia's military preparations in the Far East.
This is the favorite thesis of the Itagaki faction.
-16161-

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
The most vigorous interpellation was a speeck. by the distin-
guished liberal. Yukio Ozaki, now in his 78th year. A member of the
Diet ccntinuously since its organization in 18s0, his life had been
threatened more than once by extremists who objected to his fearless
and outspoken utterances. On this occasion, speaking for one hour and
forty-five minutes, he challenged the necessity for such a great in-
crease in Japan's military expenditures; ridiculed the German--Japanese
anti-Comintern pact; asked why Japan did not conclude a nonaggression
treaty with Russia; urged the formulatior of a better and more con-
sistent policy toward China and the cultivation of friendship also with
the United States. Great Britain. and Russia. He questioned the use of
the words, "crisis," "semiwar time," and "emergency period." so much
used by the army to justify increased military expenditures, and as a
crowning defiance of the War Office he rebuked the army's interference
in politics, saying that only in second-rate countries do the army and
nary dominate the Government. The replies of the Premier and the Minis-
ter of War were vague generalities asserting that a serious international
crisis existed in the Far East but failing to give logical explanations
of the situation. Ozaki's courageous speech may have enlightened the
Japanese public on nationel issues, but it did not min army tolerance
for the politicel parties, as subsequent events proved.
General Hayashi aroused surprisa when he appointed Naotake
Sato, Ambassador to France. as Foreign Minister on March 3. A diplomat
of the Shidehara school whose words and acts had inspired profound
respect among his foreign colleagues in Paris and elsewhere, he seems
somewhat out of placa in a cabinet diminated by the military. The
appointment indicated a swing from one extreme to the other on the Pre
mier's part, for his original selecticns for the posts of War and Navy
Ministers were Lieutenant General Itagaki and Admiral Suetsugu. both of
the aggressive militaristic type. He was dissuaded from including
either of these men in his cabinet in any capacity, thereby probably
averting a political upheaval with incalculable consequences.
Sato's initial speeck in the Lower House of he Diet. advo-
cating relations with China or a besis of equality, brought jeers from
party leaders and criticism from the arry. He was forced to recant
somewhat, saying the proposed policy did not mean an attitude of suh-
missiveness; but the caliber of the man was indicated when (according
to press report) he asked, "Having become a great nation, why does
Japar not show a more steadfast attitudo becoming a great people?"
Continuing, he said, 'If those in power discarded petty tactics and
walked down a broad highway, people would have confidence and the
powers would not have reason to mistrust Japan's policy. It is our
hope so to walk along a broad highway openly with our national policies."
Observers are pondering whether this refers to Japan's army-dictated
policy toward autonomy movements in China. At any rate. the recent
relaxation of Japanese pressure on China is probably due in part to
Sato's influence as well as to a more general realization that the for-
mer policy of force has not paid.
Finance Minister Yuti's efforts brought about a reduction of
approximately Yen 269,000,000 in the original budget rstimate, yet the
total. Yen 2. 769,200,000. is higher than that of 1936-37 by about Yen
458,000,000 -- in other words, the highest in Japan's history. The
army and navy each accepted a reduction of Yen 23.000,000 in the nature
f a "postponement." The parties were scmewhat eppeased ty the Finance
-16162

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
Minister's efforts at retrenchment and discard of certain taxation
plans, and the budget was passed without alteration on March 29. The
army and navy al1otments are Yen 704,900,000 and 658,600,000, respective-
ly, or about half of the total.
Two days later the Diet was suddenly dissolved, ostensibly
because the political parties were using bargaining tactics and ob-
structing legislation. They did not "appreciate the current situation,"
the Premier said. The real reason, of course, was the military's de-
sire to teach the parties a lesson and forestall further opposition to
their plans. According to law a general election must be held within
30 days and the campaign is in progress at this writing. It is dis-
tinguished by having no vital issues at stake on either side; that is.
none has been openly formulated. The Government has issued a statement
of policy almost as vague as that announced when it took office, but
has no known candidates in the field. The two major parties are rest-
ing their respective causes on opposition to the Government as a result
of the dissolution of the Diet for inadequate reasons. The minor
parties are welcoming the election as an almost sure means of increasing
their representation in the Diet. As one observer has said, it is a
pure example of what Premier Hayashi called the "constitutional govern-
ment peculiar to Japan."
There are, in reality, some very vital issues pending, but
the present ferment in Japanese thought has prevented their concrete
statement. Without doubt, there are Fascistic tendencies in the army,
notably in the Kwantung Army or Itagaki faction which has gained prac-
tically complete control of Manchouluo and put its theories of State
control into practice there. This group believes war with Russia is
inevitable and insists that money must be forthcoming to prepare the
army for it. A few cautious men in the military hierarchy, sensing
the public's dissatisfaction vith the army's expansionist program,
averted an Itagaki-controlled cabinet, but there is as yet no assurance
that the program for greater State control at the expense of parlia-
mentary authority can be forestalled indefinitely, despite the brave
words of liberals like Yukio Ozaki, Kunimatsu Hamada, and Foreign
Minister Sato. The parties have not regained their former prestige
with the people and even in the heyday of their influence lacked the
power wielded by political organizations in democratic countries; hence
they are not in a good position to awalten the people to what looms
ahead.
The greatest menace to parliamentary government in Japan now
is the current attempt to form a new party of Fascistlike character
which will do the bidding cf its army mentors. Rumors of the formation
of such a party have been current for some time and were given greater
substance by Premier Hayashi's announcement following the Diet disso-
lution in which the folloving significant sentences occurred. "I think
there would be nothing wrong should the Government decide to cooperate
with a new party that might come into cxistence and support it," he
declared. "The Government has not yet considered whether it should
take positive action in the direction of forming a new party. It would
be good if a party ready to support the Government came into being of
its own accord." The Itagaki faction is known to be promoting the
formation of such an organization, but apparently couid not bring it to
pass in time for the coming election. Prince Konoe, President of the
House of Peers (Upper House of the Diet) is reported to have refused
-16163

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
an invitation to head the new party, and no other leader of comparable
stature has been mooted.
Even if this party should be created, it would not be the
servant of a dictator like Mussolini or Hitler. The Japanese political
systen with a venerated Emperor at its head does not stimulate the rise
of a single leader who vould or could dare to appropriate any of the
Imperial authority, theoretical though much of it is. The purification
instituted by the army in self-defense since the February 26th inci-
dent and normal transfers of officers, moreover, have dispersed the
direct-action groups vhich clamored for a military dictatorship.
General Itagari himself nov has a routine command at some distance
from Tolyo.
Other circumstances militating against a Fascist state of
the European model in Japan are the character of the Japanese people
and the absence of specific conditions such as those prompting the rise
of the German and Italian dictatorships. Japanese have long been sub-
ject to thought control and regimentation and the exaltation of the
fanily and the clan above the individual. In that sense, Japan has
been a Fascistic state fcr somc time.
The attitude of the Japanese Navy in the present situation
and in the further bid for power which the army will certainly make is
uncertain. The Minister of the Navy tried to mediate to save the
Hirota Governmcnt from dissolution but vithout success, as already men-
tioned. The navy hierarchy forestalled the appointment of Admiral
Suetsugu and offered the higher ranking and more popular Admiral Yonai
in his placo in an attempt to keep the Hayashi Cabinet moderate. Yet
it was Admiral Yonai who took the lead in bringing about the dissolu-
tion of the Diet finally. This is interpreted to mean that the navy,
while averse to some of the army's policies, is not wholly in favor
of parliamentary government. Whether the navy would support action to
promote its much talked of "Southvard Advance" policy in opposition to
army-directed moves for expansion on the mainland remains one of the
imponderables in the present situation.
Of more immediate interest is the effect the country's eco-
nomic condition may have on the militarists' plans. An alarming rise
in commodity prices began toward the end of 1936 which has resulted in
an epidemic of strikes such as Japan has not seen for a number of
years. The higher cost of living had not tended to reconcile the
pecple to the enormously increased budget, due mostly to army and navy
demands, particularly because none of the crises so much heralded by
the military has vet occurred. The Hayashi Government's decision to
shelve certain tax plans has eased the situation temporarily, but will
only result in larger loans to cover the budget deficit. These long
continuing budget delicits constitute a greve wealness in Japan's
economic structure, as is well kmown.
The general election campaign is now nearing its end and when
this article appears in print the Japanese electorate, numbering pos-
sibly 15,000,000, will have registered its choice at the polls. An
overwhelming victory for the political parties has been predicted, but
this must be discounted in view of the very recent appeal of the Im-
perial Ex-Servicemen's Association to its 3,000,000 members, exhorting
them to vote "in accordance with the military spirit" for those
-16164-

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
candidates vho realize the necessity of strengthening national defense
and who know the gravity of the situation at home and abroad. The
political parties are denouncing this action as "another example of
military interference in politics" but the inherent loyalty of the
former recruits may prompt them to follow the association's behest.
The appeal would make it appear that a considerable number of candi-
dates favoring army views is in the field, but no information on this
point is available.
What will the militarists do if the parties do win a de-
cisive victory? One guess is that the army will compel further dis-
solutions and general elections until a Diet villing to do its bidding
is obtained. The strength of the opposition to such procedure is un-
assessable at present, but the near future should see important de-
velopments in this clash betwecn the military and the parties.

Mr. Secretary:
Department of State
NOTED
The Parliamontary BIections in Japan
894.00
The parliamentary elections in Japan, held on
April 30 following the dissolution of the Lower House on
March 30, have resulted in the return of a heavy majority
in favor of the two large parties avowedly opposed to the
cabinet of General Hayashi. The press, both in Japan and
in this country, interprets the outcome as a distinct
894.00/73
defeat for the Cabinet. The Cabinet is now faced by the
choice between resignation and an attempt to remain in
power through the extraordinary session of the Diet which
under the Japanese Constitution must be convened no later
2
than the latter part of August.
It seems apparent that the Cabinet, which is dominated
by the military, has no immediate intention of resigning,
despite the adverse vote. The measures which it will take
to remain in power, in face of the declared opposition of
the parties and in face of the overwhelming anti-govern-
ment vote of the people, remain obscure. The military
leaders behind the Cabinet, in their determination to
remain in power, may be planning to resort to an
indefinite number of subsequent dissolutions, or to some
F/FG
other device, to defeat the parties and to nullify the
ordinary workings ofparliamentary government.
It

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
It may develop, however, that some form of balance
may be reached between the parties and the Government. The
parties are aware of the futility of attempting to insist
upon a party government in the face of military opposition;
the Cabinet has just sustained a severe loss of prestige
by the adverse popular vote and may well desire to avoid a
struggle with the Diet. Out of this situation the Japanese
genius for compromise may effect a modus vivendi which
would enable the Cabinet to remain in power even after the
Diet convenes.
The press in this country has hailed the outcome of
the elections as a rebuke to the military and as a portent
of a resurgent liberal spirit. Although it is true that
militarism is less popular now than during the past few
years, it would be fallacious to regard the vote as a
victory of liberalism. Actually, the recent elections
should be interpreted more as a repudiation of the Hayashi
cabinet than as an indictment of the military. The fact
that the voting was very light may be taken as evidence
that the national consciousness has not been deeply
stirred, as it would be by an important national issue.
In any event the military are far too strongly entrenched,
both in public esteem and in the system of government, to
be affe cted very seriously by the outcome of this election.

NAY 1 8 1937
The Secretary of State,
Jashington.
Sir:
At various times in the past years Mr. Naotake Sato,
present Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairs, has ex-
pressed the conviction that a satisfactory adjustment of
foreign relations requires a fairer international dis-
tribution of natural resources. Although as an actual
guide in foreign policy this thought is no doubt not so
effective in the Foreign Minister's mind as are the
working principles discussed in the Embassy's despatch
No. 2312, March 17, 1937, the Embassy nevertheless is
of opinion that the subject merits close study. This
F
/FG
opinion

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
opinion seems to the Embassy particularly warranted
if consideration is being given to the question of
participation in any international economic conference.
On April 21, 1937, the Foreign Minister attended,
and addressed, a farewell banquet to the economic mission
which is shortly to leave for America and Europe(Em-
bassy's telegram No. 119, April 23, 4 p.m.). It was a
large function and several cabinet ministers were present.
Mr. Sato in his address stated that since 1927 the free
trade principle has lapsed, that very recently there
is noticeable a trend in the direction of its re-
establishment, and that Japan desires to join the move-
ment toward economic internationalism. Mr. Sato's
words, in their Japanese text, were not simply a plea
for lowering of tariff barriers, although that was their
apparent primary a plication; they were construable as a
statement in favor of some broader (though undefined)
removal of international obstacles to economic develop-
ment.
The following day, at a dinner at the Embassy, a
member of my staff (Mr. Coville) took occasion in con-
versation with the Foreign Linister to express interest
in the address at the economic mission farewell. Mr.
Sato responded immediately with the remark that what he
had had in mind was the basic thought expressed by
Colonel Edward M. House in certain articles published
in 1935. Mr. Sato and Lir. Coville then discussed the
form in which Colonel House's opinions had been presented
in

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
in the Japanese press, and in the course of the con-
versation the Foreign Minister stated that he would
send to Mr. Coville two publications bearing on the
subject. They have since been received and copies are
enclosed with this despatch: one is a Japanese Foreign
1/
Office reprint of Colonel House's article entitled "Wanted --
A New Deal Among Nations" (LIBERTY, September 14, 1935),
accompanied by Japanese translation; and the other is
"The Problem of Population and Industrialization in Japan",
a paper by Mir. Sato himself, read on January 27, 1936,
before the Centre d'Etudes de Politique Etrangère, Paris,
when Mr. Sato was ambassador to France.
The fact that the Foreign Office has seen fit to
translate and to print for its use the Colonel House
article is in itself worth noting. There are indica-
tions from time to time that the article has become
part of the required reading of men in the Japanese
foreign service, and it is probable thet the Foreign
Office staff has devoted detailed study to some plan
in harmony with the House principles. What those prin-
ciples are is no doubt well known to the Department:
"Germany, Italy and Japan need reservoirs into which
to pour their man power and from which to draw those
necessities and raw materials which nature denied them ....
I do not recommend annexation by force ........There are
still vast waste places throughout the earth. Why not
allow the Japanese to make them productive? ....Just as
social peace cannot prevail without some adjustment of
the

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
the capitalistic system, so international peace cannot
be preserved without drastic territorial readjustments.
Great Britain, France, Russia and the United States must
receive Italy, Germany and Japan on terms adjusted to
present world conditions and recognize their insistence
upon being given their proper part of the colonial re-
sources of the world. Chaos and catastrophe will be on
us unless those that have among the Powers are willing
to share in some way with those that have not." (Quoting
is from enclosure No. 1; the Embassy does not possess an
original copy of the issue of LIBERTY in which the article
was printed.)
By comparison with Colonel House's dogmatism, Mr.
Sato's approach to the problems of Japan's world position,
in his address of January 27, 1936 (enclosure No. 2), is
studious and restrained. Territorial readjustments are
not the solution aavocated; for remedy Mr. Sato looks
to further Japanese industrialization, supported by
access to raw materials and access to markets. He opens
the address with a discussion of Japan's population growth,
which subject he treats with commendable soundness. In
this matter he follows Professor Teijiro Ueda of Tokyo
(one of the most thorough of Japanese scholars of the
social sciences), and quotes with apparent approval
the opinion of Professor Ueda that there is already evi-
dence pointing to the cessation of increase in the popu-
lation of Japan in the not very distant future. Mr. Sato
believes this is for the best, and puts himself down as an
advocate

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
advocate of birth control and lowering of the birth rate.
This, however, as he points out, is no solution of the
problems arising from the large numbers of children already
born; and he takes the estimate of from 210,000 to 290,000
new arrivals annually in the labor market as the approximate
number to be expected each year for the next twenty years.
He then finds emigration and agrarian reform impotent as
remedies, and states that the solution must be found in
further industrialization.
The address then moves on to ex lanatory statements
about Jepanese industry in refutation of charges of low
wages and living standards, and in refutation of the state-
ments of alarm with which Japanese trade expansion has been
greeted abroad. Lir. Sato points out that in 1934, the year
of latest available statistics, Japan's exports were only
three and one-third percent of total world exports . Pursuing
the argument of the inevitability of further industrializa-
tion of Japan, Mr. Sato proceeds: "...it is indispensable
for Japan to have access, on the one hand, to raw materials
whiclh, apart from copper and coal, she lacks almost en-
tirely, and, on the other, to outlets for her manufectured
goods. Raw materials and foreign markets -- here are two
problems which have often in the past been the cause of
dangerous conflicts. They can become dangerous again if
the Powers, small as well as great, do not make a concerted
effort to destroy the cause of the evil." Mr. Sato next
quotes from Sir Samuel Hoare, then British Foreign Minister,
statements (to the League of Nations Assembly, September 11,
1935) which Lir. Sato characterizes as revealing complete
understanding:

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
understanding: " 'Abundant supplies of raw materials,'
said Sir Samuel Hoare, 'appear to give peculiar advantage
to the countries possessing them. It is easy to exaggerate
the decisive character of such an advantage, for there are
countries which, having little or no natural abundance,
have yet made themselves prosperous and powerful by in-
dustry and trade. Yet the fact remains that some countries
either in their native soil or in their colonial territories,
do possess what appear to be preponderant advantages; and
that others, less favored, view the situation with anxiety.
..So far as His Liajesty's Government is concerned, I
feel sure that we should be ready to take our share in an
investigation of these matters . . . I suggest that the
emphasis in the terms of reference should fall upon free
distribution of such raw materials among the industrial
countries which require them, so that the fear of exclu-
sion or monopoly may be removed once and for all.' "
Mr. Sato passes to the vital importance of markets,
and says: "Some authorities . . . . add that it would be
singularly dangerous for the maintenance of peace to
close territories or to monopolize colonies. For my
part, I would not go so far. Peace is not to be troubled
except in the most extreme cases. The problems of daily
life will be solved by our statesmen, who will know how
to avoid conflicts of a purely economic origin." Mr.
Sato then quotes from Colonel House and commends him.
In conclusion Mr. Sato bespeaks a policy of good under-
standing and cooperation in the economic field: "A policy
of this kind can assume many forms: it is incompatible
neither

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
neither with a return to freedom of exchange, nor with
a maintenance of certain measures of restriction; it
can be expressed through a distribution of markets among
rival countries or by the allocation to certain countries

of quotas in each market . . . It is always better to ne-
gotiate than to take refuge in distrust and abstention . . .
I repeat that the worst policy would be to oppose the West
against the East."
So much for the Foreign Minister's expression of his
fundamental convictions with regard to the economics of
Japan's foreign relations. It is not the purpose of the
Embassy to distort the importance of these politico-eco-
nomic ideas, to express which Mr. Sato is not the first
Japanese. Un the other hand, Premier Hayashi was unquestion-
ably cognizant of Mr. Sato's opinions before he was invited
into the Cabinet, and there is some weight to the evidence
that the Foreign Minister holds these views at the present
time, while in his high office. The attitude of the Japa-
nese Government toward the situation prevailing in the
Congo Lasin is one manifestation (Embassy's despatch No.
2280, February 26, 1937).

A recent article in the ASAHI (see third enclosure) further
indicates that the Foreign Minister plans to present to the
June meeting of the League of Nations committee on raw
materials a protest against such controlsas the practical
monopolization of Malayan tin, Columbian oil, and Chilean

exploitation, and against tariffs and economic blocs.

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
The Hayashi Cabinet's tenure of office is not
entirely secure, but, short of resignation of the
whole Cabinet in some Diet impasse at least several
months distant, Mr. Sato's position appears fairly
solid. Even in event of Cabinet resignation, Mr. Sato
might well serve in the following Cabinet also. That a
man of his temperament and experience can be Foreign
Minister under the anomalous present administration led by
a soldier who is defiant of the Diet is another example
of the surprises which litter Japanese politics. Mr.
Sato is a studious man, not at all an alarmist. A disci-
ple and admirer of the late Dr. Inazo Nitobe, who was
for many years the country's most noted internationalist,
Mr. Sato has a wide following among Japanese scholars.
He is completely convinced of the rightness of his policy,
which he sees as simply commonplace, common sense justice,
eventually destined to acceptance because of its reason
ableness. He is a man of great determination.

Joseph C. Grew.

SATO MAY ADVANCE
RESOURCE THEORIES
Three Principles Considered for
Submission to League Raw
Materials Committee
Fereign Minister Naotake Sato plans
to advance three basic principles at the
June mecting of the League of Nations
committee on raw materials, namely,
freedom in the acquisition of raw mate-
rials. freedom in the exploitation of
raw materials and freedom of trade and
commerce.
The first principle is based on the
contention that raw material markets
are virtually monopolized by a number
of nations. which in general keep the
door closed even in peace time, says
the Asahi. The expioitation and ex-
portation of Malayan tin, Colombian
oil. Chilean saltpeter and other raw
materials are under strong control.
which constitutes a menace to countries
not rich in resources. Raw materials
should be distributed on a fair basis,
Minister Sato is expected to argue.
Restrictions on the people who have
the power to exploit the undeveloped
resources of Africa and various islands
in the South Seas are contrary to the
welfare and prosperity of mankind, the
Foreign Minister believes, the paper
continues.
Formation of economic bloes and
erection of tariff walls have greatly
decreased the freedom of trade and
commerce. and there is an urgent
necessity for breaking down such a
condition, the league committee is to be
told.
Mr. Yasundo Sudo, commercial se-
cretary attached to the Embassy at Ber-
lin. will represent Japan at the meet-
ing. He also attended the committee's
March meeting. Mr. Sudo has been
instructed to come to Japan for a con-
sultation on the raw materials ques-
tion. the paper adds.

WANTED—A NEW DEAL
AMONG NATIONS
By Colorel Edward Mandell House
From the "Liberty", September 5, 1935.
"Italy must expand or explode." This
statement was made by Il Duce to George
S.lvester Viereck some years ago. The legions
of Mussolini marching into Africa may lay
the foundation for a new Roman Empire great
enough to give the Italian people a chance to
breathe. If Mussolini succeeds, Italy will
expand in Africa without exploding in
Europe. Let us hope that this may be done
without improperly exploiting the rights of
other people.
What Mussolini said of Italy holds true
equally of Germany and Japan. Every
statesman will admit in private conversation
that Germany, Italy and Japan need reser-
voirs into which to pour their man power and
from which to draw those necessities and raw
1

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
materials which nature denied them. But
the great possessing nations—Great Britain,
France, the United States, and Russia-are
unwilling to grant to their less fortunate
fellows more than crumbs that fall from their
colonial table.
Hate, suspicion, and economic disaster
walked in the trail of the so-called Peace
Treaty of Versailles. The Peace Treaty de
prived Germany of all her colonies; it did
not fulfill the 'colonial promises made to
Italy, and Japan was held in check. As a
result, Germany produced Hitler, the over.
compensation of her inferiority complex;
Mussolini stirred up trouble in Europe; and
Japan is looking for her easiest and most
effective channel into which she may pour her
superfiuous population and trade. There can
be no stability until the vital demands of
these three great nations are met. I do not
recommend annexation by force, nor the
ruthless exploitation of native populations,
but some satisfactory arrangement should be
reached.
Would not Gernany be less likely to boil
over if she got back the colonies mandated to
her enemies? There are, possibly, certain
2

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
regions that, for vital and strategic and other
reamons, cannot be returned. But all legiti-
mate German wishes might be met by various
devices.
For one thing. it should not be impossible
to revive the agreement between England and
Germany made shortly before the World War.
It is generally understood that there was a
provisional treaty already initialed by the
British Foreign Minister, which gave Germany
the right to acquire by purchase the Portu-
guese colonies. If the Reich receives some of
its old colonies, and if she can purchase the
Portuguese colonies and maybe the Belgian
Congo, Germany will be kept busy. Her
thoughts will not steal toward her neighbor's
property in Europe (although certain adjust-
ments in the East wonld still be advisable).
he will exploit her matchless energy for the
commereial penetration and cultivation of a
large colonial empire. It was Bismarek him-
self who urged this policy to keep Franee from
Iscoming too restless after her defeat in 1871.
It was Bismarck who aided France to acquire
more colonial territory. The world cannot
deny to the German what it freely gave to the
Frenehman.
3

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
A contented Germany and a coutented Italy
will be more effcient guaranties of world
peace than any pacts or treaties, secret or
avowed. The problem of Germany and of Italy
is important to the United States Isecause the
worid is a living whole. The problem of Japan
is of even more immediate consequence to our
country.
Most Westerners look upon the Japanese as
the most likely breeders of serious interna-
tional trouble. They recognize parts of Central
Europe as disturbing areas, but it is felt,
rightly or wrongly, that with the Great Powers
bent on peace. any trouble arising in this part
of our mundane sphere may be localized.
With Japan it is different. Japan cannot
be controlled. It looks as if she it inds to
carry out any policy that appears to her l .d.
ers as of benefit to the country's larger pur
poses. Already meveral major moves lave been
made in this direction. One was the with-
drawal from the League of Nations in order
to have unrestricted action. Another was the
notice that she had no intention of renewing
the Washington Naval Treaty.
From my point of view both were mistaken.
My sympathy is largely with the Japanese
4

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
and what they seem to have in mind. But I
think they are choosing the wrong time and
the wrong methods of bringing it about. The
world is in too much turmoil now. It needs
composure if it is to right itself. Let the
constructive forces at work in almost every
country succced, and what Japan desires will
come about easily and naturally.
It is understandable that Japan should wish
the dominating influence in the Far East, and
she has a right to demand an outlet for the
crowded population within her restricted
borders. There are still vast waste places
throughout the earth. Why not allow the
Japanese to make them productive and add
to the wealth and happiness of mankind?
Heretofore such things have been accomplished
by war, but we want no more wars of aggres-
sion. Why not me whether the problem can-
not lx worked out to matisfy the Japanese
without antagonizing other Great Powers?
I have had the opportunity to discuss the
problems of Japan with some of the wisest
of her statesmen. These conversations have
opened my eyes to certain facts which I had
hitherto only dimly perceived. I had been
somewhat prejudiced against Japan by the
5

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
clamor of her militarista, and by her refusal
to cooperate with other nations. Today I
see the situation in a different light.
There are two opposing groupe in Japan,
one led by the younger militarists, and the
other by such mature statesmen as Count
Makino. The situation there is not very dif-
ferent from what it is elmewhere among the
Great Powers, including the I'nited States.
Blatant propaganda for increasd armaments
on land and sea makes the public believe that
the mafety of their country is in danger.
Until recently, an overwhelming proportion
of the Japanem desired peace and believed
that it could be arsured hy a decreame in
armaments rather than by violent inerease.
This opinion may still be in the majority. I
hope it is, but the militarists are more noisy
there, just as they are here.
The militarists are isolationists. Clamor
ing for a big army and a big navy, they want
to live without interference, or cooperation
with the rest of the world. If this party
gains control, trouble will amost certainly be
the result. But I have contidence in the sober
judgment of the masses of Japan, and if we
do our part, the future will see us working
6

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
with them for a common purpose.
This can never be done by taking a narrow,
isolated and wholly melfish view. If we and
other nations contemplating policies based on
unenlightened national egotism should carry
out these intentions, failure is inevitable. It
may sound well to insist upon living withim
ourselves and being self-supporting. A pro-
hibitive tariff may seem mound economic
sense. A refusal to take any part in discus-
mions and wettlements of international pro-
blems may seem wise. But, as a matter of
fact, it cannot be done by such a nation as
the United States. Isolation is manifestly
mot our destiny.
In almost all countries there is a strong
sentiment for isolation. With human society
organized as at present this is practically
impossible. If complete chaos should be the
outcome of the present disturbance, and we
should evolve a different scheme for carrying
on the world's affairs, this might be done.
But if we plan to right our problems and go
on in mome such way as we have in the past,
we need more rather than less international
cooperation. India, China, and perhaps even
Russia, might exist and be content to live
7

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
entirely on their own resources, but not
Americans. If isolation, autarchy, narrow
nationalism, is the best that our virile, cour-
ageous, highly educated, one hundred and
thirty millions of peoples can do with the
wealth and the machinery they have created
from their mines, from their factories and
from their land, then the American experiment
is not worth while -we have not justified our
existence.
I refuse to be a pessimist. America is not
at the end but at the beginning. It is my
belief that steadily we will advance along the
path we have chosen. We will make the
world a better place in which to live, and lift
mankind to a higher and better plane.
We cannot refuse to be our brothers keep
ers; we cannot "disinterest" ourselves in
Europe and Asia. Chance, destiny, or fate
-call it what you will—has given us an
opening to take leadership in the effort
toward the betterment of our race. If we
heed the isolationists, we shall become the
China of the Western World. I say this with
all respect for the Chinese. They are a re-
markable people, with a unique civilization
behind them, but their theory of government
8

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.98)
is centuries behind the times. They cannot
cope with the thought of the rest of the worl
or of their virile neighbors, the Japanese,
who are becoming more modern than the
Western peoples themselves.
The yellow race, led by its most progressive
members, will coalesce eventually. It is
desirable that this bloe almost invineible if to
the weight of numbers is added the new
impetus which China as well as Japan has
received from the Western world—should not
be imbued with antagonism against the white
race. But it will be thus imbued if we are
unwilling to envisage a new deal interna-
tionally as well as nationally.
Germany will be dissatisfied until she
regains what the exile in Doorn has called
her "place in the sun." The tension in
Europe will lead to new disasters unless the
imperial urge of Mussolini has the oppor-
tunity to spend itself on African soil. Japan
will see red until her crying needs are ack-
nowledged. The four Powers possessing the
world- Great Britain, France, Russia, and
the United States must realize that mankind
can no tonger le governed by the adage, "He
that hath, to him shall be given: and he that
9

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
hath not, from him shall be taken even that
which he hath."
Just as social peace cannot prevail without
some adjustment of the capitalistic system,
so international peace cannot be preserved
without drastic territorial readjustments.
Great Britain, France, Russia and the United
States must receive Italy, Germany. and
Japan on terms adjusted to present world
conditions and recognize their insistence upon
being given their proper part of the colonial
resources of the world. Chaos and catastrophe
will be upon us unless those that have among
the Powers are willing to share in some way
with those that have not.

=== Page 171 === (confidence 1.00)
NEW DEAL AMONG
NATIONS
By Prince Fumimaro Konoe
From the “Liberty", December 4, 1935.
The greatest problem posed for humanity
is how to secure lasting peace for the advan-
cement of mankind. From time immemorial
thinking men of all countries pondered this
problem. Opinions have been expressed,
movements have been launched.
Yet the solution has not been found, the
eternal quest still goes on. Today, we are
confronted with the need for a reëxamination
of the conceptions and contrivances which
have been advanced for world peace. We are
bidden to find a new approach to the old
question, we must devise a new system based
upon a new idea.
What, then, is the matter with the prevail-
ing peace system?
In the past the world has devoted much
11

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
attention to the methods of preserving peace,
but has been inclined to ignore the causes
which threatened it.
Prior to the World War the chancellories
of Europe strove to keep peace by maintain-
ing a balance of power among the leading
nations. That ended in failure.
After the war the statesmen hoped to
preserve world peace by a system of collective
security embodied in the League of Nations.
The result has hardly measured up to the
expectation. Europe has continued to be
haunted with a sense of insecurity. Not only
that, but war has virtually started in East
Africa embroiling two nations who are both
members of the League.
Peace, then, is as much a matter of devis-
ing methods or systems as it is a question of
dealing with the causes which are at the
bottom of international friction. The peace
pacts and non-aggression treaties, noble in
conception and useful in practice. have not
dissipated fears of war, because they faited
to take full cognizance of those causes.
Universal brotherhood and common happi-
ness are the ideal of mankind. In our
struggle towards that goad we most hear in
12

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.88)
mind that there are some peoples which are.
un der the existing world order, denied the
opprortromity to develon their native capacity
ad to fulfil their mission as independent
nr They fel That their national exis
to t ro tled. their natural development
t! orted. To them it is a matter of life and

of peaee And the proper approach to this
en s  a conderation of how to secure a
mmore equitable  ment by all nations of
land, raty meteriods and other resources which

le mrintenance of the status quo in the
13

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
distribution of land and resources musi in-
evitably remain a source of international fric-
tion and conflict.
If the above premise is true, the road to
peace is not far to seek. It seems to lie in
the mutual recognition among all nations of
the peculiar and especial needs, requirements,
and positions of cach, with a view to meeting
legitimate aspirations for peaceful develop-
ment on the part of those progressive yet
unfortunate nations whose naturai capacity
for advancement has been checked by their
lack of the necessary resources.
It, then, behooves the fortunate nations,
which are blessed by the status quo, to be
prepared to readjust their fortune and to share
their happiness with the unfortunate nations
whose existence has been made dificnlt or
whose natural growth has been checkmated by
the same status quo.
It is not wise to fill old bottles with new
wine. Metaphorically, new wines have been
and still are formenting in our modern world
in tie shape of once backward or dormant na-
tions now conscions of their innate abilities
and demamnding a due share in the benetits
which they think have monopolized by the
11

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
powers who happened to appear before them
upon the stage of world politics. Cram them
down in the old bottle of the status quo, and
the bottle may burst.
Thus the process of equalization seems to be
at work. It cannot be repressed without dis
astrous consequences. The only wise way is
to guide it into the right channel.
Colonel Edward M. House refers, in his
admirable artiele, to Japan's withdrawal from
the League of Nations and her renunciation
of the Washington Naval Treaty.
It will be recalled that Japan disagreed
with the League of Nations on the question
of maintaining the peace of Eastern Asia, and
that the disagreement brought about her with
drawal from the League. Had the League had
a clearer understanding of the Far Eastern
situation and of Japan's peculiar position
therein, it might not have adopted the mea-
sures which forced us out of its own fold.
Unfortunate as our withdrawal from Geneva
appeared to be, it at least caused the League
to ponder and reflect on the problems of the
Far East. Because of it, the League today,
it seems to me, has a better appreciation of
these problems.
15

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
Japan's renunciation of the Naval Treaty
of Washington furnishes another event whose
real import has not fully been appreciated by
the western world. Japan is in this respeet
actuated by a sincere desire to effect a sub-
stantial reduetion in naval armaments with
its corollary of lightening the financial bur-
dens now weighing upon all tax ridden na-
tions. If the powers under-taned Japan's
motive as well as the mission imposed upon
her by reason of her peculiar position in the
Far East, they will not fail to appreciate the
stand she has taken on the naval question.
It is refreshing to see so enlightened an
American statesman as Colonel House frankly
recognize defects in the peace policies hereto-
fore followed by the leading powers, and
openly urge a new deal in the readjustment
of international relations. He seems to think
that emphasis should be laid not so much
upon the system of maintaining peace as upon
the method of eliminating the factor. which
menace peace.
Among nations, as among individuals, the
"haves" always resist the clamors of the
“have nots", however justitiable such clamors
may be. But to resist them indefinitely and
16

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
uncompromisingly would spell disaster to
peace, to civilization, to humanity itself. By
common consent foremost among the nations
that Fave" are the United States of America.
Great Britain, France, and Soviet Russia,
follos ed. perhaps, by a few other countries.
The time seems to be at hand when great,
rich. powerful nations, animated by a spirit
of noblesse oblige, should face the problem
squarely and courageously, and do their part
in the establishment of a new and lasting
peace.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 1, 1937
MA:
The Japanese Cabinet is reported to
have approved the establishment of a pro-
posed Planning Board (Kikakucho). It
appears that the Board is to incorporate the
Cabinet Inquiry Bureau and will have com-
prehensive functions. It is apparently
designed to meet the needs of a planned
economy, and seems to be in part a
manifestation of the military demand for
administrative reform which has persisted
since the February 26 Incident of 1936.
The Embassy comments that it is
impossible to state at the moment whether
or not the government hopes to make
the Planning Board the central organ of a
future fascist government. Until the pro-
posed Board assumes more concrete form there
is no reason for speculating in detail about
its personnel and powers.

Cabinet is reported to have reopened the matter by ap-
