Proposals in l936 of administrative reform, of which
the War Minister's plan of September 22 was the climax,
were superseded in the public interest by a succession
of more important questions (relations with China, the
Cabinet change, Diet dissolution), and nothing was heard

take it up again. Premier Hayashi, a military man, ap-
parently has no interest in avoiding it; suddenly the
Cabinet is reported to have taken active steps in this
army-favored matter, without any spurring from the public.
Until an official decision is actually made (which
has not yet been made) there is no reason for speculating
in detail about the personnel and powers of the proposed
1/
Planning Board. A clipping enclosed herewith lists many
important men in finance and business who have been sug-
ested by Domei as likely members. It would appear that
the Board is a project proposed to meet the needs of a
planned economy in a manner which no existing body can;
and it would appear that it is in part a manifestation
of Premier Hayashi's conviction that the ordinary political
institutions of Japan are inadequate to the governmental
leadership which he believes is essential to the modern
needs of the nation. It is impossible to state, at the
moment, whether or not the present Government hopes to

make the Planning Board the control organ of a future

The past two weeks have, in fact, brought forth
a multitude of suggestions for new commissions and bureaus.
Among them are a new propaganda bureau for the Cabinet,
a central economic council, and a commission on the rise
of prices. The phenomenon is a reasonable consequence of
distrust in the constituted political machinery. Edi-
torial opinion generally reflects impatience with the
multiplicity of organs proposed.

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.98)
EA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 20, 1937
This despatch reporting in regard to
the Japanese elections was written on
May 1, on the day following the elections.
It adds very little to what is already
known in FE.
Only about sixty percent of the
qualified voters exercised their right,
showing that the public was apathetic.
The most illuminating declaration in this
colorless campaign was made by Finance
Minister Yuki, who is more and more
proving himself the outstanding personality
of the Hayashi Cabinet. Mr. Yuki stated
that after the elections the Government
will assist the formation of a new party
by men who share the Government's views,
and that if necessary the Government will
thereafter not hesitate to dissolve the
Diet again. The Embassy interprets this
statement as meaning that the Government
is determined not to permit the old
party system of Government to return.

DÉPARTMENT OF STE
Sir:
A general election throughout Japan for choice of
members of the House of Representatives was held yesterday,
April 30, 1937. Partial reports thus far are in no way
startling or significant in change. Public interest was
not great, and abstention from voting reflected the athy
atorage of forty percent of the qualified voters absmaihed;
Bocause of delay of the ship carrying the pouch
scheduled
F/FG

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
scheduled to leave today, this despatch will be held
1/
open to enclose a clipping from tomorrow morning's press,
with partial election results.
The last two weeks of the campaign offered few
developments to modify the statements of the Embassy's
1729
despatch No. 2367/of April 16, 1937, reporting on the
campaign until that date. No party stepped forward to
accept the Premier's open invitation for champions. No
definition was offered by the Cabinet as to what, if any,
result of the election would be taken as defeat. No
promise was made of the Government's future course con-
tingent upon the elections. No indication was given by
the Cabinet as to how the voters might register either
support or protest. In fact, no convincing argument was
offered in substantiation of the oft-heard high moral
exhortation to vote.
In the absence of issues, the Government unctiously
confined its efforts to insisting on clean elections.
When the Minister of Commerce and Industry made an in-
nocuous forecast to a newspaper reporter to the effect
that one of the major parties might lose ten seats or so,
the Metropolitan Police notified the newspapers that the
Minister's statement could not be printed -- might be
prejudicial to the free elections.
The most illuminating declaration in this colorless
campaign was made by Finance Minister Yuki, who is more
and more proving himself the outstanding personality of
the Hayashi Cabinet. Mr. Yuki stated that after the
elections the Government will assist the formation of
a

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
a new party by new men who share the Government's views,
and that if necessary the Government will thereafter
not hesitate to dissolve the Diet again. He also stated
that the Government's chief object in the dissolution
was the disciplinary effect on the members of the House
of Representatives. Perhaps if he had spoken with even
greater frankness he would have clarified the actual
situation by saying: Non-party government since 1932
has been better than the party government before that time;
we shall not permit a return to party government; with
time we can form a new party and control the Diet; mean-
time, if the Diet should by chance get out of hand, there
can be another change of non-party cabinet, and nothing
lost; but whatever happens we shall not go back to the
old party system.
A reply given by the Premier to a question in the
Privy Council suggests that the new Diet will be con-
vened in August, and that the Government hopes that the
session can be confined to ten days or so.
Respectfully yours,
JophS. Jur
Joseph C. Grew.

THE PROBLEM OF POPULATION

LE PROBLÈME DE LA POPULATION

=== Page 209 === (confidence 1.00)
THE PROBLEM OF POPULATION AND
INDUSTRIALISATION IN JAPAN
I wish to thank you, Mr. Chairman, for the very kind
words with which you have introduced me. I wish to ex-
press also my thanks to the Study Centre for having given me
to-day this opportunity of addressing such a distinguished
audience. I must confess that I rather hesitated before decid-
ing to accept its invitation, for I am neither an economist nor
a jurist; indeed, I can give you only opinions based on a
diplomatist's personal experience. If I am speaking here to-day.
it is because ! was tempted to lay before you two problems
which are particularly important not only to my own country.
but also to the improvement of international relations. I should
like to repeat, however, that the reflections which I am submit-
ting for appraisal by French scholarship are quite simple. My
object will be attained if I am able to call attention to certain
aspects of these problems.
I.
THE POPULATION QUESTION.
Growth of Population.
The total area of the Japanese Empire is 675,118 square
kilometres. Japan proper, however, has an area of only
1) Address deiivered by Mr. Naotake Sato. Japanese Ambassador to France
at the meeting of the members of the Centre d'Etudes de Politique Etrangère
Jan. 27, 1936 with M. André Siegfried, membre de l'Institut, professeur au
College de France, presiding.

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
LE PROBLÈME DE LA POPULATION ET
L'INDUSTRIALISATION DU JAPON
Je vous remercie, Monsieur le Président, des paroles trop
aimables par lesquelles vous venez de m'accueillir; et je re-
mercie le Centre d'Études de l'occasion qu'il me donne de
prendre la parole aujourd'hui devant un auditoire si distingué.
J'avoue que j'ai hésité avant d'accepter cette invitation: je ne
suis ni orateur, ni économiste, ni juriste; la seul chose que je
puisse vous apporter, ce sont les réflexions qu'inspire à un
diplomate son expérience personnelle. Mais je me suis laissé tenter
par l'occasion de poser devant vous deux problèmes particulière-
ment importants non seulement pour mon pays, mais pour
l'evolution des rapports internationaux. Je répète que ce sont
de simples réflexions que je soumets à l'appréciation des
savants français. Mon but serait atteint si je pouvais
contribuer à attirer leur attention sur certains aspect de ces
problèmes
1.
LA QUESTION DE LA POPULATION.
Accroissement de la population.
L'Empire japonais a une superficie totale de 675.118
kilomètres carrés; mais le Japon proprement dit ne compte que
(1) Conférence faite devant les membres du Centre d'Etudes de Politique
Etrangère, le 27 janvier 1936, par M. Naotake Sato, Ambassadeur extraordinaire
et plénipotentiaire du Japon en France, sous la présidence de M. André
Siegfried. Membre de l'Institut, Professeur au Collège de France

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
382,314 square kilometres, with a population of 69,251,265,
according to the census of October 1, 1935. These figures
show that in Japan proper, that is to say, in the most im
portant and thickly populated part of the country, the density
of population is considerable, namely 181 inhabitants per
square kilometre. This is a high density, even when compared,
for example, with the density in Belgium-the most thickly
populated country in the world—which, in 1934, hadi a popula-
tion of 271 inhabitants per square kilometre. In Belgium,
however, the ratio of arable land to the total area of the
country is 403 to 1000, whereas in Japan the ratio is only 155
to 1000. Thus, in proportion to cultivable area, the density of
population is far greater in Japan than in Belgium, the
statistics for 1933 showing densities of, respectively, 1120 and
670 inhabitants per square kilometre of arable land.
Moreover, the population in Japan is not stationary. It
is rapidly expanding. In recent years, the total number of
inhabitants has increased by about one million a year, and it
is generally held that the rate of annual increase-approximately
12 per thousand—will be maintained for some time to come.
According to the figures worked out on this hypothesis, in
thirty years' time Japan will have a population of one hundred
million inhabitants. It has been said that Japan has an Oriental
birth-rate whereas her death-rate is tending towards the
European level. According to this theory, Japan must expect
an enormous and, indeed, limitless increase of population in
the future. If this view were true, Japan would be like a vast
boiler destined sooner or later to explode.
The study of demographic movements is far from simple.
It calls for special qualifications and complicated calculations.
I must confess at the outset that such a study is not within
-2一

possible to determine exactly the number of inhabitants which
constitutes the maximum population for a given country. The
number varies widely with geographical, political, economic

materials for its industry, can support a far larger population

dependent on agriculture. Now Japan is an industrial country,
although about half her population is still agricultural. She
can support to-day a much greater number of people than

was still living exclusively on its agricultural production. It
follows, therefore, that the statement that the present level and
rate of increase of population in Japan are excessive, can be

Remedies for Overpopulation.
For these reasons, which I have had to state far too
brieffy, I am unable to contemplate without hesitation the idea
of a contracting Japanese population, however desirable it may
be to bring it about for the progress of civilisation, a diminu-
tion of the supply of labour, a rise in the wage-level and the
greater welfare of the masses. I frankly admit that I have
not yet found any satisfactory escape from so cruel a dilemma.
Let us set aside for a moment the problem of restricting
the increase of population, and turn to the question of how
overpopulation and its effects are to be dealt with to-day.
I have already said that absolite overpopulation, that is
to say, the extreme limit beyond which a country could no
longer support its population, exists only in wholly exceptional
cases. The highest number of inhabitants which a country
can sustain being a function of a great variety of conditions.
the term overpopulation which is currently applied to Japan is
not, in my opinion, appropriate and may give rise to erroneous

ries jadis florissantes à l'intérieur du pays risqueraient de se
transportnr chez ses voisins. Plusieurs filatures japonaises de
coton ont été, les unes créées, les autres transférées sur le

d'autres fabriques suivraient cet exemple le jour où les con-

Emigration, agrarian reform, the development of industry
and foreign trade, and birth control are commonly suggested
as remedies for overpopulation.
There is no need for me to examine here in detail the
first two remedies. A mass movement of population from one

how to provide for the needs of at least 200,000 young men

II.
INDUSTRIALISATION AND FOREIGN TRADE
Faced with the urgent problem of an increasing popula-
tion, successive governments and public opinion in Japan have
become more and more convinced in the last forty years that
the country could no longer depend solely upon agriculture.
ani that an intensification of the industrialisation of the
country and a development of home and foreign trade would
henceforth be indisp nsable to the maintenance of national
life. This new policy was to cause Japan to seek in industry
and commerce the resources required to provide the people
with the means of living. We have thus striven to manu-
facture everything required for internal consumption with the
objcct of reducing the quantum of imports, for a chronically
unfavourable trade balane, was tending constantly to increase
our foreign indebtedness. A few figures will show the develop
ment of industry in Japan. In 1859, the total number of
factories employing at least ten workers did not exceed 2.259;
in 1933, the figure had risen to 31,293. In the same period
the number of workers of bo h sexes had increased from
220,128 to 1,747.418.
Industrial Cost-Prices.
Once we had made suflicient progress in the manufacture
of various commodities, we began to search for outlets in
foreign countries. During the great war, we passed through a
brief period of intense commercial expansion. That, however.
was but a transient experience which not only came to an end

understanding of the factors of the problem.
Since the manner of living of a people is not capable of

will remain what they are to-day for many years to come and.

In a study entitled Memorandum on Japanese Com-
petition "', another eminent economist, Dr. T. E. Gregory,
• Professor in the University of London, has recently shed new
light on the conditions in which Japanese competition is taking
place. He points out that the inequality of standards of life
has long been in existence and has not prevented Western
merchants from placing on the markets of the East the pro-
ducts of their manufacture. This is true, and the explanation
lies in the fact that the high wages of the West have b.en
balanced by a high productive power. In fact, the Occident
still possesses to-day technical superiority and an intellectual
training difficult to surpass: with such formidable assets,
pessimism and anxiety on its part would not be casily under-
stood.
In the case of Japan, Professor Gregory has noted that
efficiency and productive power have developed much more
quickly than wages and the standard of living, though the
latter have, of course, also moved upwards. He points out
that the differences are much greater in cost prices than in
wages. The standard of life in Japan is not really low in any
absolute sense; it is not the case that it has not risen or that
it is not capable of rising in the future. These, however, are
not the decisive considerations. The decisive consideration is
the co-existence of a relatively high (and rising) standard of

The Marketing Problem.
A reorganisation of the distribution of raw materials.
although of capital importance, does not constitute the only
problem to be solved if one is trying to dissipate the sense of
uneasiness in a country like my own. There is another pro-
blem which, under the modern system of production, calls for
a simultaneous solution. That is the problem of the re-
organisation of world markets.

are allowed to succeed, what Japan desires will come about

III.
CONCLUSION.
I come now to my conclusion.
I have attempted to explain the conditions in which my
country has seen her population increase and her industry and
trade develop. To provide our growing population with the
means to exist, we have had to create work which industry
alone has been able to give us. Industrialised Japan has
extended her manufactures. She imports great quuntities of
raw materials and transforms them into commodities which
she exports. Our manufacturers feel that they are able to

compete with advantage in the foreign markets. The rapid

has aroused criticism and caused anxiety. Now there must
be justice for all men. Japanese manufacturers, being obliged
to secure an access to a world market where European and
American industrialists had long had their place, have made
great efforts to produce at low prices. The Japanese people.
brought late into contact with the civilisation of the West.
have had to assimilate its methods rapidly in order to make
up for lost time.
I met last year an aged and distinguished French industri-
alist, who, I grieve to say, has since died, and who spoke to
me in substance as follows:
"Japanese competition, which certainly came somewhat
suddenly, but which represents the magnificent result of the
persistent efforts of a whole nation, has revealed to us Oc-
cidentals how much the old world has become accustomed to
comfort and easy profits Japan cannot be repronched for
producing cheaply. What we need is to be shaken and
aroused to make the same effort."
This was the sincere admission of a man of experience.
I would not wish it to be unpalatable to a French audience,
yet it must give food for serious reflection to any enlightened
and impartial mind.
Embarrassed by this new competition, the old world is
raising economic fortifications: it is constructing an enclosure
of customs barriers, and it is building up walls of quotas
without pausing to consider the difficulty of reconciling this
system with the most favoured-nation clause of international
treaties. In spite of the energetic criticism which can be
levelled against this ingenious system. we look upon it as a

definitely avoided if the people of the white race come to
realise that they must treat the other races as equals. If they
do not do so, concludes Colonel House, they are preparing a
most terrible conflict.
My own view is that a danger of this kind can be
avoided. I believe in the possibility of fruitful collaboration
between the West and the East. Yet such collaboration must
be prepared on a basis of mutual understanding.

qui s'étend jusqu'à la jungle. . . . On ne sait quel rôle le
Japon jouera dans ce grand mouvement de réajustement des
races; si notre politique est bonne, il se peut qu'il trouve
que son intérêt est solidaire de celui des races blanches. ...
Mais le conflit qui menace ne peut être véritablement écarté
que si les peuples de race blanche se rendent compte qu'ils
doivent traiter les autres peuples comme des égaux. S'ils ne
le font pas — conclut le colonel Edward House — ils préparent
le plus terrible conflit.
Pour ma part, je crois qu'un danger comme celui-là peut
être évité; je crois à une collaboration féconde de l'Occident
et de l'Orient. Mais encore faut-il que cette collaboration soit
préparée par un effort de compréhension mutuelle.
Ce qu'a très bien compris le professeur Gregory, ce

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
standing and co operation in the economic field. A policy of
this kind can assume many forms; it is incompatible neither
with a return to freedom of exchange, nor with a maintenance
of certain measures of restriction; it can be expressed through
a distribution of markets among rival countries or by the
allocation to certain countries of certain categories of supplies
or again by the assignment of quotas in each market.
It is not possible for me, of course, to make a choice
between the various measures surveyed by this English
economist. The point which I should like to emphasise is the
idea of the necessity for co-operation. It is always better to
negotiate than to take refuge in distrust and abstention. What
I can affirm here is that my country will always be found
ready to take part in discussions.
I repeat that the worst policy would be to oppose the
West against the East. No effort must be spared to establish
the vital requirements on both sides. I have had no other
object this evening than to outline to you with all frankness
some of those necessities. I am convinced that when the
nations have better understood each other's needs and aspira-
tions, economic peace-that peace to which everyone aspires -
will have been assured.

SKH
The ist of this despatch from Tokyo
is that little or no progress appears to
have been made toward the establishment
of a proposed new political party which
would support the administration.
The despatch points out that the two
present major parties represent no ideals and
have no real political objectives. They
are entirely opportunist organizations
seeking to obtain political power, and are
groups of men banded around outstanding
chieftains. In view of the insistent demand
in certain quarters that parliamentary
government be given some meaning, there is
some expectation that the present parties
will break up and that the component
elements will be reunited into narties
which may be characterized as "liberals"
and "conservatives".

Department of State
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
728
No. 2364, dated April 16, 1937, on the subject of the
movement to establish a new party, of a reactionary tinge
and designed to support the present Cabinet or similar
N
FLLED
future Cabinets, and to report that little or no progress
appears to have been made during the past month toward
the establishment of the proposed new party.
in the meantime, however, rumors have appeared that,
out of the present welter of politics in Japan, two new
parties will emerge. It is well known that the two major
political
G

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
political parties in Japan, the Seiyukai and the Min-
seito, have no decided political color. They have at
21.F
times posed as the representatives of certein classes
of the population, such as the agriculturists and seri-
culturists, or of the merchants and bankers, and at
other times they have been known to be supported by and
to support one or the other of the great financial groups,
but they have not in recent years adopted any definite
political color, such as conservative or liberal. The
members of the parties themselves comprise all shades of
political complexion, ranging (according to a Seiyukai
member of the Lower House) from extreme liberalism to die-
hard reactionism. In the past there has been little
tendency for these various elements in the parties to
separate, as the parties simply consisted of groups of
men banded together for the purpose of obtaining poli-
tical power, with the perquisites that such power gives.
During the past year, however, the increasingly-
important conflict between the forces of reactionism or
fascism and those of liberal parliamentarism has developed
fissiparous tendencies within the parties. While the
parties as units are opposed to the Hayashi Cabinet and
are striving for a return to strict parliamentary govern-
ment, many members of the parties are individually in
sympathy with the Hayashi regime and its policies. The
politicians are now beginning to perceive that parlia-
mentary government is meaningless unless the parties
represent some shade of political complexion and can give
the

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
the people an opportunity to express their desires in
regard to the character of government through their votes.
The futility of elections when the parties have no de-
finite complexion was well illustrated by the election
of April 30, 1937. The Government had dissolved the
Lower House as a disciplinary measure and a general
election was necessary. But there were no issues in-
volved upon which the people could vote. Consequently,
after an apathetic election, the people returned to the
Diet much the same class of men as those who had been
members when the House was dissolved. The Government
had put up no candidates, and the people had had no
opportunity to express their desires. The parties claim
that the election resulted in a victory for parliamentary
practice and a defeat for the Cabinet, but it is difficult
to see how the election could have produced any result
other than the election of the party candidates.
These factors have tended to divide the parties
within themselves into factions which, for the want of a
better term, might for the present be called "liberals"
and "conservatives". It is impossible to state at the
moment how far this tendency has gone and what the eventual
out-come will be, but many political observers in Tokyo
believe that the present parties will break up and a
liberal and a conservative party will emerge from the
resulting chaos. Japanese domestic politics will certainly
be vastly improved should this move eventuate.
Respectfully yours,
800.

In despatch No. 2402 May 1, 1937, the Embassy
made a preliminary report, on incomplete returns, of
the Japanese general election of April 30, 1937. Numeri-
cal results and certain comments were embodied in telegram
726
No. 122, May 3, 3 p.m. These communications the Embassy
now supplements by the present despatch.

F/G

=== Page 299 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
The eleotion returns are not surprising. A tabulation
1/2
enclosed lists them in detail. The notable result was the
large gain (though still ineffective in the total) of the
Shakai Taishuto (Social Mass Party). The percentage of
control of the newly elected House by the various groups is
as follows, in comparison with the old House which ended on
March 31, 1937:
Percentage

The Kokumin Domei and the Showakai are the only parties
now supporting the Government. In the new House they con-
trol, between them, thirty seats(6 1/2% of the total).
The Japanese press generally has chosen to echo the
clamor of the parties to the effect that the eleotion is a
defeat for the Hayashi Cabinet. The Premier, in two separ-
ate statements to the press, has refused to accept that in-
terpretation. He says that the returns were not a surprise
to the Government, that the Government had no intention of
going out and winning the election. In this he is supported
by the figures: had every single candidate put up by the
government-supporting parties been eleoted, only fifty-six
seats

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.97)
2
2
-3-
seats would be under control. It is clear that the Premier
thought it desirable to call a general eleotion without hav-
ing as objeot to win control of the House of Representatives
through Government parties.
The fact appears to be that the issue which underlies
present Japanese politics is the issue whether or not the
country shall return to government by party - that is, the
practice of choosing cabinets from the party dominant in
the Lower House of the Diet, a practice abandoned at the time
of Inukai's assassination on May 15, 1932. The Hayashi Cabi-
net is for continuation of non-party government; there is
really no other political principle to which the present
administration is definitely tied. The parties now loudly
insist upon return to party government, but their tone is
apt to be more moderate by the time the new Diet is convened
in the course of the summer. Reflection on the experience
of January 1937 should make the parties less impulsive.
Hirota fell before the Diet blast, but Ugaki was blocked, and
the cabinet that came in was in not the slightest degree a
concession to the party principle. If Hayashi be likewise
foroed out there is no indication whatever that the present
temper of the military would concede a bit more; it would be
a change from one non-party cabinet to another with nothing
gained.
Dissolution was an act of discipline. The new election
has affected the political atmosphere very little. As yet
there

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
there is no threat to the life of the Hayashi Cabinet.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

FROM

UN4-1937
14ε, June 4, 1 p.m.
NOTE
JUN 7 1937
894.002
My telegram No. 145 June 3
One. As of early this morning Konoe required only
the formal acceptance by Kaya of the Finance portfolio
894.00/744
to complete formation of the new Cabinet. It is expected
that after Kcya has ascertained the character of the
economic policies to be followed by the new Cabinet he
will give his acceptance, and that formal investiture
of Cabinet will take place today.
Tm.. Tho selection of Konoe as Premier has been
JUN 10
FILJD
popular, the comment most generally heard being that the
selection of the person favored over all others by public
opinion is a significant indication of trend toward more
normal condition. However, in one of his first statements
after receiving command to form a Cabinet, Konoe announced
that, although he would endeavor to include in his
cabinet two members of the political parties (which he has
done), they would be selected on the basis of their
personal merits and not as representatives of the parties.
F/F
This statement has perceptibly cocled the ardor with which
the parties acclaimed the news of Konoe's appointment.
G
There

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
67-2
-2-
JR #148, June 4, 1 p.m., from Tokyo.
There seems to be little doubt but that the parties now
possess a destructive power, but their inability to put
forward an intelligent and constructive program leads most
observers to believe that Konoe's present great popularity
warrants his avoiding too close a sort of association
with the parties.
Three. The disclosure of composition of the new
Cabinet has had an adverse effect upon the enthusiastic
response of the press to selection of Konoe.
Four. Comment by press and by individuals is that the
continuance in office of present Ministers of War and
Navy indicates that increased armament and doctrine of
"increasing power of industrial production" favored by the
armed services are to be among the Cabinet's policies.
In business circles the appointment to the Home Ministry
of Baba, whose views on finance are considered to be unsound
as well as unorthodox, is regarded with apprehension.
Although it is reported that Kaya will if appointed follow
the policies of his predecessor, it seems doubtful whether
he has sufficient prestige and force to counteract the
probable endeavors of Cabinet officers previously mentioned
to give effect to policies leading toward more rigid state
control over industry and toward uncontrollable financial
inflation.
Five. The collapse of the Hayashi Cabinet of itself
has

3a
JR #148, June 4, 1 p.m., from Tokyo.
has eliminated certain elements of uncertainty and the
formation of a new Cabinet will undoubtedly tend to
stabilize political and economic conditions. I shall
withhold further effort at interpreting significance of
the present change of government until the policies of the
Konoe Cabinet have been disclosed and discussed in the
press.
Six. I hope to have an opportunity to call next week
on Hirota.
Soven. lease make available copies to Commerce and
Treasury.

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 8, 1937.
Mr. Bullitt became very well
acquainted with Mr. Sato (who was
for a long time Japanese Ambassador
to France and is now Japanese Minis-
ter for Foreign Affairs) in Paris.
Mr. Bullitt tells me that he formed
a very high regard for Sato, that
Sato was extraordinarily well
informed, very broad in his perspec-
tive, moderate in his views,
critical of the military element
in Japan, and very desirous that
Japan follow a policy of moderation
and friendliness toward other
countries.

MSM:
In this despatch from Tokyo the
Embassy discusses various public utterances
of Mr. Sato, the then Foreign Minister and
endeavors to analyze his views toward
foreign affairs. The despatch is of some
interest desnite the change of government
in Japan. You may wish to glance through
it noting especially the marked portions,
and the first enclosure.
In the oninion of the Embassy,
Lir. Sato continued to be confident of the
efficacy of discussion and reason in the
solution of Janan's international problems.
However, there is noticeable in this
attitude toward China a certain stiffening,
which is probably accounted for by
pressure from the army.
Note that in Sato's opinion "Relations
with the United  tates are satisfactory
on the whole", and that "Problems arising
from Japanese expanding trade have
gradually been reduced by mutual under-
standing".

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

Tokyo, May 28, 1937.
SUBJECT: RECENT SPEECHES BY FOREIGN MINISTER SATO.
Ff (381)
894.00

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

O.N.I. AND M.J.D.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
7
Washington.
8940/734
As reported in the Embassy's telegram No. 131, May
1/
Affairs, on May 17, 1937, delivered a speech on foreign re-
lations to the prefectural governors of the country
2/
assembled in Tokyo. On May 24 he addressed a banquet of
the Tokyo Jiyu Tsusho Kyokai (Tokyo Free Trade Association).
JUN 21937
FILED
On May 26 in conversation with me he made certain remarks
which were reported in the Embassy's telegram No. 137, May
3/
26, 1 p.m., and set forth in a memorandum herewith. The
purpose of the present despatch is to review these various
statements in order to determine how far the estimates
embodied
F/G/

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
794.00/116
embodied in the Embassy's despatches No. 2312, March 17,
854.00/735
1937, No. 2390, Apri1 30, 1937, and No. 2410, May 11, 1937,
794.00/122
need to be modified.
Analysis leads the Embassy to these conclusions:
first, that Mr. Sato continues confident of the efficacy of
discussion and of reason for the solution of Japan's inter-
national problems, and in such discussion is ready to admit
that Japan has faults to correct; second, that he continues
to view these problems as chiefly economic; and third, that
a certain stiffening is noticeable in the Foreign Minister's
attitude toward China.
894.00/734
Mr. Sato's May 17 speech (Embassy's telegram No. 131,
May 17, 8 p.m.) was of such importance that the Foreign
Office released a complete text in English, given in enclo-
sure No. 1 herewith. The speech was carefully prepared and
comprehensive in scope, and (aside from statements of merely
local applicability) had substantially the following con-
tent:
The national policy of Japan is (1) inter-
national justice, (2) stability of eastern Asia,
and (3) common prosperity of all nations. Im-
portant to Japan's international position is the
strengthening of the inseparable relation with
Manchuria and improvement of relations with China
and the Soviet Union.
Relations with China have been impeded by
incidents of the past year. United opposition
against Japan is the current slogan in China;
anti-Japanese sentiment is a pawn of domestic
politics there, and even the Nanking Government
exploits it by urging internal harmony for the
particular purpose of resisting foreign aggres-
sion. And it is a fact that Chinese unification
is making progress. Japan's designs in China
are not aggressive, and that must be made clear.
The Japanese Government seeks greater harmony
through frequent personal contact and through cultural
and

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
and economic cooperation, looking to an inter-
dependent economic relationship in the common
interest.
In relations with the Soviet Union the
Japanese Government is trying to eliminate
unnecessary friction. Cessation of mistreat-
ment of Japanese by Soviet officials has been
requested, and Japanese authorities reciprocally
should also be careful. Soviet Far Eastern arma-
ments are being hastened.
Friendship with Great Britain is highly
desirable. A number of problems with regard
to China and with regard to trade require solution.
The Keelung incident settlement and the perpetual
lease agreement have eased the atmosphere, and
the visit of Prince and Princess Chichibu to London
is adding to Anglo-Japanese friendship.
Relations with the United States are satis-
factory on the whole. Problems arising from
Japanese expanding trade have gradually been re-
solved by mutual understanding. The visit of
American cotton industry representatives resulted
in the conclusion of an agreement satisfactory
to both sides. The Japanese economic mission
now in America should enhance such friendly at-
mosphere. Japanese-American economic relations
are complementary, and on that foundation I hope
collaboration for the preservation of peace in
the Pacific will be continued.
With Germany Japan concluded in November 1936
an agreement against the Third Communist Inter-
national, because the Comintern declared Japan
and Germany the objectives of its sovietizing
campaign. This agreement is of the greatest value
and significance.
Freedom of trade and access to raw materials,
always advocated by the Japanese Government, are
fundamental to Japan's claim to the right of
existence. Untrammeled trade and economic inter-
course is the prime requisite of peace.
Japanese foreign trade in 1936 set a new
record of five and a half billion yen, but is
still less than four percent of the world total.
Meantime other countries have been vigilant in
imposing restrictions. But the plea for removal
of barriers is gaining ground. The March meeting
of the League of Nations commission on raw materials
disclosed that several countries support Japan's
contentions.
In the Foreign Minister's speech on May 24 the salient
points

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
points are (1) a reiteration of former Foreign Minister
Hirota's statement that there would be no war during his
tenure of office, (2) further emphasis of the importance to
Japan of having freer access to foreign raw materials and
foreign markets, and (3) admission that standards of living
of some classes of Japanese labor are unsatisfactory and
should be studied by the authorities. This speech, not
attempting to be so comprehensive as that of May 17, gives
more play to what are evidently Mr. Sato's inner convictions,
and simply adds weight to the conclusions stated in the Em-
854.0073.5
bassy's despatch No. 2390, April 30, 1937, that Mr. Sato,
far from being an alarmist, comes to his duties determined
to ohtain by opendiscussion international acceptance of fur-
ther increase in Japan's foreign trade, as well as of the
corollary requirements of such increase. The Foreign Minister
desires that Japan approach its international problems in a
studious, intelligent manner, and as evidence of good faith
in favoring this approach he is willing to admit that not all
the right is on Japan's side.
But with regard to China it appears that Mr. Sato has
already come up against difficulties. There are indications
that in this field of foreign relations he has alrdady ac-
knowledged that something more is called for on Japan's part
than an insistence on intelligent discussion. Speaking on
May 17 he was content to temper his general tone of friendliness
toward China merely by making clear parenthetically that Japan
has noted Nanking's deliberate exploitation of the anti-Japanese
movement. By May 24 Mr. Sato found it wise (possibly prompted
by others) to include in his speech the statement that although
war

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
War is never profitable Japan must be willing to fight
when honor and prestige are at stake. Then, by the time
of my conversation with him on May 26 (telegram No. 137,
3/
May 26, 1 p.m., and enclosure No. 3 herewith), he was ready
to admit the possibility that a grave view would have to be
taken of the relatively unimportant incident at Swatow (ar-
rest of a Japanese police officer and detention for a few hours).
This growing firmness with regard to China on the part of
a foreign minister who is bending every effort toward open-
minded foreign relations is probably a significant indication
of the direction in which any Japanese foreign minister, no
matter how liberal, must give way to a bent of Japanese
foreign policy which has been fixed by the events of the last
years beyond the possibility of voluntary alteration. So
fundamentally has the Japanese army grounded the standing of
Japan in China on the display of force that it is now difficult
to visualize how that attitude can be appreciably changed.
Respectfully yours,
Sosth6
oseph C. Grew

Minister Sato, the Ambassador and Counselor Dooman,

for Foreign Affairs Horinouchi, May 26, 1937.

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclgsure No. 2, to despatch
dated May28, 1937.
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
The Japan Advertiser.
Tokyo, wednesday, May 26, 1937.
PREVENTION OF WAR
IS SATO'S FIRST AIM
Foreign Minister Outlines Atti-
tude Toward Problems in
Tokyo Address
Stressing that the starting point of
Japanese diplomatic policy should be
to prevent war, Foreign Minister Nao-
take Sato gave an outline of his atti-
tude toward problems affecting Japan
externally and internally in an address
before the Tokyo Free Trade Associa-
tion at the Bankers' Club Monday, the
Asahi reports.
"War is never profitable," he said.
"However, once the honor and prestige
of our country are injured, we must be
willing to fight. It may be the opinion
of some that Sato would yield to a
foreign country even in such a moment,
but I wish only to follow the normal
path of diplomacy until the last mo-
ment, if the worst is to come.
"When Mr. Koki Hirota was Foreigr
Minister. he said there would be no
war while he was in office. My view
is the same as his, and such a determi-
nation is necessary for those who have
charge of diplomacy.
"Our population is growing year by
year. The best solution of that pro-
blem seems to lie in the development
of domestic fortunes. The only alter-
native is to secure and exploit raw
material resources in foreign lands and
to demand the open door policy for
Japanese goods. By universal recogni-
tion of this demand. peace could be
established and war exterminated. This
point will be maintained by Commer-
cial Attache Yasundo Shudo, who will
attend the second meeting of the raw
materials and resources committee at
Geneva next month.
"Japan's foreign trade is being ob-
structed by numerous barriers. To re-
move these barriers, foreign trade
should be carried on in the spirit of.
fair play. In this, the first question
that arises is labor conditions. It
would never be profitable for a nation
to compete with foreign goods by sacri-
ficing its laborers.
"Criticism always has been directed
against labor conditions in our country,
and we have always explained the dif-
ference in the living standard and the
Japanese family system. Also we have
always maintained that Japanese labor-
ers are not inferior to those of other
countries. However, there are cases
in which I am forced to admit that
Japanese laborers' living standards can-
not be compared with those of labor-
ers in other countries. I believe this
point should be given more considera-
tion by those in charge of administra-
tion."

=== Page 327 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 3 to despatch
No. 2443 dated May 28, 1937,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
May 26, 1937.
Mr. Naotake Sato, Minister for Foreign
Affairs
The Ambassador and Mr. Dooman.
Subject: Sino-Japanese Relations.
I called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs this
morning to present Mr.Dooman.
After a brief conversation, during which the Minister
for Foreign Affairs referred in terms of gratification to
the improved state of relations between the United States
and Japan, he supposed that the disturbed relations between
Japan and China were probably causing uneasiness in the
United States and thus impeding the placing of the relations
between the United States and Japan on a stable and satis-
factory basis. The Foreign Minister then continued sub-
stantially as follows:
The greatest difficulty in the way of re-
solving the problems pending between Japan and
China lies in the inability of the various ele-
ments in Japan to agree upon a common point of
view, "various quarters within the Army", es-
pecially those at Tientsin and HsinKing holding
certain opinions to which he could not subscribe.
He was nevertheless hopeful that it would be
possible to proceed with plans to open negotia-
tions with the Chinese Government in the not
too distant future, perhaps some time during
the

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the first half of June. He was, however,
somewhat apprehensive with regard to reaction
in Japan to reports of two unfortunate incidents
having recently arisen, one being that of the
firing by vessels of the Chinese Customs Patrol
on Japanese fishing vessels, and the other an
attack upon a Japanese consular policeman at
Swatow by the Chinese police. Nevertheless,
Mr. Kawagoe is to leave on about June 10 for
Nanking, and he is to be instructed to open
negotiations as soon as practicable with the
Chinese Government.
IMr. Kawagoe had asked to be relieved of
his post as Ambassador to China. There is no
other person at the present time qualified and
in a position to represent the Japanese Govern-
ment in China, and therefore he (Mr. Sato) had
persuaded Mr. Kawagoe to return to China. It
is quite true that the previous negotiations
carried on by Mr. Kawagoe with the Chinese
Government had been unsuccessful, but those ne-
gotiations had been instructive to Mr. Kawagoe;
and it was felt that Mr. Kawagoe's previous ex-
perience in negotiations with the Chinese Govern-
ment would be helpful to him in endeavoring to
lead the forthcoming negotiations to a mutually
satisfactory conclusion.
The conversation then concluded with an exchange of
amenities.
J.C.G.

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to despatch
No. 2443 dated May 28, 1937,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
May 26, 1937.
Mr. Horinouchi, Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs
The Ambassador and Mr. Dooman.
Subject: Sino-Japanese Relations - Swatow Incident.
After my call this morning on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs (see separate memorandum of conversation), I pre-
sented Mr. Dooman to the Vice Minister, Mr. Horinouchi.
1.11
Mr. Horinouchi appeared to be somewhat agitated over
the occurrence of the two incidents affecting relations be-
tween Japan and China mentioned by the Foreign Minister.
Mr. Horinouchi said that no detailed report had been received
with regard to the alleged firing by Chinese Customs Patrol
vessels on Japanese fishing vessels, but that the facts of
the incident at Swatow, where a Japanese consular policeman
was said to have been injured by the Chinese police, were as
set forth in despatches appearing in the papers this morning.
It was his impression that a grave view would be taken in
Japan of the Swatow affair. He remarked that he was at a loss
to understand why the Chinese Provincial authorities had taken
such a rigorous position. He explained that a regulation had
been issued by the Provincial authorities requiring that all
foreign residents register with the Chinese police. The
Japanese policeman concerned had been staying at a Japanese
inn at Swatow and had informally made known to the Chinese
police his place of residence. He then moved to the house
of a Japanese merchant and had informed the Chinese police,
presumably by telephone, of his change of residence. The
Chinese

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Chinese police insisted that he fulfill the requirements
laid down in the Provincial regulations above-mentioned,
but the Japanese policeman had refused, for the reason that
he was, first a foreign national enjoying extraterritorial
rights, and second, he was a Japanese Government official.
The Chinese police then visited the house in which he resided
and demanded that the Japanese policeman proceed with them to
the police station. The Japanese policeman refused, an al-
tercation followed and he was forcibly seized by the Chinese
police, taken to the police station and handcuffed. The
Japanese Consul succeeded in obtaining the man's release after
several hours' detention.
Mr. Horinouchi then went on to say that the Japanese
Government is expecting to enter into negotiations with the
Chinese Government looking toward the settlement of several
issues of long standing, but that there was some possibility
of the opening of negotiations being delayed by the occurrence
of the Swatow affair.
I expressed regret that there should have occurred at
this time any incident which might prejudice the development
of friendly relations between Japan and China.
After a brief conversation of a personal nature, Mr.
Dooman and I withdrew.
J.C.G.

June 7, 1937.
JUN 14 1937
Composition of the new Japanese Cabinet.
note
Mr. Grew telegraphed on June 4 that Prince Konoye,
894.002
the new Prime Minister, required only the formal acceptance
by Mr. Kaya of the Finance portfolio to complete the
formation of the new Cabinet. According to the press
Kaya's acceptance was subsequently received and formal
89
investiture took place on the same day.
94.00/747
A memorandum on the selection of Prince Konoye as
Prime Minister was prepared by FE under date June 2.
Mr. Hirota, the new Minister for Foreign Affairs, was
formerly Prime Minister and also Minister for Foreign
Affairs. Ambassador Grew reports that in his initial
interview on June 7 with Mr. Hirota, Hirota asked the
Ambassador to tell the President and the Secretary that
his guiding policy in conducting Japan's relations with
the United States would be based upon the exchange of
messages with the Secretary in 1934. With regard to
Sino-Japanese affairs, Hirota stated that his three points
were
1. Recognition de facto, if not de jure, by China
F/FC
of "Manchukuo";
2. Cooperation between Japan and China in economio
matters;
G
3. Japan to cooperate with China in military
measures to be directed toward eradication of communist
elements.

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
were too abstract for present circumstances and that he,
therefore, proposed to find concrete solutions of the
various outstanding problems between Japan and China.
Three of the new ministers, Lieutenant General
Sugiyama, Minister of War; Admiral Yonai, Minister of the
Navy; and Mr. Shiono, Minister of Justice, are hold-overs
from the previous cabinet. Mr. Ryutaro Nagai, Minister
of Communications, and Mr. Chikuhei Nakajima, Minister of
Railways, are party leaders of the Minseito and the
Seiyukai, respectively.
The Minister of Home Affairs, Mr. Eiichi Baba, who
was Minister of Finance in the Hirota Cabinet, is a pro-
ponent of the expansion of expenditures and of the exten-
sion of state control of industries. Ambassador Grew
expresses doubt whether Mr. Kaya, the new Minister of
Finance, has sufficient prestige and force to counteract
the probable endeavors of Baba and other Cabinet officers
to give effect to policies leading toward more rigid state
control of industries and toward uncontrollable financial
inflation.
Mr. Yoshino, the Minister of Commerce and Industry, is
devoted to the development of Japan's industries. He is
practical and relatively friendly to foreign interests.
The three remaining ministers are little known outside
Japan. Mr. Yasui, the Minister of Education, is a career
bureaucrat who was previously governor of Osaka Prefecture;
Count

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Count Arima, the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry,
is an expert on agricultural policy; and the Minister of
Overseas Affairs, Mr. Sonyu Otani, has a strong Buddhist
background, being a son of Count Otani, Abbot of Honganji,
who has been conspicuous in Japanese colonization activities.
Mr. Otani is a member of the House of Peers.
Ambassador Grew reports that the disclosure of the
composition of the new Cabinet has had an adverse effect
upon the enthusiastic response of the press to the selection
of Prince Konoye as Prime Minister. Comment by the press
and by individuals is that the continuation in office of
the present Ministers of War and of Navy indicates that
increased armament and industrial mobilization are to be
among the Cabinet's policies. The Ambassador believes,
however, that the collapse of the Hayashi Cabinet of
itself has eliminated certain elements of uncertainty and
that the formation of a new cabinet will undoubtedly tend
to stabilize political and economic conditions.

SUBJECT: FALL OF THE HAYASHI CABINET.
894.08
note
54
Dietr tution-Cherk
S
137 UN 2 2  1
M/D
894.002
ON1
PIES SENT TO
U.N.I. AND M.J.D.
894.00/748
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
OF STAT
JUN 2 8 1937
B
Department of State
The Honorable
20
1037
VA
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir!
738
On May 31, 1937, as reported in my telegram No/ 141,
May 31, 7 p.m., the entire Japanese Cabinet, under the
premiership of General Senjuro Hayashi, submitted its
FILED
resignation. The Emperor accepted the resignation, sub-
ject to continuation of duties until the installing of
a new cabinet.
1/
The Hayashi Cabinet's period in office has been one of
the shortest in Japanese parliamentary history. Genoval
Hayashi received the Imperial command on January 29, 1937,
and the ceremony of installing was performed on February 2.
On February 15 the Diet resumed its sessions and proved
fairly
G

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
fairly cooperative. March 3 Mr. Naotake Sato took over
the duties of foreign minister, performed until that time
by Premier Hayashi himself. The important action required
of the Diet was the passage of the 1937-1938 budget, which
was carried through on March 30. Dissolution of the House
of Representatives came the very next day. In spite of the
comparatively cooperative mood which the House had shown,
the Premier explained the dissolution as an act of discipline;
and the inconsistency in this explanation did much to alienate
public sympathy from the Hayashi Cabinet. The consequent
general election was held on April 30, following a campaign
in which the Government studiously avoided any statement of
platform or of support of candidates which could make the
voting a measure of the electorate's acquiescence in or opposi-
tion to the Government. This formless election brought in
an indecisive result, with a new House of Representatives not
conspicuously different from the previous one (except that
the small Social Mass Party succeeded in doubling its seats
to eight percent of the total). The Premier publicly de-
clared that the result of the election was about as expected
and that his Cabinet would continue in office. The two major
parties, about equal in number of seats and neither controlling
an absolute majority, then commenced a campaign to bring about
the defeat of the Hayashi Cabinet during the Diet session which
must convene not later than August. The newspapers kept the
matter continuously before the public.
For several weeks there was no indication of the opinion
of Japan's inner circle of statesmen with regard to Hayashi's
contention

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
contention that no change of policy or of cabinet was required
by the result of the election. But on Saturday, May 29, out
2/
of the blue, Prince Konoe, president of the House of Peers,
told newspapermen that the refusal of Hayashi to admit mem-
bers of the victorious parties as cabinet ministers, after
the election, had led to a conflict impossible of compromise.
He also planted a discreet suggestion about admission of
party men in the next cabinet, well calculated to invite at-
tention to himself as a logical prime minister in the present
contingency.
Action followed, and quickly. At one o'clock on Monday,
May 31, Premier Hayashi went to the Palace after a regular
cabinet meeting, as is usual. From the Palace he proceeded
to call on the president of the Privy Council, after which
a special cabinet meeting was immediately called and the
resignation decision reached. I am informed by a wholly re-
liable Japanese official that the possibility of resignation
had not been suggested at the cabinet meeting in the morning,
and that General Hayashi had disclosed his intention to re-
sign only as a result of his conversation at the Palace with
a high personage, presumably the Emperor.
General Hayashi as Premier has been handicapped by some
vague thinking on the subject of the political parties, vague
thinking which was responsible for his primary error, the
March 31 dissolution of the House of Representatives. Perhaps
first attracted by the hope of thereby playing into the hands
of the movement for a new, dominating, military-guided party,
he apparently came to the decision in the conviction that
dissolution

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
dissolution (and the implied threat of further dissolutions
whenever necessary) would have a disciplinary effect and
make the Diet less obstructive of the desires of the Govern-
ment in the future. Discipline of the parties came to be
Hayashi's political formula, even publicly expressed.
It is quite true that the influence of political parties
in Japan is emasculated - or at least greatly restricted -
by the constitutional structure and tradition of the country,
but it is apparently not true that the parties can be over-
ridden with the frank, openly stated casualness of General
Hayashi. Some formal concessions,however nominal, are
called for. During the last days it would appear that the
Emperor's advisers have been able to convince the army group
that a more considerate handling of the parties is desirable.
The inner circle, however cognizant of the power of the military,
is not ready to accept endless disciplining of the Diet, en-
forced by dissolutions and the threat of dissolutions. This
thesis the army group seems now to accept with the qualifi-
cation that a change of cabinet entail the smallest possible
change of policy.
The primary purpose of the resignation of the Hayashi
Cabinet, in the Embassy's opinion, is to throw to the parties
a sop which will work to forestall more fundamental opposition.
A change of cabinet is conceded for the purpose of heading
off an attack on policy.
The exit of Premier Hayashi marks a step forward, modest
though it be, in non-military weight in the governing of
Japan.

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Japan. In point of major policy the fall of the Hayashi
Cabinet does not constitute an important turning point.
Its real lesson, if the military wish to heed it, is that
national objectives can be gained more economically under
the guidance of a premier of greater political acumen than
General Hayashi. It is not a sweeping victory for the poli-
tical parties, in which the public still has no great faith;
but it has demonstrated that the political parties have re-
gained a certain measure of destructive strength which must
be included among the factors determining future developments.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 29, 1937.
Tokyo's No. 2466 of June 10, 1937,
relates to the significance of the apooint-
ment of Foreign Minister Hirota as head
of the Planning Board.
The ordinance, a copy of which is en-
closed with the despatch, is so vague in
its terminology and generalities that a
clear conception of the functione of this
Board may not be obtained from an examina-
tion of the ordinance. It would appear,
however, that the Board is, generally, de-
signed to he a conrdinetor of government
policy. It is to submit to the Cabinet
budget estimates relating to important
policies and, upon request hy the Premier,
is to examine important projects proposed
to the Catinet.
In view of the desire of the militery
for the establishment of the Planning Board
as a national policy-forming hody, the ap-
pointment of Mr. Hirota as head of the
Board takes on particular significance.
The military had favored Dr. Baba as head
of the Board, whereas Japanese financial
interests had favored either Mr. Kaya, the
present Minister of Finance, or Mr. Yoshino,
present Inister of Commerce and Industry,
for the office of the head of the Planning
Board. Consequently, the appointment of
Mr. Hirota

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
..r. Hirote, being & compromise between the
desires of the military and of the financial
interests, would appear to constitute only a
limited concession to the demands of the
military. The Arny has not as yet had its
vay in the selection of the personnel of
the body.
In view of the fact that the functions
of this Board are not cle rly defined, it
is di ficult to predict the influence which
may be exerted by the Planning Boerd in the
administration of the Konoye Cabinet.

894.00/749
The Honorable
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
DEPAR
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
OF STATE
Sir:
On June 7, 1937, Premier Konoe focused public atten-
tion on the Planning Board (Kikakucho) by appointing
oreign Minister Hirota as its head. The Planning Board
山
was established by Imperial ordinance (translation enclosed
1U0L10 1937
herewith) last month, on May 13, while the Hayashi Cabinet
was in power, and there has been considerable interest in
the question whether or not the board would be continued
by the present Konoe Cabinet, which has just come into
office.
The ordinance itself does not much help the reader
toward comprehending what are to be the functions of the
board. Aside from stating what shall be the rank of the
officials
F/FG

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
officials appointed to the board, the ordinance indulges
only in generalities with regard to function. Apparently
the board is designed to be a coordinator of government
policy. When requested by the Premier, it is to examine
important projects proposed to the Cabinet; it is to
submit to the Cabinet budget estimates relating to important
policies; it is to have access to all the governmental de-
partments for any information required. All in all, the
powers of the board as set forth in the ordinance are so
vague as to be useless. How important the Planning Board
is to be awaits the test of the next months.
The Department is already aware that there has been
pressure from the politically minded among the Japanese mili-
tary, extending over the past year or two, for the creation
of organs of government in Japan to parallel the already
established administrative structure in some way which would
permit the military more directly to carry out political re-
forms, particularly projects of state socialism. The Embassy's
734
despatch No. 2395/ April 30, 1937, and previous despatches
discussed this tendency. Now, with a new and popular premier
recently raised to office, whose dominating concern seems
to be to maintain balance and harmony between the various
conflicting forces of Japanese politics, it is apparent that
the desire of the military to make something of a national
policy body is to be respected. But on the other hand the
appointment of Mr. Hirota as head of the board shows that
it is a limited concession. The leaders of the Japanese
Army have favored as head of the board Dr. Baba, who proved
himself

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
himself one of their friends as Finance Minister in the
Hirota Cabinet by bringing in a budget which included all
the demands of the military. Dr. Baba is distrusted by
Japanese business and banking, and Prince Konoe wisely re-
fused to make him Minister of Finance in the present Cabinet.
He was nevertheless admitted to the Cabinet as Home Minister.
Japanese financial interests have been in favor of either Mr.
Kaya, the present Minister of Finance, or Mr. Yoshino, present
Minister of Commerce and Industry, for the office of the head
of the Planning Board. Prince Konoe has chosen to settle
the issue by naming to the office Mr. Hirota, who is a com-
promise choice. Because Mr. Hirota is a former premier, his
appointment could hardly be opposed by those who desire to
make the Planning Board an important organ. On the other
hand Prince Konoe could not have made a wiser choice if he
has in mind quietly side-tracking the Planning Board by the
process of making it imposing in personnel and then overlook-
ing it in practice.
There is almost nothing to show what part will be played
by the Flanning Board in the administration of the Konoe
Cabinet, which is just commencing. So far, the Army has not
had its way in the personnel of the body. It is a vaguely
defined organ, the real functions of which must inevitably
depend upon what the Government from now on actually does
make of the board.
2/
An editorial comment is.enclosed.
Respectfully yours,
ChG.
Joseph C. Grew

Imperial Ordinance
Having consulted the Privy Council
We sanction the organization of the Kikakucho, and hereby
order that this be promulgated.
Imperial Sign Manual.
May 13, 1937.
Prime Minister Senjuro Hayashi

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
Imperial Ordinance No. 192.
Organization of the Kikakucho.
Article 1. The Kikakucho shall be placed under the
control of the Prime Minister and deal with the following
matters:
(1) Formulation of important policies and draft plans
relating to the coordination of such policies, undor ordors
of the Prime Minister, and submission of same with explana-
tion thereof.
(2) Examination of important draft policies presented
to the Cabinet by the various ministers of state, and sub-
mission to the Cabinet of analysis and opinion in regard
thereto.
(3) Investigation concerning coordination of major
policies.
(4) Submission to the Cabinet of budget estimates
relating to major policies, with analysis and opinion.
The Kikakucho call upon the various governmental de-
partments for information and explanation whenever deemed
necessary for the execution of the forogoing paragraphs.
Article 2. The Kikakucho shall comprise the follow-
ing personnel:
President.
One vice president (of chokunin rank).
One private secretary (of sonin rank).
Twenty full-time investigators (of sonin rank, but five
may be of chokunin rank).
One

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
One full-time secretary (of sonin rank).
Fifteen full-time assistant investigators (of sonin rank).
Two full-time managers (of sonin rank).
Forty full-time subordinate officials (or hannin rank).
The president shall be appointed from among the Ministers
of State by Imperial order.
The private secretary shall be appointed from among the
higher officials of the Kikakucho.
Article 3. In addition to the investigators stipulated
in the foregoing article, investigators may also be appointed
by the Cabinet from among high officials of the governmental
departments concerned, by request of the Prime Minister sub-
mitted to the Emperor.
Article 4. The Kikakucho shall have standing counsellors
who shall deal with the affairs of the Kikakucho at all times.
Standing counsellors shall be appointed by the Cabinet
from among high officials of chokunin rank in the governmental
departments, by request submitted by the Prime Minister to
the Throne.
Article 5. The Kikakucho shall have counsellors who
shall deal with affairs of the office.
Counsellors shall be appointed by the Cabinet from among
persons of special knowledge and experience.
Counsellors shall be accorded the same treatment as offi-
cials of chokunin rank. However, such counsellors as hold
offices in other governmental dopartments shall be ranked ac-
cording to such other offices.
The

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
The term of service of counsellors shall be two years.
However, in special circumstances they may be released from
service at any time even before the expiry of such term.
Article 6. The Kikakucho may set up committees to in-
vestigate matters of special character.
Members of such committees shall be appointod by the
Cabinet from among persons of special knowledge and experience,
by request of the Prime Minister submitted to the Throne.
The committee shall terminate upon the completion of in-
vestigation of such special matters submitted.
Article 7. The president shall direct the affairs of
the Kikakucho, supervise officials of the various soctions,
and exorcise full authority in the appointment and dismissal
of officials of hannin rank.
Artice 8. The vice president shall assist the president
in the direction of the affairs of the Kikakucho.
Article 9. The private secretary shall deal with confi-
dential affairs under the orders of the president.
Article 10. Examiners shall conduct investigations and
draft plans under orders of senior officials.
Article ll. The secretary shall conform with orders of
senior officials.
Article l2. Assistant examiners shall assist examiners
in the conduct of investigations and the drafting of plans
under orders of senior officers.
Article 13. Managors shall conform with orders of senior
officials.
Article l4. Subordinate officials shall be responsible
for

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.97)
-4 -
for miscellaneous duties under orders of senior officials.
Supplementary Rule.
The present ordinance shall be put into force from
the day of promulgation.
The Naikaku Chosa Kyoku (Cabinet Inquiry Bureau) shall
be abolished.

June 29, 1937.
Tokyo's No. 2469 of June 11, 1937,
presents certain considerstions touching
upon the viability of the Konoye Cabinet
installed on June 4, 1937.
It is the view of the Embassy that a
reescnably Tone tenure of office may be
expected by and of the Konoye Cabinet.
Although in the light of the resignation
of the Hayashi Cabinet "The inflence of
the parties, though real, remeins primarily
destructive", and although the joint
Minseito-Seiyuka1 committee for the over-
throw of the Hayashi Cahinet declared at
ite final meeting that the new Cahinet's
stand in regard to the parties was regret-
teble in severel respects, it would appear
that the Konoye Carinet may rely upon the
cooperation of the Minseito and the Seiyukai
parties. One of the several indications
advenced in support of this view is the
fact thst the ahove-mentioned committee
stated at the same final meeting that the
parties should give the Carinet their moral
cooperation.
There would appear likewise to be
grounds for the bellef that the new Cabinet
may also count upon the support of the
military. The Emhasey alludes to the fact
that Prince Konoye, prior to accepting the
task of forming a cabinet, obtained the
consent of the Army and Navy to the
continuance in office of General Sugiyame
and

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.98)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
and Admiral Yonai, this consent constituting
therefore an expression of confidence in
the new Premier on the pert of the army and
Havy rather than an approval of the
individuals appointed as inisters of War
and of Navy.
Adverting to the weaknesses of the
new Cabinet as constituted, the Embassy
alludes to the anpointment of Dr. Baba
es .inister of Home ffeirs. The selection
of Dr. baba has been viewed with apprehen-
sion by business men end financiers. Also,
it is to he remarked that the unsatisfactory
state of health of the present Fremier may
possibly preclude & long tenure of office
on his part.

expressed by several Japenese that Hirota
is being groomed to renlece Honoye should
such contingency arise.

of the House of Peers.
 Minister of Justice: Mr. Suehiko Shiono, continuing in
office.
Immediately following the first meeting of the new Cabinet,
held on the same day, Prince Konoe issued to the press a
statement asking for the cooperation of the nation. Matters
of political policy were not touched upon. The evening of
the same day Prince Konoe addressed the nation by radio, and
his words were notable for their directness and simplicity.
He announced it would be his policy in foreign relations to
seek international justice, and in domestic relations to
seek social justice. It was a wise appeal for the peoples'
confidonce.

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
confidence, in a manner quite unknown to former Premier
Hayashi whose attitude was that explanations to the people
with regard to government policy are not necessary. How-
ever, Prince Konoe's radio address does not constitute a
political platform. He stated, in fact, that he could not
yet announce concrete points of the new Cabinet's platform,
and he left the public with the impression that the policy
of the Cabinet is to meet questions as they arise rather
than to seek to carry out a formulated platform. The radio
address contains no promises, but it was spoken to the
people at large as partners in the interests of government,
and as such its dominant tone was generally appreciated by
the public.
An attitude of acceptance of the new administration
by the Minseito and Seiyukai parties was indicated by an
action taken by them on the day of installation of the Konoe
Cabinet. The joint Minseito-Seiyukai committee for the over-
throw of the Hayashi Cabinet not only disbanded, but in its
final meeting agreed that while the new Cabinet's stand with
regard to the parties is regrettable in several respects,
the parties should give the Cabinet their moral cooperation.
This is one of several indications that the major parties
will not be troublesome in the next session of the newly
elected House of Representatives. The Diet has been ordered
to convene on July 23 and to be in session for fourteen days.
Although the Konoe Cabinet contains but one minister
taken from the Seiyukai and but one minister takon from the
Minseito

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Minseito party, both in loss important ministerships, the
loud clamor of the political parties against former Premier
Hayashi because of his disregard of the results of the
general election appears to have been succossfully dissipated.
The influence of the parties, though real, remains primarily 
destructive.
In his public statement following the installation of
the Cabinet, Premier Konoe did not attach any conditions to
the Minseito and Seiyukai appointments. There had been an
earlier apprehension that representatives of the parties
would be accepted as cabinet ministers only upon the condition
of their not being recognized as representatives of their
parties. Prince Konoe decided against any such requirement,
and the apparent satisfaction of the parties with the new
administration is partly attributable to that fact.
The notable weakness of the Konoe Cabinet is its inclu-
sion of Dr. Baba as Minister of Home Affairs. The military
like Dr. Baba for his proven willingness as Minister of
Finance in the Hirota Cabinet to accede to all their demands.
Prince Konoe successfully resisted pressure for Baba's re-
instatement as Finance Minister, but made the concession of
admitting him to the Cabinet in another office. As Home
Minister Dr. Baba will no doubt have to do with projects
advocated by the military in furtherance of their ideas of
state socialism, and this is no doubt the reason why Japanese
business men and financiers are openly apprehensive about
his appointment. It is feared that Dr. Baba will be an agent
of

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
of the military eloment in promoting plans for expansion
of rigid state control over industry and for exponsive
schemes which will strain the country's credit and re-
sources.
The question of military and naval approval or dis-
approval of the composition of the Cabinet was relegated
to the background by the acceptance, early in Prince Konoe's
negotiations, of posts in the new Cabinet by both the War
Minister and Navy Minister of the Hayashi Cabinet. This
marks a new turn in the procedure of forming cabinets as
compared with the last few years. It is perhaps a signi-
ficant change. Prince Konoe, before accepting the task of
forming a cabinet, first obtained the consent of the Army
and Navy to the continuing in office of General Sugiyama
and Admiral Yonai. This consent thereby became an expression
of confidence in Prince Konoe as Premier rather than an appro-
val of the individuals to fill each post in the cabinet.
That the Army and Navy were willing to give this consent in
advance of determining the personnel of the cabinet is proof
of an important change of outlook toward their part in poli-
tics. It is not likely that, when he obtained the consent
of the Army and Navy, Prince Konoe was either willing or
able to disclose the entire composition of his still unformed
cabinet.
A few individuals in the new cabinet are of special in-
terest to the American Government on account of their part
in foreign relations. The new Foreign Minister, Mr. Koki
Hirota (Embassy's telegram No. 151, June 7, 2 p.m.), stated
when he received me on June 7 that his guiding policy in
relations with the United States will be the ideas ombodied
1n

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6-
in the 1934 exchange of messages with the Secretary of
State (see the Department's release to the press under
date of March 20, 1934). He took occasion also to state
that the three-point policy toward China which he announced
when formerly in office is too abstract for the presont re-
quirements of relations between Japan and China, and that
instead of an approach to those relations from the viewpoint
of that policy he will seek concrete solutions of the out-
standing problems with China. Mr. Hirota apparently recognizes
that broad statements of policy toward China are of doubt-
ful value, and that a solution of such problems one by one is
to be preferred. (Copy of memorandum of my conversation with
Foreign Minister Hirota is enclosed).
The new Minister of Commerce and Industry, Mr. Shunji
Yoshino, (Embassy's telegram No. 149, June 4, 5 p.m.) is a
practical, realistic, and competent business administrator
who understands the needs of and is friendly to foreign com-
mercial interests. When he served previously as Vice Minis-
ter he proved himself sympathetic to the suggestions of foreign
oil interests in the solution of their difficulties.
Although the life of cabinets is dependent upon sudden
changes in the relations between political elements, some
of which do not appear on the political stage, it is perhaps
worth recording that the expectation of a long tenure of office
by the Konoe Cabinet is very general. Several Japanese have
expressed to me privately, however, their fear that Prince
Konoe may be unable because of the unsatisfactory state of
his health to bear the heavy burden of his office. They are
of

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
of the belief that Hirota is being groomed to replace
Konoe should such contingency arise. Prince Konoe brings
to his office the high prestige of rank and conspicuously
successful parliamentary experience. Although not yet
forty-six years old, his name is well known to the public
and he has general popular support. His success in avoiding
enmities during his political career is now bearing fruit,
when the particular requirement of a premier seems to be
to smooth out factional differences.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew
