Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 2469 dated June 11, 1937.
Conversation
June 7, 1937.
The American Ambassador, Mr. Grew,
with the Minister for Foreign Affairs,
Mr. Hirota.
Japanese-American Relations
In my initial talk today with the new Foreign
Minister, Mr. Hirota said that the exchange of good will
messages between Mr. Hull and himself in 1934 would be
his guiding principle in conducting Japanese-American
relations, and he asked me to convey this fact to the
President and to Mr. Hull.
Sino-Japanese Relations
I asked Mr. Hirota whether he visualized any new
departure in conducting Sino-Japanese relations. The
Minister said that conditions had canged since he was
last in office and that his "three points" were too abstract
for present circumstances. He proposed therefore to try
to find concrete solutions of the various issues out-
standing between the two countries.

The Secretary of State,

Sir:
In informal letters dated May 2, 1936, and May 16,
1936, to the Chief of the Division of Far Eastern Affairs,
I made brief mention of the rumored arrest of General Mazaki
ASSITANT SECRETARY
beause of alleged complicity in the incident of February
1936. I now have the honor to state that, according
AUG311937
OF STATE
a reliable informant, General Mazaki was recently sen-
ced by a court martial to six years' imprisonment.
OF STATE
A member of my staff was today told by a former minister
a
the Japanese Foreign Service, who is today the executive
F/G
officer

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
officer of a private association analogous to the Foreign
Policy Association, that the court martial sitting on the
case issued sentence about three weeks ago, but that the
sentence was not to be made publio until after the comple-
tion of the present extraordinary mession of the Diet. In
view of the serious turn which the North China Incidont has
taken, the informant thought it likely that publication of
the sentence would be still further deferred.
It may be recalled that sentence of execution was car-
ried out on fifteen of the seventeen officers who led the
revolt of February 26, 1936. The informant stated that the
two former officers who had not been executed gave conclu-
sive evidence at the court martial that General Mazaki had
previous knowledge of the plans of a revolt, and that he
had, in fact, given the ring-leaders encouragement; that
the prosecuting officer demanded a sentence of ten years'
imprisonment; but that the court, taking into account
General Mazaki's "advanced years and distinguished services,
considered six years' imprisonment adequate punishment.
Assuming that the foregoing information is true, it
is not unlikely that commentators and publicists will,
upon publication of the results of the trial, seek to read
into such results a greater significance than the facts
would warrant. The first and primary effect upon the Japanese
public of the Revolt was to restore confidence in the Army as
an organization completely responsive to the commands of
the Emperor - and, therefore, restore confidence in the Army's
discipline. It is my opinion that the principal significance
of the sentence passed upon General Mazaki is that it manifests
continued

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
continued determination on the part of the high military
command to maintain discipline. By somewhat involved and
tortuous processes of reasoning, it might perhaps also be
interpreted as reflecting intention to keep the Army out
of internal politics; but it would be, in my opinion, quite
illusory to contend that the results indicate the trend in
struggle between the "liberal element" and the "reactionary
element" in the shaping of foreign policy. I have heard it
said that the Emperor, at the instance of Mr. Matsudaira,
0B1
Minister of the·Imperial Household, and of other personal
Hinhi
advisers, gave approval to the actions of the Japanese
Government in North China on condition that effort be made
to avoid aggravation of the situation. If that is true,
there is an element in Japan which is endeavoring to exer-
cise restraint on the rapidity with which the extremists
would wish to have the "Continental Policy" developed, but
there is no evidence that, since the beginning of the North
China Incident, there has been in progress any struggle over
the carrying out of that policy.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatches
1752
1755
No. 2519 of August 3, 1937, and No./2551 of August 18, 1937,
and to previous despatches concerning the trial of General
Mazaki and the participants in the Incident of February
1987
26, 1936.
For the alleged reason that the evidence was considered
C
insufficient for conviction, on September 25, 1937, the
Tokyo Court Martial acquitted General Jinzaburo Mazaki of
the charge of having aided the rebels in the uprising of
1/
February 26, l936. There is encloseda clipping from the
JAPAN ADVERTISER of September 26, 1937, containing the Tokyo
Court Martial's statement with regard to its decision, as
given in translation by Domei.
It

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
68-2
-2-
It would appear from this statement that the Court
Martial was in possession of ample evidence to convict
General Mazaki not only of complicity in the Incident but
also of having lent moral encouragement to the insurgents,
if not of having given them active aid. For example, as
admitted by the Court Martial, General Mazaki had full
knowledge of the "Showa Restoration" movement as far back
as the summer of 1935; he had frequent interviews with the
plotters from December l935 until the outbreak and was well
aware of their aims; and he let it be known to the plotters
that he was bitterly opposed to his dismissal in July 1935
from the post of Inspector General of Military Education and
to the death sentence pronounced against It. Col. Aizawa on
May 7, 1936, for the murder of Major General Nagata on
August 12, 1935, thus contributing to the unrest of the
insurgents. Furthermore, he was infcrmed by one of the rebels
at about four-thirty o'clock on the morning of February 26,
1936, that the Premier, the Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal,
and others were to be attacked, and he subsequently interceded
with the higher authorities of the War Office in favor of the
insurgents, stating that he "understood very well the spirit
in which the rebels had acted."
It is probable that despite the apparently conclusive
evidence against General Mazaki the Court Martial found it
advisable to acquit him in view of his distinguished career
and of the unfavorable repercussion that might have resulted
from convicting him at this time of national emergency.
The NICHI NICHI of September 25 quoted a War Office
spokesman as having said that the disposal of General Mazaki's
case had ended the consideration by the Tokyo Court Martial
of the February 26 Incident. In this connection, it should
be stated that Zei Nishida and Kazuteru Kita, who were sen-
tenced to death on August 14, 1937 (Embassy's despatch No.
2551

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.96)
68-3
-3-
2551 of August 18, 1937), as ringleaders in the insurrection,
were executed on August 19, as announced by the Ministry of
War.
Respectfully yours,
Nose h 6. f
Joseph C. Grew.

politics. Lir. Tannsaare has prepared to date in

(1) Japanese industries, with particular reference
to the cheap comnodities produced in Japan; (2)
Low standard of living in Japan, supplemented by
statistical material; (3) Introduction of Western
Luropean methods into Japan, which have succeeded
industrially, but not socially; and (4) Description
of Japanese character, social life and the position
of wonen in Jajan.
A. H. Ta umsaare is the pen name of Mr. Anton
Hangen, concoming whom a confidential biographic
sketch is being prepared at this office and will
shortly be furmished the Department. It appears
that Mr. Hansen is a voluainous writer, with no
particular political leaning.

Office, I have been inrormed that the article, of
which a resumé is enclosed, has in nowise been in-
spired officially nor has it any official endorsement
on the part of the Estonian Government. In fact,

ns of possible interest to the Pmbassy I  ave the

or the Foreign Office.
Yesterday I attended a luncheon given by the
Brazilian Consul General in Kobe. Among the guests was
Mr. Miura, a olose personal friend of the Coneul General.
Over the coffee oups the convsrsation drifted to the war
in China. There were no British present. In a running
conversation Mr.  iura made remsrks whieh in substanee
were as follows.
Referring to the recent demonstration of three
hundred students before the British Embassy in Tokyo, he
stated that foreigners could not realize the growth of
anti-British feeling in Japan; this he said was unfortunate
but entirely natural as Japan's diffieulties in China were
greatly augmented by England's attitude, England, he said,
is now playing her old game of matohing one country agminst
another for her own advantage. She feels that her vast
Asiatio interests are reasonably safe as long as she can
keep Japan and China fighting: in every way she is
"ogging" Ohina on, supplying money to Nanking, and other
support of both a moral and physioal kind; the Foreign
ofrie

and alive to the danger in the situation. England's
game in Asia, however, is a dangerous one for her and
Japen is not being fooled not even the common people.

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 9, 1938.
JWB
Tokyo's No. 2722, January 6, 1938, (by
Mr. Dooman) discussing the decline of party
politics in Japan, is instructive and may
well be read in its entirety.
It is pointed out that Japan is now on
a war footing and that the consciousness of
the consequences of failure at this time,
coupled wiiù the moral opposition to Japan
of the greater part of the world, has
developed a sense of national unity not
conducive to political factionalism.
The evolution and decline of Japanese
political parties is reviewed and the situa-
tion until recently discussed. Then follows
a discussion of the present situation, in
which it seems that parties are crystallizing
along new lines, the Social Mass Party on
the left and a Nationalist Party on the right.
However, as a result of an extensive roundup
of Communists at the end of 1937, in the
course of which leading spirits of the
Social Mass Party were arrested, the develop-
ment of this party has been impeded.
The real bases for parties exist in the
conflict between medievalism and modern
thought. It has been possible up to now to
preserve medieval thought among factory
laborers.

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
laborers. However, out of the war is grow-
ing a heavy metals industry, the artisans
of which are men prone to think along modern
lines. The writer believes that when there
comes into existence a substantial number
of such modern thinking workmen, there will
be solid ground for a real two-party system
in Japan.
FE: Langdon: SMJ

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.94)
69-1
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
AMERICAN EMBASSY
No. 2722.
Tokyo, January 6, 1938.
SUBJECT:
TRENDS IN JAPANESE
PARTY POLITICS.
Mh. HO MB:CK
EB 101939
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
.1
31
SGVCOONY
lui
19938 AN PM
894.00/770
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
HAN21938
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
Whatever may be the label put on the conflict with
China, it has led to the placing of Japan on a war basis.
85198
About one million men are estimated to be with the colors,
and every form of national effort is being subordinated to
the attainment in as short a time as possible of military
and political objectives in China.
There is taking place,
as a normal consequence of the mustering of Japan's mater-
ial and spiritual resources, a change in virtually every
F/FG
aspect of Japanese life.
There is a pervading conscious-
ness

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
69-2
-2-
ness of the consequence of failure in the present effort,
and that consciousness, stimulated by the disapprobation
and moral opposition of the greater part of the world, has
developed a sense of national unity which does not nourish
political factionalism. It is not being suggested that
the decline of political parties in this country can be at-
tributed entirely to the conflict with China, which in the
larger sense began, not in July 1937, but on September 18,
193l; that trend really falls within a trend with a wave-
length of far greater amplitude.
Japan as a world power is still in a transitional
stage.
It renounced me ievalism eighty years ago, and al-
though amazing progress has been made in "things that are
of the earth earthy", and although there have been advances
in certain social problems, notably in the position of the
womanhood of the country, there has not been sufficient time
for dilution by purely natural processes of archaic spirit-
ual and moral ideas.
There are J:panese still living who
wore armor and fought with bow and arrow.
The "revolt of
youth" after the World War was considered to be something
of a phenomenon in America and Europe, but, ever since the
middle of the Nineteenth Century there has been in Japan a
chronic revolt of each new generation against its predecessor.
The impact of events and new ideas varies with the individual
and with the measure of medievalism he has inherited and as-
similated.
The political struggle in Japan, therefore, is
primarily a struggle between generations, and not between
classes.
During a period of forty years after the establishment
of

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.99)
69-3
-3-
of the Diet in 1890, there appeared to be a substantial,
if slow, progress out of military oligarchy, through bu-
reaucracy, and toward constitutional democratic government.
The first twenty years were the concluding decades of the
Meiji Era, one of the most brilliant epochs in Japan's his-
tory, and from the point of view of progress they were the
most important decades. Behind a "modern" political facade,
an extra-constitutional group of powerful personages -- the
so-called Elder Statesmen -- r led the country, but as time
passed and these personages grew older or died, men came who
performed in plain view on the political stage. These flour-
ished and gained inf uence, and around them collected the
minor political fry.
It was from such agglomerations that
the "old line" parties developed.
They were not, however,
associations of persons animated by a comon political objec-
tive, but rather they were loosely formed groups of which
each member was bound to its leader under a tacit agreement
by which the leader gave patronage and in return he was giv-
en support.
In l913 there occurred the first change of
government arising out of defeat in the Lower House on a
vote on a Government measure, and by force of repetition
there evolved the beginnings of a tradition -- for the Con-
stitution is silenton this point -- of a parliamentary sys-
ten.
A further impulse in this direction was given by a
tendency which became marked during the second decade of
this century for the parties to divide along the lines of
conflict of interest between the industrial and agrarian
Owing to circumstances which
elements of the population.
need

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.99)
69-4
-4-
need not be gone into in this despatch, the land-owning
classes tended to support the Seiyukai, and the perversity
of politics made the Minseito the instrument of their op-
ponents.
The two parties thrived so long as men with capacity
for leadership, such as Count Kato and Mr. Hara, could be
found to fill the shoes of such political geniuses as
Count Okuma and Prince Ito; but when they died their places
were taken by party wheel-horses with nothing to raise them
above the common ruck.
Personal leadership, the element
which created p rties and gave them cohesion, was lost, and
they have since been beset with factional jealousies and
strife.
To add to their troubles the venality and corrup-
tion which existed on a wide scale in politics were brought
to light by a series of prosecutions conducted with cour-
age by the law-enforcing authorities against a number of
politicians of no small importance, including former Cab-
inet officers. The disclosures of the immorality of the
politicians, in contrast with the standards enforced among
public servants, especially Army and Navy officers, have
created such mistrust and suspicion of party politicians
that no competent observer today seriously believes that
these parties can be rehabilitated without reforms so
drastic as to change them out of all recognition.
There appeared in the meantime two new elements in
the political situation. The adoption in 1924 of
universal manhood suffrage made possible some concerted
expression

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.99)
69-5
-5-
expression for liberal thought. There was at first a
general movement of the newly enfranohised toward the parties
then existing, but these were firmly controlled by conservatives.
In their disillusionment, they found much that was attractive
in advanced Marxian thought, or they turned to the Second
International, or again to the ideas of the British Labor Party.
Each of these various groups produced a party, and from the
three there finally appeared the Social Mass Party. At the
opposite end of the political scale an effort was being made
to bring together a large number of reactionary and nationalistic
groups. These are the lineal descendants of the faction in
the Shogunete who agitated, under the Prince of Mito, to
"Drive out the Barbarians." Some of these groups are completely
disreputable and, under the guise of patriotism, exist only
for blackmail and bullyragging. The most important is the
notorious Amur (more commonly translated as the Black Dragon)
Society, the head of which is the equally notorious Mitsuru
Toyama, a colorful and venerable political adventurer.
Until quite recently the indications were strong that a
basis was being formed for the revival of government by
political parties -- not by the old-line parties, which are
not divided by any difference of political doctrine worth
mentioning, but by the Social Miass Party on the left and by
a Nationalist Party in process of creation on the right.
The Sôcial Mass Party gained considerably in respectability
when, some weeks ago, it purged itself of the Moscow element
and eliminated from its platform certain planks which the
Moscow element insisted upon when the party was formed.
on
the other hand, Certain progress was apparently made toward
unification

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.98)
6 9-6
-6-
unification of the nationalistic groups by the promoters
1oshi
of this idea, the most indefatigable of whom is Mr. Shiratori
(who obtained considerable publicity during the Manchuria
Incident as the "Foreign Office Spokesman"), as indicated
by the fact that Toyama, Prince Sanjo, a kugé, or one
of the ancient nobility, and Admiral Yamamoto, a retired naval
officer, issued jointly a manifesto advocating the formation
of a Nationalist Party. The stage seemed set for interesting
developments, but during the closing days of 1937 the police
undertook an extensive round-up of "Communists" and among
those gethered in was Mr. Kaju Kato, a member of Parliament
and one of the leading spirits of the Social Mass Party, who
was arrested as he returned from making a "comfort visit" to
the soldiers in China! This has led to further upheavals in
the Party, and it is now difficult to say what the future will
bring.
The collapse of party politics after almost fifty years'
effort can be attributed in the finel analysis to ignoring the
real bases for political divergences. These bases exist in the
conflict between medievalism and modern thought. The mechaniza-
tion of industry is affecting every aspect of Japanese life and
is bringing in its train a horde of new problems which in
advanced countries would be primary political issues. Instead,
they heve in effect been banned from the political arena, and
effort is being made witn the cooperation of the politicians to
solve them within the fremework of what the press is pleased
to call "Jananese tradition and polity. An example from the
particular will clarify this generality: The cotton spining
and weaving industry is the most important industry in the
country

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.99)
69-7
-7-
country. The fact that the operatives are largely young
girls recruited from the farms, that they are indentured
for a period of years, and that they are housed in dormitories
and paid a low wage is well known. This arrangement works
admirably in the interests of both the mill operators, who
are supplied with cheap labor, and the landowning classes,
whose tenants obtain added income, but at the same time it is
admirably calculated to preserve medieval concepts and to
prevent the inevitable -- a desire by labor for a greater
share in the profits of industry.
These conditions lead to the observation that it is
unlikely that a two-party system can rest on solid ground
in Japan until there comes into existence a substantial
number of trained mechanics and artisans employed in
mechanized factories. Unlike the native artisans, working along
manual lines, they divest themselves readily of feudal concepts
of relations between employer and employee, and what is also
to the point, they are apt to have more schooling than the
average laborer. One of the results of the war with China is
a growing heavy metals industry, and as aftermath of the war
there might well be introduced into Japanese politics a new
honesty and vigor.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

" Embassy, Peiping.

Sir:
Enclosed with this despatch are copies of trans-
lations as published in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of the public
messages released on January 1 by Japanese officials, in
accordance with the prevailing custom of new year pronounce-
ments.
All the messages maintain a tone of optimism. Most
put in a word for renewed exertions by the nation, and some
forecast

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.98)
70-2
-2-
forecast 1938 as a year of greet importance in the destinies
of Japan.
1/
Premier Konoe speaks with a sense of impending renaissance,
changes in 1938 more significant than the Meiji restoration
period or the Russo-Japanese war. He says that Japan's effort
1
is to help China recover from degeneration, and that nothing
short of such recovery is vital to Japan's own security. There
is probably a warning of further government control when the
Premier states that Japan must more fundamentally mobilize
the whole strength of the whole nation for the objectives of
the state. In discussing China he holds out an invitation
to a new régime. He asserts that responsibility is keenly
felt for mishaps to British and American vessels on the
occasion of the fall of Nanking.
The Foreign Minister calls attention to the Sino-Soviet
non-aggression pact as a political guarantee of continued
supplies to China o1 Soviet munitions. He expresses pleasure
over the amicable settlement of the PANAY incident, and an-
V
ticipates continued friendship with the United States as a
good neighbor. Mr. Hirota expresses gratitude for the friend-
ly attitude of Germany and Italy, and soft-pedals the anti-
comintern accord by ascribing its significance to cultural
relations. He speaks with favor of Italian recognition of
"Manchukuo", and of exchange of notes of recognition between
"Manchukuo" and the F ranco régime in Spain.
2/
The Home Minister, Admiral Suetsugu, glorifies the vic-
tories in China and admonishes preparation for protracted war,
asserting that the international situation confronts Japan
with

=== Page 433 === (confidence 0.98)
70-3
-3-
with difficulties in the years ahead.
Finance Minister Kaya speaks as if pleased with the
results of emergency financial measures thus far and pleads
for diminution in populer consumption.
4/
The message of Mr. Yuki, the governor of. the Bank of
Japan, has ore to say than Mr. Kaya's. Although finding
the operation of emergency measures satisf ctory he points
out that more money was put in circulation by the govern-
ment in 1937 than in 1936, and that in 1938 the sum must be
larger still. He admits that absorption of government bonds
has not been fully satisfactory, he favors maintenance of
the foreign exchange rate, and he anticipates that the coming
year, though not one of panic, will be one of recession.
Mr. Yoshino, Minister of Comnerce and Industry, states
()
that Japanese imports for eleven months through November
5
exceeded exports by ¥ 650,000,000 and that the adverse balance
in 1938 will be worse. He urges economy in consumption while
keeping open the im ortation of essentials, and in general
betrays no reat concern for the present condition of the
country.
6/
The Minister of Agriculture and Forestry gives himself
particularly to exhorting the rural population to continued
sacrifices. He is however frank enough to indicate that in
rural districts the people are beginning to feel shortages in
labor, agricultural machinery, fertilizers, fodder, and oil.
New year messages would ordinarily be optimistic rather
than otherwise, but even making full allowance for this na-
tural bent it still remains unlikely that the dominant views
of the officials of the government are other than those ex-
pressed.
The nation is confident. Hostilities in China have
progressed

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
70-4
-4-
progressed rapidly and successfully, and the serious economic
load necessary therefor has been borne with very little domestio
hardship. It is in the country districts that the burden will
in all probability fall with the greatest impact, but that ef-
fect can be deferred for some time, and has not yet become a
pressing problem. Two convictions appear to prevail among the
people and among the men in office: first, that Japan is at
grips with problems of prime importance in the country's history;
and second, that the nation is equal to the task.
Respectfully yours,
Somnfth . om
Joseph C. Grew.

REGARDING: Visit of Ambassador Bullitt to War saw. Memorandum of
conversetion between the Ambassador and the Japanese
Ambassador to Poland. Ambassador Sakoh stated that no
one in the Jepenese Government today knew the status of
Japan's policy with regard to China. He expressed the hope

mili tary men would get out of Central and Southern China

five Northern Provinces.
Memorandum of conversation between Ambassador Bullitt and
General Geering in Berlin. Goering expressed the belief that
the Japanese aim in China was to organize the Northern
Provinces as a base from which to attack the Soviet Union, since
it was obvious that this attack was inevitable due to the
geographical position of Vladivostok.

SATURDAY,
FEBRUARY
Japanese Empire
Day is Enhanced
By Mass Amnesty
Citizenship Rights Restored
to 300,000 People
LEADERS OF TOKYO ARMY
REVOLT INCLUDED
Tokyo, Feb. 11.
Three hundred thousand Japanese,
including 150,000 political offenders,
have been reinstated to full citizenship
by an Imperial Amnesty issued to-day
on the occasion Empire Day which
marks the 2,598th anniversary of the
Accession of Emperor Jimmu, Japan's
first Ruler, and the 50th anniversary
of the promulgation of the Constitu-
tion.
Death sentences have been com-
muted to life sentences, while life
sertences have been commuted to im-
prisonment or penal servitude for
twenty years. Those already serving
time have had their terms commuted
to half the original sentences.
The former Capt. Ittaro Yamaguchi
and six other leaders of the revolt in
Tokyo on February 26, 1936, have had
their life sentences commuted to
twenty years' imprisonment, while the
former Naval Sub-Lieutenant Kiyoshi
Koga and three other naval officers,
who were involved in the assassina-
tion of the then Premier, Baron
Inukai, and other crimes in the ter-
rorist demonstration on May 15, 1932,
have had the remainder of their
sentences commuted by one quarter.
An Army orde announces the
cancellation, from o-day, of "an
Army order of punisament issuèd
in connection with olfences com-
mitted previous to ebruary 11

The bill was drafted by Government agencies (the Planning
Board and the Eureau of Legislation) and is offered to the
Diet as a Government measure.
工.
The bill is of the nature of enabling legislation.
Its aim is to ive the Governrent the autlority to control
the people and the procuction of the country in such a way
as best to serve war cbjectives. The provisions of the bill
cenerally are limited by the ualification of (a) necessity
to ceneral mobijizaticn and (b) time of war or incident similar
to war. Within the limite of thece twe ualifications the
powers which the enacted bill would grant to the Government
are sweepin. such powere extend to the drafting of Japan-
ese pubject: rer eneral mobilization enter rises; to control
of Japanese juricical persons; to determination of wages
end labor concitichs; to the settlement of lebor disputes;
to full pomer cver im orts, exporte, and customs tariff; to
the exproprietion of coode, plants, land, houses, mining
pro erties, water ri. itu; te fixiny of transportation charges;
to limitaticn or what can be published; to a vocational census;
to fixing storing requirements; to giving compensation; to
eranti subsicies; to the control of any business or enter-
prise necessary to mobilizatior; and to the control of any
goods or services necessary to mobilization. Carrying out
of the provisions would be by ordinance. A mobilization
compensation commission is provided for. Punishments for
violations are stipulated.
工I.

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
工I.
In studying this bill three articles of the Japanese
constitution snould be recalled. Article 5 states that the
Emperor exercises the legislative power with the consent of
the Diet. It is in pursuance of this article that the mobili-
zation bill is being presented to the Diet for enactment like
any ordinary law. Two other articles, not invoked in the
present instance, nave heretofore been generally thought of
as the basis for emergency action: articles Sl and 70.
Article 3l states thet the constitution's guarantees of
certain civil rights to subjects shall not, in time of war
or national emereency, limit exercise of powers appertaining
to the ereror. Article 7o statos that when the Diet can not
e convoked, and in event of urgent need for the meintenance
of public safety, the lovernment may teke all necessary
firancial measures by means of crdinance; but the matter shall
be submitted to the Diet at its next session. Should the
Diet at such subsequent session refuse approval, those
financial measures would, from the date of such refusal,
cease tc be effective.
工II.
The mobilization bill in its generaj form is said to
date fron some ten years eco when Jepan, like other countries
which desired to profit by the lessons of the war of 1914-1918,
studied plans for wartime economic mobilination to keep pace
with military requirenents. Such lans as were drafted ten
yeers

-4-
years ago were not made public, but in February 1938
the Government allowed to be printed in the newspapers
a draft of a mobilization law, with the announcement
that the currert session of the Diet would be asked to
enact it. This trial balloon provoked vigorous protest
to certain featur s, and several alteraticns were made
before the Cabinet passed on the craft described at the
beginning of this despatch and in enclosure 1. The bill
submitted to the Liet ciffers fron the eerlier newspaper
draft in several poirt. Four erticles have been deleted
vhich in the newspa er dr ft provided for (a) restriction
or prohibition f meetings er organizing, and for (b)
suppression of offendin newsnarers.
 further concession
in the bill as com ered with varlier draft is that there
is rovision for a mobilization commission to advise on
measures to be taken under authority of the act; and state-
ments have been made in the Diet by the Government promising
that Liet members woulc be well represented in this commission.
IV.
The Liet's reception cf the bill has been marked by
lengthy interpellation end by discussion which, in the
anemic political traditions of recent years in this country,
can be classed as vigorous. Fron the com.encement a number
of Diet members were suspicious of the bill, and this attitude
was not dispelled on finding the Governnent spokesmen poorly

at first, avowing a colf, and the discussion wandered ir-
regularly

be foreseen, and to keep the emergency prerogative of

V.
Successfully disarming as was Premier Konoe's eir of
frankness before the com:ittee, it can haraly be said that
he replied in complete candor. If Japan has ahead a long
and difficult task in carrying to completion the policy upon
vhich the country has launced on the continent, some political
discontent on the part of the classes on whom the burden will

the coverning of Janan will be a less difficult task if there
is no necessity of subnitting to subsequert Diet sessions

tive. Crdinances issued under the enabling legislation of
a mobilization law would not come un for subsequent approval:
the Dict would have hac its only say in enactine the sweeping
provisicns of the bill. It is not difficult to imagine many

VI.

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
VI.
The mobilization bill's prospects for enactment can
only be surmised from the known attributes of Japanese
politics and from the opinion of observers and a few high
Japanese. At best the Diet does not have great independence,
and in times like tiese there is added pressure of public
opinion for full support of measures for the prosecution of
the hostilities. Prince Konoe is a popular premier, and the
presentation of the bill is well timed. The Government still
can call on the method of conmromise to cut off a few un-
essentials of the bill as a olite concession if required. It
would be difficult at the present juncture to find basis for
believing that the bill will be defeated.
Althou h the Diet has shown a disposition during the
present session to contest legislation presented by the Gov-
ernment which would, ii eracted, circumscribe personal rights
and private initiative, any assumption that such opposition
reflects a rowtn of discontent with the Government's conduct
of foreien reletions or of its foreign policies takes on the
color more of speculation than of deduction fron facts.
Respectfully yours,
800
Joseph C. Grew.

March 24, 1938.
Despatch no. 2798 from Tokyo regarding a
Janan Advertiser interview with Ceneral Araki is
of Interest. Amons other ideas the General em-
phasized the self-sufriciency of Japanese thought
and rlitical n ilosonhy, and in reference to the
anti-Communist pact assertec that Japan's partici-
nation therein was based on the usefulness of the
pact and not on a spiritual background shared with
the Fascist states of Turone. The Advertiser
headlined the interview "Janan's Link to Fascism
ixnedieney, Says Araki". The following day the
journsl puhlished a letter from Araki in which
w :le acmitting that the interpreter may have used
ie word exped iency, Arali said he had not meant
to inly any sac ifice cf moral princinles to
facil tate scme enis. Later the interviewer,
Mr.  lend bussell, ard vis interpreter, were sum-
mone tc Ara i's residence, where they were con-
fronted hy tle :ererel and two men obviously
nolitical ruffians. Those two latter took charge
of the interuiew and after some minatory prelimin-

The original interview was first granted by General
Araki to Mr. Frank Hedges, an American newspaper correspondent
long resident in Tokyo. Mr. Oland D. Russell, at present
a member of the staff of the JAPAN ADVERTISER, asked Mr.
Hedges if he could accompany him. This was arranged, and
both Mr. Hedges and Mr. Russell attended. Mr. Hedges has
continually uphela Mr. Russell's report of what was said, and
there has in fact been no direct questioning of its accuracy.
Mr. Russell is an experienced newspaperman who was news
editor of the JAPAN ADVERTISER a number of years ago, went
to New York where he served until recently as cable editor
of the NEW YORK WORLD TELEGRAM, and is now in Tokyo on leave
of absence for e few months vith & commission from Little,
Brovm and Company to write a book about the Mitsui family.
While temporarily in Tokyo his services are being availed
of by the JAPA! ADVEnTISER in a part-time capacity.
On the part of General Araki the interview itself was
arranged largely by a certain Kumasaki, who acted also as
the General's interpreter. Mr. Kumasaki is an experienced
linguist, brother of a former officer of the Japanese foreign
service, and is often used by government departments at
Tokyo in foreign contact work.
Among other ideas General Araki put considerable emphasis
on the self-sufficiency of Japanese national thought and
political philosophy, and in reference to the Japan-Germany-
Italy pact against communism made a good deal of the assertion
that Japan's participation is motivated by the anti-communist
usefulness of the pact, not by a spiritual background shared
with the fascist states of Europe. The ADVERTISER raised this to
headlines

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
5226
-3-
headlines in the words "Japan's Link to Fascism Expediency,
Says Araki."
The paper immediately received a letter in General
Araki's name admitting that the General's interpreter
might have used the word expediency in the interview, and
stating that he in fact had no intention of conveying the
meaning of a sacrifice of moral principles to facilitate
2/
some end. The following morning, February 5, this letter
was printed on the front page, as the interview had been.
Later in the day (February 5) the ADVERTISER office
received a telephone message, purportedly from General
Araki's household, stating thet the General woula like to
see the editor of the paper, Mr. Wilfred Fleisher. It
was explained thet it would be difficult for the editor
to come at that time but that Mr. Russell would be glad
to call upon the General. In discussing the time when a
conversation could be arranged ir. Russell suggested a
meeting a few days hence, but was told in peremptory manner
that it would be to the interest of the paper to comply
immediately. A meeting that evening at the Tokyo Club
was agreed to. When ir. Russell appeared with his inter-
preter at the Tokyo Club at the agreed hour he found a
message there directing him to proceed to the General's
house. Arriving at the residence Mr. Russell and his
interpreter overheard a heated discussion going on in an
adjacent room, at the conclusion of which the General,
flanked by two men obviously ruffians, came out to the
reception room where Ir. Russell and his interpreter were
waiting. The General appeared plainly embarrassed and began
in

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
5227
-4-
in an apologetic manner, whereupon his escorts took
matters in their own hands and coming up to Mr. Russell
said that things would soon be hard for Mr. Fleisher's
Jew paper and that it might just as well close down now.
These remarks were accompanied by some pushing and obvious
efforts to give the impression of threatening physical
violence. Mr. Russell asked what objections to the
paper's policy he could report to Mr. Fleisher, and the
conference got down to its immediate purpose. The General's
two attendants referred to the interview with General Araki
as reported in the ADVERTISER of February 4. After hearing
their suggestions Mr. Russell drew up a statement (later
3/
published on the morning of February 7), and this was
finally agreed to after a discussion between the General's
men, in which the General was practically overlooked, according
to Mr. Russell's account.
Thereupon Mr. Russell mentioned General Araki's statement,
in his letter of February 4 commenting upon the interview
as published, that the General was impressed with the accuracy
with which the interview was rendered into English. When
this comment by Mr. Russell was translated into Japanese the
General's attendants were infuriated. It was obviously their
first information of the letter and they immediately demanded
explanations of General Araki. The subsecuent discussion
seemed to disclose that the letter was the work of Mr. Kumasaki.
After some time it was agreed that in spite of the letter the
ADVERTISER should nevertheless print the statement just
drafted by Mr. Russell.
Mr. Russell next drew attention to the fact that he had
cabled his interview to the United States and that it had
already

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.98)
228
-5-
already been published. This aspect, to Mr. Russell's
surprise, caused no interest. The men stated that the
reaction abroad is a matter of no importance, and pointed
to the reaction within Japan as their only interest. There-
upon the conversation ended and the ADVERTISER printed the
statement agreed to.
Mr. Russell's interpretation of the incident is that it
suggests that there are groups in Japan aiming at keeping
General Araki's record clear in order that he may be used
as the head of some political movement, probably reactionary,
aiming at the displacement of the present political parties
by a single organization. The evidence of the Japanese in-
terpreter who accompanied Mr. Russell is that the General's
two attendants were unmistakably of the organized ruffian
class which is by all odds the most dangerous to deal with
in Japanese life. Even when General Araki was Minister of
War he was largely a figurehead for other leaders, and there
is plausibility in the estimate that he is still thought of
for a similar use again by certain groups having extremely
reactionary plans for the solution of Japan's present difficul-
ties. Such groups would wish him to keep clear of any public
statements which might tend to alienate fascist sympathies.

Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2, to despatch
No.8798, dated March 2,1938.
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
The Japan Advertiser.
Tokyo, Saturday, February 5, 1938.
READERS IN COUNCIL
"Expediency" Explained
by Araki
To the Editor,
Reading the English version of my
interview published in today's edition
of your valuable paper, I have been
impressed by the accuracy with which
it has been rendered into your langu-
age. There is ce point. however.
which I would like you to further
clarify a little.
It is the part bearing on the Italo-
German-Japanese Pact against the
menace of Communism. You have us-
ed the word "expediency." which my
interpreter might in fact have used or
my remarks. if boiled down, might
have amounted to that. I fear how-
ever this word is liable to be inter-
preted in the light of a "sacrifice of
mora! principles for the sake of facili-
tating some end."
I do not mean that, or to disparage
the value or character of the Anti-
Comintern Pact. O:ly I meant to lay
emphasis on the fact that the conclu-
sion of this pact was not a gesture of
defiance against the political idcology
of the democratic countries. but that
its sole inducement was the common
fear of the danger of the eneroachment
of Cemmunism. cnd that, therefore, if

might rot have been coneluded. Is
this what you understand by "expedi-
eney?"
Wishing you would clarify this point
a little further.
SADAO ARAKI

You may wish to glance
through this interesting des-
patch on Wartime Thought Control
Exhibit in Tokyo.

=== Page 487 === (confidence 0.99)
230
-2-
The exhibit is one illustration of the many means
being currently employed in Janan to sustain support in
public opinion for prosecution of the Government's for-
eign policy. Its principal theme is that opinion abroad
is manufactured, and that where such foreign opinion is
anti-Japanese it has been made so by organized effort.
There is of course no sugeesticn that opinion in Japan
is amenable to like iniluences.
It is noteworthy that from Germany Japan has now
taken over as new motif the campaign against the Jews.
In the exhibit the Jews are identified with the Communist
International, are described as having effective world-
wide orcanization, and are represented as having sinister
power in the control of the news. That this propaganda-
stimulus can be thou ht potentially valuable by Japan,
which has no Jewish cuestion, is a :rovoking reflection
on the susceptibilities of tie human emotions.
The py mania was deliberately fed by the exhibit.
Increasing sus icion to ard forei ners and annoyance by
would-be patriots is probably to be expected as the war-
time psycholcy grows herearter.
The moet disturbing symptom to be observed in this
exhibit, as also in the present trend of the press and
thought in Japan, is the un ualified acceptance of a world
alignment of countries in hostile systems. Japan no longer
sees herself as a country workirg out a solution of prob-
lems confined to one part of the earth:
more and more
the thought is being built up that Japan is party to a
world-wide crusade against communism.
The pact between
Germany,

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.98)
5231
-3-
Germany, Japan, and Italy is bearing fruit in the field
of thought. In 1931 and 1932 the public mind was guided
to think of Manchuria as the life-line of Japan; later it
was pressed to see that a Chine not friendly to Japan could
not be tolerated. Now the propaganda stage is further ex-
panding.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

The mobilization bill which was the subject of
the Embassy's despatch 2800, March 3, 1938 was passed by
the Japanese House of Representatives on March 16 with-
APR8-1938
FILED
out amendment. The bill is now under consideration in
the House of Peers,where it is expected to meet little
real opposition.
Opposition in the House of Representatives was at
first loud, but the politicians had to give way in spite
of their blustering. The concessions were small and
left the bill unchanged. The Government in the course
F/M
of

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.98)
今
- 2 -
of debate accepted an undertaking that the mobilization
commission authorized under article 5o to advise on
measures to be taken in applying the act will be com-
posed in majority of Diet members. This commitment
need hardly embarrass the Government, inasmuch as the
commission is advisory only, and the entire Diet, in-
cluding the Peers, gives a wide choice of appointment.
By way of face-saving for the politicians the Lower
House concurrently adopted a resolution urging the
Government (1) to avoid resort to ordinances for establish-
ing regul tions capable of embodiment in laws for Diet
consideration, (2) to reform the civil service, and (3)
to renovate diplomacy.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew

country.
